The mod- p p representation theory of the metaplectic cover of GL 2 ⁡ ( 𝐐 p ) \operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
Robin Witthaus

Abstract
. Half-integral weight modular forms are naturally viewed as automorphic forms on the so-called metaplectic covering of GL 2 ⁡ ( 𝐀 𝐐 ) \operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Q}}) – a central extension by the roots of unity μ 2 \mu_{2} in 𝐐 \mathbf{Q} . For an odd prime number p p , we give a complete classification of the smooth irreducible genuine mod- p p representations of the corresponding covering of GL 2 ⁡ ( 𝐐 p ) \operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p}) by showing that the functor of taking pro- p p -Iwahori-invariants and its left adjoint define a bijection onto the set of simple right modules of the pro- p p Iwahori Hecke algebra. As an application of our investigation of the irreducible subquotients of the universal module over the spherical Hecke algebra depending on some weight, we prove that being of finite length is equivalent to being finitely generated and admissible. Finally, we explain a relation to locally algebraic irreducible unramified genuine principal series representations.



1.
Introduction

The Shimura correspondence
[
28
]
is a map
\operatorname{Sh}
from the space of half-integral weight cusp forms to the space of even-integral weight modular forms, satisfying
\operatorname{Sh}\circ T_{p^{2}}=T_{p}\circ\operatorname{Sh}
for each prime
p
, where
T_{p^{2}}
is the half-integral weight Hecke operator defined by Shimura and
T_{p}
is the usual integral weight Hecke operator, see also
[
25
]
. This correspondence has been reformulated in terms of automorphic representations by Gelbart and Piatetski-Shapiro
[
12
]
as well as, independently and more generally, by Flicker
[
11
]
: letting
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Q}}
denote the ring of rational adèles, they consider the
metaplectic cover

1\to\mu_{2}\to\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Q}})\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Q}})\to 1,

which is a central topological extension by the roots of unity
\mu_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
in
\mathbf{Q}
. It is constructed from corresponding local metaplectic covers

1\to\mu_{2}\to\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})\to 1

as
p
ranges over all places, see
[
13
]
. These local extensions are defined using the degree-two Hilbert symbol, and they split over the maximal compact open subgroup if
2<p<\infty
. The quadratic reciprocity law implies that the global covering splits over
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q})
, and Shimura’s correspondence is then incarnated as a map

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Genuine complex}\\
\text{automorphic representations}\\
\text{of $\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Q}})$}\end{array}\right\}\to\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Complex}\\
\text{automorphic representations}\\
\text{of $\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Q}})$}\end{array}\right\},

where “genuine” means that the subgroup
\mu_{2}
acts non-trivially. This map is defined by means of local Shimura correspondences relating those smooth irreducible admissible complex representations of
\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
which are genuine to those of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
(here we restrict to finite places
p
).

Given a prime
\ell
, it is natural to ask whether there exists an
\ell
-adic analogue of the local Shimura correspondence for the group
\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
, attaching an irreducible admissible unitary
\ell
-adic Banach space representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
to a genuine one of the covering group. If
\ell\neq p
, then passing to smooth vectors still remembers a large part of the representation (the smooth vectors will be dense). In fact, Vignéras
[
29
]
proved that taking smooth vectors and remembering the commensurability class of the smooth part of an invariant unit ball sets up an equivalence onto a suitable category. Since smooth representations do not see the topology of the coefficient field, one may then try to use the existing complex local correspondence to define an
\ell
-adic one. The case
\ell=p
is more subtle (and therefore more exciting to study) as there are usually no smooth vectors at all and it is not clear how to possibly formulate a
p
-adic local Shimura correspondence. By passing to the mod-
p
reduction of an invariant unit ball, any such correspondence should give rise to a semi-simple mod-
p
local Shimura correspondence (for
p>2
), which seems easier to attack. Hence, instead of directly looking for a
p
-adic correspondence, it is reasonable to first study the mod-
p
situation. In order to do this, it will be necessary—or at least very useful—to have a good understanding of the smooth irreducible admissible mod-
p
representations of
\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
. On such an object, the central subgroup
\mu_{2}
either acts trivially, i.e. the representation descends to
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
, or it acts via the non-trivial character in which case the representation is called genuine.

The smooth irreducible
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
-linear representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
(admitting a central character) are well-understood by the work of Barthel–Livné
[
1
]
and Breuil
[
4
]
: Letting
Z\cong\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
denote the center of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
, their approach is based on the spherical Hecke algebras
\operatorname{End}_{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}_{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})Z}(\sigma))
, where
\sigma
is an irreducible representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{F}_{p})
viewed as a representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})Z
via inflation and letting the central element
p
act via a fixed non-zero scalar. Namely, Barthel and Livné show that the Hecke algebra is a polynomial ring
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}[T]
in a single Hecke operator
T
, corresponding to the double coset
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})Z\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})Z
in a suitable sense, and they observe that any of the irreducible objects admitting a central character is a quotient of the cokernel of a linear polynomial in
T
, for some
\sigma
. For
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}^{\times}
, they compute the cokernel
\operatorname{coker}(T-\lambda)
to be, up to semi-simplification, equal to a principal series, which is generically irreducible and otherwise of length two; they called the remaining irreducible representations, i.e. those arising as a quotient of
\operatorname{coker}(T)
for some
\sigma
, supersingular. It was Breuil who proved the cokernel of
T
to be in fact irreducible by computing the subspace of vectors fixed by the standard pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup
I_{1}
, implying that any smooth irreducible
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
-representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
admitting a central character is admissible – Berger
[
2
]
later proved the existence of central characters for any smooth irreducible
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
-representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
.

The consideration of
I_{1}
-invariants was studied more generally by Vignéras
[
30
]
who proved that passing to
I_{1}
-invariants defines a bijection between the set of isomorphism classes of smooth irreducible (admissible)
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
-representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
and the set of isomorphism classes of (finite dimensional) simple right modules of the pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}=\operatorname{End}_{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}_{I_{1}}(\mathbf{1}))
, where
\mathbf{1}
denotes the trivial
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}^{\times}
-valued character of
I_{1}
. We emphasize that her result relies on Breuil’s previously mentioned computation. Ollivier
[
20
]
took this further by establishing that, at least after fixing a central character, the adjoint pair
(-\otimes_{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}_{I_{1}}(\mathbf{1}),(-)^{I_{1}})
defines an equivalence between the category of right
\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}
-modules and the category of smooth
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
-representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
generated by their
I_{1}
-invariants.

On the other hand, to the best of the author’s knowledge, the smooth
genuine
mod-
p
representation theory of
\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
has not yet been investigated. The aim of this work is to fill this gap in the literature and prove a complete classification result for the irreducible objects. It is the point of view of Vignéras and Ollivier that we will use to tackle this task. We also obtain a clear picture in terms of the approach of Barthel-Livné in the genuine setting. Despite not finding any existing results for the covering of the general linear group, we point out that Peskin
[
24
]
studied the smooth mod-
p
representation theory of the metaplectic covering of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
by considering spherical Hecke algebras; however, it only allowed her to obtain a classification up the supersingular representations. We are optimistic that, by studying the restriction of the irreducible
\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
-representations to the covering of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
, one should be able to recover and complete her classification.

Finally, we note that Breuil
[
5
]
computed, depending on small weights, the mod-
p
reduction of locally algebraic unramified principal series, the unitary completions of which were later
[
3
]
shown to be irreducible admissible
p
-adic Banach space representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
.

The metaplectic counterpart of this task is interesting for two reasons: 1) it might shed some light on how to “lift” a mod-
p
Shimura correspondence to a
p
-adic one and 2) his computations led Breuil to a formulation of a semi-simple mod-
p
local Langlands correspondence (LLC) for
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
, which ultimately led to a
p
-adic LLC
[
7
]
, and so a possible semi-simple mod-
p

metaplectic
LLC for
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
might give some valuable information about a
p
-adic version.

We check that Breuil’s computations also go through in our setting if the weight is at most
p-1
. While we do not attempt to formulate a mod-
p
Shimura correspondence, we do establish a relation between the smooth genuine mod-
p
representation theory of the metaplectic covering group of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
and Galois representations in
[
31
]
.

1.1.
Results

Fix an odd prime number
p
and let
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
be our field of coefficients.
1
1

1

In the main text we work over an arbitrary algebraically closed field of characteristic
p
.
Put
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
so that the metaplectic covering takes the form

1\to\mu_{2}\to\widetilde{G}\to G\to 1.

It splits over the maximal compact open subgroup
K=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
and thus uniquely over the pro-
p
group
I_{1}
, so that we can unambiguously identify
\widetilde{I}_{1}=I_{1}\times\mu_{2}
as groups. Let
\iota\colon\mu_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}\subset\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}^{\times}
denote the non-trivial character, and let
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p})
denote the category of smooth
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
-representations of
\widetilde{G}
, which are genuine, i.e. on which the central subgroup
\mu_{2}
acts via
\iota
. Let
\mathcal{H}=\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\iota))
be the genuine version of the pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra. Denoting by
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{H}}
the category of right
\mathcal{H}
-modules, we have the adjoint pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I})
,

\mathcal{T}(-)=(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\iota)\colon\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{H}}\rightleftarrows\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p})\penalty\mspace{6muplus1mu}{:}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu\mathpunct{}\mspace{2mu}(-)^{I_{1}}=\mathcal{I}(-).

Theorem 1.1
.

The adjoint pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I})
defines a bijection

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Simple right}\\
\text{$\mathcal{H}$-modules}\end{array}\right\}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Smooth irreducible genuine}\\
\text{$\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}$-representations of $\widetilde{G}$}\end{array}\right\},

where both sides are considered up to isomorphism.

This result is a consequence of theorems
4.31
,
4.54
and
5.17
, the last of which says that being of finite length as a smooth
\widetilde{G}
-representation is equivalent to being finitely generated and admissible, as well as a careful analysis of the objects appearing on the left-hand side. In order to describe the latter, we follow the approach of Vignéras in
[
30
]
and decompose the Hecke algebra into a direct sum of smaller algebras whose centers we compute to be either isomorphic to
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY)
(regular case) or to
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}[\mathfrak{Z},Z^{\pm 1}]
(non-regular case). We show that a simple
\mathcal{H}
-module is finite dimensional and thus admits a central character. It will be called supersingular if it is killed by
X
and
Y
(resp. by
\mathfrak{Z}
), otherwise we call the module principal. This leads to the following result, which is a summary of propositions
3.27
,
3.28
and
3.36
.

Theorem 1.2
.

The simple right
\mathcal{H}
-modules fall into two subclasses:

(a)

Principal modules

(b)

Supersingular modules.

Each of these subclasses is in itself described explicitly.

As a consequences of the two theorems, we obtain an explicit description of the smooth irreducible genuine
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
-representations of
\widetilde{G}
. In particular, there are only two kinds of such objects: principal series and supersingular representations (i.e. those not occuring as a subquotient of a principal series). From the point of view of modules over the pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra, the supersingular objects naturally come in pairs. Moreover, it turns out that a genuine principal series is always irreducible; they are in bijective correspondence with pairs of smooth characters on the subgroup
S
of squares in
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
: We have an equality of groups
\widetilde{B}_{2}=B_{2}\times\mu_{2}
, where
B_{2}
is the subgroup of
G
consisting of those upper-triangular matrices having elements of even valuation on the diagonal, and the correspondence is given by
(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})\mapsto\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\chi_{2}^{\prime}\boxtimes\iota)
for any extension
\chi_{i}^{\prime}
of
\chi_{i}
to the subgroup
p^{2\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.

As for the spherical Hecke algebras, having fixed an explicit splitting
\widetilde{K}\cong K\times\mu_{2}
, we may form the smooth irreducible genuine
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
-representation
\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}^{2})\boxtimes\iota
of
\widetilde{K}
for
0\leq r\leq p-1
. The center
Z(\widetilde{G})
being equal to the product
Z^{\operatorname{sq}}\times\mu_{2}
, we may extend it to a smooth genuine
\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})
-representation by letting the central element
p^{2}
act trivially. Inside the spherical Hecke algebra
\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}^{2})\boxtimes\iota))
one then has the Hecke operator
\widetilde{T}
corresponding to the double coset represented by a lift of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, which also exists in characteristic
0
, i.e. if
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
is replaced by
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
or
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
. We summarize propositions
5.2
,
6.7
and Theorem
5.13
as follows.

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume that
0\leq r\leq p-1
and let
a_{p}\in\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
.

(i)

The spherical Hecke algebra
\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}^{2})\boxtimes\iota))=\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}[\widetilde{T}]
is a polynomial ring in the Hecke operator
\widetilde{T}
.

(ii)

The canonical map

\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{2})\boxtimes\iota)/(\widetilde{T}-a_{p})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{2})\boxtimes\iota)/(\widetilde{T}-a_{p})

is injective and defines a
\widetilde{G}
-stable bounded lattice.

(iii)

Let
\bar{a}_{p}\in\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
denote the reduction of
a_{p}
modulo the maximal ideal in
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
.

•

If
\bar{a}_{p}
is non-zero, then
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}^{2})\boxtimes\iota)/(\widetilde{T}-\bar{a}_{p})
is a principal series representation.

•

If
\bar{a}_{p}=0
, then
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}^{2})\boxtimes\iota)/(\widetilde{T})
is an extension of two distinct supersingular representations, which is split if and only if
r=\frac{p-1}{2}
.

The pairs of genuine supersingular representations appearing in part (iii) are precisely those alluded to above in terms of the classification of simple
\mathcal{H}
-modules.

In Corollary
6.5
, we also give a description of the locally algebraic representation appearing in part (ii) of the theorem in terms of locally algebraic
irreducible

\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-representations – in fact, it will generically be a locally algebraic irreducible unramified principal series. This is deduced from the corresponding statement
[
5
]
for the group
G
by using Savin’s local Shimura correspondence
[
27
]
formulated in terms of Iwahori Hecke algebras.

While we have not investigated the existence (let alone its mod-
p
reduction) of a
\widetilde{G}
-stable bounded lattice in such a locally algebraic representation in case
p\leq r\leq 2(p-1)
, we still expect the metaplectic version of
[
5
, Proposition 5.3.4.1]
to be true. We can prove a necessary consequence of such a result, namely the non-vanishing of the Ext-space in part (iii) of the following proposition, see Proposition
4.36
. For two smooth characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
of
A=p^{2\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, define the corresponding normalized principal series by
\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})=\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}((\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2}\omega^{-1})\boxtimes\iota)
only depending on the restriction of
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
to the subgroup
S
of squares in
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
, where
\omega\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}
is the projection onto the torsion part.

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
and
\chi_{1}^{\prime},\chi_{2}^{\prime}
be two pairs of smooth characters
A\to\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}^{\times}
.

(i)

The space

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2}),\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1}^{\prime},\chi_{2}^{\prime}))=0

vanishes
unless
(\chi^{\prime}_{1}|_{S},\chi^{\prime}_{2}|_{S})
is equal to
(\chi_{1}|_{S},\chi_{2}|_{S})
or
(\chi_{2}|_{S},\chi_{1}|_{S})
.

(ii)

The map

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{T_{2}}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2}\omega^{-1},\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2}\omega^{-1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2}),\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})),

induced by the exact functor
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(-\boxtimes\iota)
, is an isomorphism, where
T_{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A&0\\
0&A\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

(iii)

If
\chi_{1}|_{S}\neq\chi_{2}|_{S}
, then

\dim_{\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2}),\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{2},\chi_{1}))=1.

This proposition is proved by comparing it to the situation for the group
G
, where the statement is known, see for example
[
10
]
.

1.2.
Conventions and notations

Fix an odd prime number
p
and an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
p
. Denote by
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
the completion of the rationals
\mathbf{Q}
with respect to the
p
-adic valuation
v\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}\to\mathbf{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
. We use the standard notations

G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})\supset B=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbf{Q}_{p}\\
0&\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\end{pmatrix}\supset T=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\end{pmatrix}\supset Z,

where
Z\cong\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
denotes the center of
G
, and we let
K=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
be the standard maximal compact open subgroup of
G
.
It will be convenient to also introduce the following subgroups of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
:

\mu_{2}=\{\pm 1\},\hskip 2.84544ptA=p^{2\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\supset S=\{x^{2}:x\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\}.

For a topological group
\mathscr{G}
, we denote by
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\mathscr{G}}(k)
the category of
k
-linear smooth representations of
\mathscr{G}
. Here, smooth means that the stabilizer of each vector is open.

If
\mathscr{H}\subset\mathscr{G}
is a closed subgroup and
\pi\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\mathscr{H}}(k)
, we denote the smooth induced representation by
\operatorname{Ind}^{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathscr{H}}(\pi)
. It is right adjoint to the restriction functor
(-)|_{\mathscr{H}}
, which we will refer to as Frobenius reciprocity.

If
\mathscr{H}
is an open subgroup, we denote the compact induction of
\pi
by
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathscr{H}}(\pi)
. It is the subrepresentation of
\operatorname{Ind}^{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathscr{H}}(\pi)
consisting of those functions having finite support modulo
\mathscr{H}
, and defines a left adjoint to the restriction functor
(-)|_{\mathscr{H}}
, which we will also refer to as Frobenius reciprocity.

For
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\mathscr{H}}(k)
, we will identify
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathscr{H}}(\sigma_{1}),\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathscr{H}}(\sigma_{2}))
with the
k
-vector space

\left\{\varphi\colon\mathscr{G}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}):\begin{array}[]{l}\diamond\hskip 2.84544pt\varphi(h_{2}gh_{1})=\sigma_{2}(h_{2})\varphi(g)\sigma_{1}(h_{1}),\forall h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathscr{H},g\in\mathscr{G}\\
\diamond\hskip 2.84544pt|\mathscr{H}\setminus\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)/\mathscr{H}|<\infty\end{array}\right\}

with left
\operatorname{End}_{\mathscr{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathscr{H}}(\sigma_{2}))
-, resp. right
\operatorname{End}_{\mathscr{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathscr{H}}(\sigma_{1}))
-module structure given by the convolution product

(\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2})(g)=\sum_{x\in\mathscr{H}\setminus\mathscr{G}}\varphi_{1}(gx^{-1})\varphi_{2}(x),\text{ for all $g\in\mathscr{G}$.}

For a subgroup
\mathscr{H}\subset\mathscr{G}
,
\pi\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\mathscr{H}}(k)
and
g\in\mathscr{G}
, we denote by
\pi^{g}=\pi(g(-)g^{-1})\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{g^{-1}\mathscr{H}g}(k)
the conjugated representation.

If
\mathscr{G}
is abelian, we write
\mathscr{G}^{\operatorname{sq}}=\{g^{2}:g\in\mathscr{G}\}
for the subgroup of squares.

For a ring
R
, we denote by
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}
the category of right
R
-modules and by
M_{n\times n}(R)
the ring of
n\times n
-matrices with entries in
R
, for
n\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

2.
The metaplectic covering group

The metaplectic covering group is defined by an explicit cocycle relying on the Hilbert symbol, which we briefly remind the reader of following
[
19
, V. §3]
.

2.1.
Hilbert symbol

Let
L=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\sqrt{\smash[b]{\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}}})=\mathbf{Q}_{p^{2}}(\sqrt{\smash[b]{p}})
be the unique abelian extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
with norm group
S
. By local class field theory and Kummer theory, respectively, we have isomorphisms
\operatorname{Gal}(L/\mathbf{Q}_{p})\cong\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}/S
and
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Gal}(L/\mathbf{Q}_{p}),\mu_{2})\cong\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}/S
. In particular, the evaluation map
\operatorname{Gal}(L/\mathbf{Q}_{p})\times\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Gal}(L/\mathbf{Q}_{p}),\mu_{2})\to\mu_{2}

is identified with a non-degenerate bilinear map

(-,-)\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}/S\times\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}/S\to\mu_{2},

which is called the
Hilbert symbol
. We will usually view it as a bilinear map on
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\times\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
via inflation.

Denote by
\omega\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow[\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}]\cong\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}
the projection onto the torsion part, where
[-]
denotes the Teichmüller lift. By
[
19
, V. Satz (3.4)]
, we then have

(1)

(a,b)=\omega\left((-1)^{v(a)v(b)}\frac{b^{v(a)}}{a^{v(b)}}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\text{ for all $a,b\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}$.}

From this one deduces the following properties of the Hilbert symbol.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
. Then

(i)

(aa^{\prime},b)=(a,b)(a^{\prime},b)
;

(ii)

(a,bb^{\prime})=(a,b)(a,b^{\prime})
;

(iii)

(a,b)^{-1}=(b,a)
;

(iv)

(a,1-a)=1
and
(a,-a)=1
;

(v)

(a,x)=1
for all
x\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
if and only if
a\in S
;

(vi)

(a,b)=(b,a)
;

(vii)

If
a,b\in A
, then
(a,b)=1
.

Proof.

Parts (i)-(v) are contained in
[
19
, V. Satz (3.2)]
, while (vi) and (vii) are immediate from (
1
) noting that an element in
\mu_{2}
is self-inverse.
∎

2.2.
Definition and first properties

The
metaplectic covering of
G
is the central topological extension

(2)

1\to\mu_{2}\to\widetilde{G}\to G\to 1

defined by the two-cocycle
\sigma\colon G\times G\to\mu_{2}
,

(3)

\sigma(g_{1},g_{2})=\left(\frac{\mathfrak{c}(g_{1}g_{2})}{\mathfrak{c}(g_{1})},\frac{\mathfrak{c}(g_{1}g_{2})}{\mathfrak{c}(g_{2})}\det(g_{1})\right)\text{ for all $(g_{1},g_{2})\in G\times G$},

where
\mathfrak{c}\colon G\to\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
is the function
\mathfrak{c}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\begin{cases}c\text{ if $c\neq 0$}\\
d\text{ if $c=0$,}\end{cases}
cf.
[
16
, p. 41]
.

In other words,
\widetilde{G}=G\times\mu_{2}
and the product of two elements
(g_{i},\zeta_{i})
,
i=1,2
, is equal to
(g_{1},\zeta_{1})(g_{2},\zeta_{2})=(g_{1}g_{2},\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\sigma(g_{1},g_{2}))
.

Notation 2.2
.

Given a subset
D\subset G
, we denote by
\widetilde{D}
its preimage in
\widetilde{G}
under the projection map
\widetilde{G}\to G
. For
g\in G
, we put
\tilde{g}=(g,1)
.

Example 2.3
.

The multiplication law on the covering group
\widetilde{T}
of the torus
T
is given by

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}&0\\
0&y_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{2}&0\\
0&y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}x_{2}&0\\
0&y_{1}y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),(y_{2},x_{1})\right),

for all
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
,
i=1,2
. In particular, it is not abelian, cf. Lemma
2.6
(ii) below. We also note that for all
x,y\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
,

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&y\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)^{-1}=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x^{-1}&0\\
0&y^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right),(y,x)\right)\text{ and }\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&x\\
y&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)^{-1}=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&y^{-1}\\
x^{-1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right).

Lemma 2.4
.

The metaplectic covering (
2
) splits over the subgroups
K=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
,
B_{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A&\mathbf{Q}_{p}\\
0&A\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
B_{S}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbf{Q}_{p}\\
0&S\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. More precisely:

(i)

The map
K\to\mu_{2}
given by

g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{cases}(c,d{\det(g)}^{-1})\text{ if }c\in p\mathbf{Z}_{p}\setminus\{0\}\\
1\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

defines a splitting
K\times\mu_{2}\cong\widetilde{K}
.

(ii)

We have equalities of groups
\widetilde{B}_{2}=B_{2}\times\mu_{2}
,
\widetilde{B}_{S}=B_{S}\times\mu_{2}
.

Proof.

Part (i) is contained in
[
16
, p. 43]
, while part (ii) follows from the cocycle
\sigma
being trivial when restricted to the subgroups
B_{2}
and
B_{S}
, respectively, which is a consequence of Lemma
2.1
.
∎

Convention 2.5
.

From now on we will fix the splitting on
K
defined in part (i) of the lemma and view subgroups of
K
as subgroups of
\widetilde{K}\subset\widetilde{G}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

We have the following equalities of groups describing the centers of
\widetilde{G}
and
\widetilde{T}
, respectively:

(i)

Z(\widetilde{G})=Z^{\operatorname{sq}}\times\mu_{2}
;

(ii)

Z(\widetilde{T})=T^{\operatorname{sq}}\times\mu_{2}
.

Proof.

Note that by Lemma
2.4
the groups on the right-hand side of the equation are indeed subgroups of
\widetilde{G}
. Property (v) of the Hilbert symbol in Lemma
2.1
implies that they are contained in the respective center. For proving the converse inclusions, we first consider part (ii): Let
(t,\zeta)\in Z(\widetilde{T})
. We may assume that
\zeta=1
as
\mu_{2}
is a central subgroup. By the formula for the multiplication law in Example
2.3
, the commuting of
\tilde{t}
with elements in
K\cap T
forces the diagonal entries of
t
to lie in
A
, and the commuting with
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)
and
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)
then forces
t\in T^{\operatorname{sq}}
, which finishes the proof of (ii). Part (i) now follows from the inclusion
Z(\widetilde{G})\subset Z(\widetilde{T})\cap\widetilde{Z}
.
∎

2.3.
Genuine representations and further notations

Fix the non-trivial character
\iota\colon\mu_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}\subset k^{\times}
. Let
\mathscr{G}\subset G
be a closed subgroup.

Definition 2.7
.

We let
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{G}},\iota}(k)
denote the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{G}}}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p})
consisting of
genuine
representations, i.e. those objects on which
\mu_{2}
acts via the non-trivial character
\iota
.

Since
p
is prime to the order of the central subgroup
\mu_{2}
, we have a decomposition of categories

\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{G}}}(k)=\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{G}},\iota}(k)\times\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\mathscr{G}}(k),

where the second factor is viewed as a full subcategory via inflation.

Recall that a smooth representation
\pi\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{G}}}(k)
is admissible if the subspace
\pi^{\mathscr{H}}
of
\mathscr{H}
-fixed vectors is finite dimensional for every open subgroups
\mathscr{H}\subset\mathscr{G}
. Since the identity in
\mathscr{G}
admits a basis of open neighbourhood consisting of pro-
p
groups and we are working with mod-
p
coefficients, admissibility can be checked for a single open pro-
p
subgroup
\mathscr{H}
, see
[
15
, Proposition 36]
. Examples of open pro-
p
subgroups of
K
(and hence of
\widetilde{G}
) are given by the congruence subgroup of level
m\geq 1
and the pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup:

K_{m}=1+p^{m}M_{2\times 2}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}),\hskip 2.84544ptI_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p}&\mathbf{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbf{Z}_{p}&1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p}\end{pmatrix}.

Let
I=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbf{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbf{Z}_{p}&\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{pmatrix}
denote the Iwahori subgroup and set
H=\begin{pmatrix}[\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}]&0\\
0&[\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}]\end{pmatrix}
, we have the semi-direct product decomposition
I=I_{1}\rtimes H
.

Finally, let us agree on the following notation: given a subgroup
C\subset G
together with a splitting
\widetilde{C}\cong C\times\mu_{2}
, we denote the image of an object
\pi
under the induced equivalence
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{C}(k)\cong\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{C},\iota}(k)
by
\pi\boxtimes\iota
.

3.
The pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra

In this section, we study the pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra by decomposing it into smaller algebras which we then describe explicitly, enabling us to determine the simple modules.

By Lemma
2.4
(i), the metaplectic covering splits over the maximal compact open subgroup and thus also over the pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup
I_{1}
. Since
p
is odd, any such splitting is unique and we in fact have an equality of groups
\widetilde{I}_{1}=I_{1}\times\mu_{2}
because
1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p}\subset S
so that the splitting in the cited lemma is trivial on
I_{1}
. We will simply view the elements of
I_{1}
as elements of
\widetilde{I}_{1}
and drop the
\mu_{2}
-component (which is
1
) from the notation. Taking the trivial character
\mathbf{1}
on
I_{1}
, we obtain the genuine
k^{\times}
-valued character
\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota
on
\widetilde{I}_{1}
, which is just the inflation of
\iota
.

Definition 3.1
.

The
pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}
is the endomorphism ring of the compact induction
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)
in the category of
\widetilde{G}
-representations, i.e.
\mathcal{H}=\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota))
.

As agreed upon in our list of conventions and notations
1.2
, we will identify
\mathcal{H}
with the
k
-algebra

\left\{\varphi\colon I_{1}\setminus\widetilde{G}/I_{1}\to k:\begin{array}[]{l}\diamond\hskip 2.84544pt\varphi(g\zeta)=\zeta\varphi(g),\forall g\in\widetilde{G},\zeta\in\mu_{2}\\
\diamond\hskip 2.84544pt|\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)|<\infty\end{array}\right\},

the product being given by convolution.

3.1.
Generators and relations

In this subsection, we prove some useful identities of Hecke operators by following
[
21
]
, which treats the case of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
over a local field. We start by writing down a basis of
\mathcal{H}
as a
k
-vector space. Let
N_{G}(T)
be the normalizer of
T
in
G
. The following two elements of
N_{G}(T)
will play a prominent role in this work:

s=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }\Pi=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{pmatrix}.

Putting
W_{1}:=N_{G}(T)/(I_{1}\cap T)
, we have the decomposition

(4)

\widetilde{G}=\bigsqcup_{w\in W_{1}}\widetilde{I}_{1}\tilde{w}\widetilde{I}_{1},

see also
[
21
, Lemma 2.0.4]
. The “genuine characteristic functions” of the double cosets appearing in this decomposition will define a
k
-basis of
\mathcal{H}
– provided they are well-defined, which we now check.

Lemma 3.2
.

For all
g\in I_{1}
and
n\in N_{G}(T)
,
\tilde{n}g(\tilde{n})^{-1}=(ngn^{-1},1)
. In particular, for all
n\in N_{G}(T)
,
\mu_{2}\cap I_{1}\tilde{n}I_{1}(\tilde{n})^{-1}=\{1\}
.

Proof.

Recall that
N_{G}(T)=T\sqcup sT
. Let
n\in N_{G}(T)
and write
g=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in I_{1}
. The end of Example
2.3
gives a formula for the inverse
(\tilde{n})^{-1}
of
\tilde{n}=(n,1)
. Distinguishing between the cases
c=0
and
c\neq 0
, if
n\in T
, and the cases
b=0
and
b\neq 0
, if
n\in sT
, the lemma follows from the definition of the cocycle (
3
) and the identities of the Hilbert symbol in Lemma
2.1
, bearing in mind that
1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p}\subset S
.
∎

Definition 3.3
.

For
w\in W_{1}
, let
T_{w}\in\mathcal{H}
be the unique function with

\operatorname{supp}(T_{w})=\widetilde{I}_{1}\tilde{w}\widetilde{I}_{1}\text{ and }T_{w}(\tilde{w})=1.

If
n\in N_{G}(T)
, then we also write
T_{n}
for the operator defined by the class of
n
in
W_{1}
.

Corollary 3.4
.

The operators
T_{w}
, for
w\in W_{1}
, are well-defined and the collection
\{T_{w}\}_{w\in W_{1}}
is a
k
-basis of
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

For
t\in T\cap I_{1}
, we have
\tilde{g}\tilde{t}=\widetilde{gt}
for all
g\in G
, so the condition
T_{w}(\tilde{w})=1
is independent of the choice of a representative of
w
. By Lemma
3.2
, the operator
T_{w}
is then well-defined. The decomposition (
4
) implies the assertion about the basis.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Let
n_{1},n_{2}\in N_{G}(T)
. Writing
I_{1}n_{2}I_{1}=\sqcup_{x\in I_{1}/(I_{1}\cap n_{2}^{-1}I_{1}n_{2})}I_{1}n_{2}x
, the convolution product of
T_{n_{1}}
and
T_{n_{2}}
reads

(5)

(T_{n_{1}}T_{n_{2}})(g)=\sum_{x\in I_{1}/(I_{1}\cap n_{2}^{-1}I_{1}n_{2})}T_{n_{1}}(gx^{-1}(\tilde{n}_{2})^{-1})\text{ for all $g\in\widetilde{G}$.}

In particular, if
n_{2}
or
n_{1}
normalizes
I_{1}
, then
T_{n_{1}}T_{n_{2}}=\zeta T_{n_{1}n_{2}}
, where
\zeta\in\mu_{2}
is such that
(n_{1}n_{2},1)(n_{2},1)^{-1}=(n_{1},\zeta)
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\widehat{H}=\operatorname{Hom}(H,\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times})=\operatorname{Hom}(H,k^{\times})
be the group of
k^{\times}
-valued characters of
H
. For
\chi\in\widehat{H}
, define the Hecke operator

e_{\chi}=\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h\in H}\chi(h)T_{h}.

Lemma 3.7
.

The following identities hold true for all characters
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\widehat{H}
:

(i)

e_{\chi}^{2}=e_{\chi}
;

(ii)

e_{\chi}e_{\chi^{\prime}}=0
if
\chi\neq\chi^{\prime}
;

(iii)

\sum_{\chi\in\widehat{H}}e_{\chi}=1
;

(iv)

T_{s}e_{\chi}=e_{\chi^{s}}T_{s}
;

(v)

T_{s}^{2}e_{\chi}=\begin{cases}0\text{ if }\chi\neq\chi^{s}\\
-\chi(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right))T_{s}e_{\chi}\text{ if }\chi=\chi^{s}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The identities can be checked in the endomorphism ring of the induction
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{K}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)
. Via the splitting
\widetilde{K}\cong K\times\mu_{2}
, this is identified with the endomorphism ring of
\operatorname{Ind}^{K}_{I_{1}}(\mathbf{1})
, so the lemma follows from
[
21
, Lemma 2.0.10, Lemma 2.0.12]
.
∎

Definition 3.8
.

Given a character
\chi\in\widehat{H}
and a pair
(i,j)\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
, define the twisted character
\chi[i,j]\in\widehat{H}
by

\displaystyle\chi[i,j]\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}[\lambda]&0\\
0&[\mu]\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}[\lambda]&0\\
0&[\mu]\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)(\lambda^{i}\mu^{j})^{\frac{p-1}{2}},\text{for all $\lambda,\mu\in\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}$.}

Recall that we put
\Pi=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in N_{G}(T)
, which normalizes
I_{1}
. We will make use of Remark
3.5
as well as the identities in Example
2.3
during the proof of the following lemma.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
. The following assertions hold true.

(i)

T_{\Pi}
is invertible with inverse
T_{\Pi^{-1}}
;

(ii)

T_{\Pi}^{2}=T_{\Pi^{2}}
;

(iii)

T_{\Pi}^{4}=(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}T_{\Pi^{4}}
is central, and
T_{n}T_{\Pi^{4}}=T_{n\Pi^{4}}=T_{\Pi^{4}n}
for all
n\in N_{G}(T)
;

(iv)

T_{\Pi^{2}}T_{s}=T_{\Pi^{2}s}=T_{s\Pi^{2}}=(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}T_{s}T_{\Pi^{2}}
;

(v)

for all
\chi\in\widehat{H}
,
T_{\Pi}e_{\chi}=e_{\chi^{s}[1,0]}T_{\Pi}
;

(vi)

(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{m}=T_{(s\Pi)^{m}}
;

(vii)

(T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{m}=T_{(\Pi s)^{m}}
;

(viii)

T_{(s\Pi)^{m}}T_{s}=T_{(s\Pi)^{m}s}=T_{s}T_{(\Pi s)^{m}}
;

(ix)

T_{\Pi}T_{(s\Pi)^{m}}=T_{\Pi(s\Pi)^{m}}=T_{(\Pi s)^{m}\Pi}=T_{(\Pi s)^{m}}T_{\Pi}
;

Proof.

(i) This follows from
(\Pi,1)^{-1}=(\Pi^{-1},1)
.

(ii) This follows from
(\Pi^{2},1)(\Pi^{-1},1)=(1,1)
.

(iii) The first equality follows from
(\Pi^{2},1)^{2}=(\Pi^{4},(p,p))=(\Pi^{4},(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}})
. This element is central element in
\widetilde{G}
by Lemma
2.6
(i), which implies the other equalities.

(iv) The first equality follows from
(\Pi^{2}s,1)(s,1)=(\Pi^{2},1)
, the second one is trivial, and the last equality follows from
(s\Pi^{2},1)(\Pi^{2},1)^{-1}=(s\Pi^{2},1)(\Pi^{-2},(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}})=(s,(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}})
.

(v) Let
h=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}[\lambda]&0\\
0&[\mu]\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in H
and denote by
h^{s}=shs
its conjugate by
s
. The two equalities
(\Pi h,1)(h^{-1},1)=(\Pi,1)
and
(h^{s}\Pi,1)(\Pi^{-1},1)=(h^{s},\mu^{\frac{p-1}{2}})
translate into

T_{\Pi}T_{h}=T_{\Pi h}=T_{h^{s}\Pi}=\mu^{\frac{p-1}{2}}T_{h^{s}}T_{\Pi},

which implies the assertion.

(vi) The case
m=1
follows from the equality
(s\Pi,1)(\Pi^{-1},1)=(s,1)
. It now suffices to prove
T_{(s\Pi)^{m}}T_{s\Pi}=T_{(s\Pi)^{m+1}}
for all
m\geq 1
. Since the support of the product of two operators is contained in the product of the supports (as follows from the definition of the convolution product), the left-hand side has support contained in the preimage in
\widetilde{G}
of

I_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{m}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)I_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)I_{1}=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}I_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{m}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&[\lambda]\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)I_{1}=I_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{m+1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)I_{1}.

The decomposition
I_{1}s\Pi I_{1}=\sqcup_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}I_{1}s\Pi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p[\lambda]&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
yields that the evaluation of
T_{(s\Pi)^{m}}T_{s\Pi}
at
((s\Pi)^{m+1},1)
is equal to

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}T_{(s\Pi)^{m}}\left(((s\Pi)^{m+1},1)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-p[\lambda]&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)(s\Pi,1)^{-1}\right).

If
\lambda\neq 0
, then
(s\Pi)^{m+1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-p[\lambda]&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)(s\Pi)^{-1}
does not lie in
I_{1}(s\Pi)^{m}I_{1}
, so only the summand corresponding to
\lambda=0
contributes and gives the value
1
.

(vii) This is analogous to (vi).

(viii) Since
I_{1}(s\Pi)^{m}I_{1}sI_{1}=I_{1}(s\Pi)^{m}sI_{1}
, it is enough to evaluate at
((s\Pi)^{m}s,1)
. Now
I_{1}sI_{1}=\sqcup_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}I_{1}s\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&[\lambda]\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, so

T_{(s\Pi)^{m}}T_{s}(((s\Pi)^{m}s,1))=\sum_{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}}T_{(s\Pi)^{m}}\left(((s\Pi)^{m}s,1)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&[\lambda]\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)(s,1)\right).

If
\lambda\neq 0
, then
(s\Pi)^{m}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
[\lambda]&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
does not lie in
I_{1}(s\Pi)^{m}I_{1}
, so only the summand corresponding to
\lambda=0
contributes and gives the value
1
. This proves the first equality. The second one is proved similarly.

(ix) The first identity follows from

(\Pi(s\Pi)^{m},1)((s\Pi)^{-m},1)=(\Pi,(p^{-m},p)(-p^{m+1},p^{-m}))=(\Pi,1),

the second one is trivial, the last one follows from
((\Pi s)^{m}\Pi,1)(\Pi^{-1},1)=((\Pi s)^{m},1)
.
∎

Corollary 3.10
.

The operator
T_{\Pi}
restricts to a
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant isomorphism

T_{\Pi}\colon\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi^{s}[1,0]\boxtimes\iota),

for each
\chi\in\widehat{H}
.

Proof.

By definition,
T_{\Pi}
is a
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant endomorphism on
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)
. The assertion follows from Lemma
3.9
(v) and (i), noting that
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)=e_{\chi}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)
.
∎

3.2.
Decomposing the pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra

It follows from Lemma
3.7
that we have the decomposition of
\widetilde{G}
-representations

(6)

\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)=\bigoplus_{\chi\in\widehat{H}}e_{\chi}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)=\bigoplus_{\chi\in\widehat{H}}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi\boxtimes\iota).

As we will see below, there are non-zero intertwinings between two direct summands if and only if the two respective characters are conjugate in the following sense: The group
\widetilde{N_{G}(T)}
acts on the set of genuine characters of
\widetilde{H}
by conjugation. Since
\mu_{2}
is central, this action descends to
N_{G}(T)
. Given
\chi\in\widehat{H}
and
n\in N_{G}(T)
, we let
\chi^{\tilde{n}}\in\widehat{H}
be the unique character satisfying
\chi^{\tilde{n}}\boxtimes\iota=(\chi\boxtimes\iota)^{\tilde{n}}
as genuine characters on
\widetilde{H}=H\times\mu_{2}
. This depends only on the image of
n
in
N_{G}(T)/(T\cap I)
and defines an action of the latter group on
\widehat{H}
. In particular, the subgroup generated by
\Pi
acts on
\widehat{H}
.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
\chi\in\widehat{H}
.

(i)

Define
O_{\chi}=\left\{\chi^{\tilde{w}}:w\in N_{G}(T)/(T\cap I)\right\}
to be the
orbit of
\chi
. Denote the set of all orbits by
\mathcal{O}
.

(ii)

The
\Pi
-orbit of
\chi
is the orbit of
\chi
under the action of the subgroup generated by
\Pi
, i.e. it is equal to
\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
.

Recall from Definition
3.8
that to
\chi
and each pair
(i,j)\in(\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})^{2}
we have associated a twisted character
\chi[i,j]
.

Remark 3.12
.

For
h=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}[\lambda]&0\\
0&[\mu]\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in H
, one has
\tilde{\Pi}\tilde{h}(\tilde{\Pi})^{-1}=(h^{s},\mu^{\frac{p-1}{2}})
, i.e. for
\chi\in\widehat{H}
,
\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}}=\chi^{s}[0,1]
. Thus, the
\Pi
-orbit of
\chi
is equal to

\chi^{\widetilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}=\left\{\chi,\chi^{s}[0,1],\chi[1,1],\chi^{s}[1,0]\right\}.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
\chi\in\widehat{H}
.

(i)

If
(\chi^{-1}\chi^{s})^{2}\neq 1
, then the map

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}\times(\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})^{2}

\displaystyle\cong O_{\chi}

\displaystyle(a,(i,j))

\displaystyle\mapsto\chi^{s^{a}}[i,j]

is bijective.

(ii)

If
(\chi^{-1}\chi^{s})^{2}=1
, then
the map

\displaystyle(\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})^{2}

\displaystyle\cong O_{\chi}

\displaystyle(i,j)

\displaystyle\mapsto\chi[i,j]

is bijective.

Proof.

The group
N_{G}(T)/(T\cap I)
is generated by the elements
\Pi
and
s
. Remark
3.12
gives a description of the
\Pi
-orbit. Since
(\chi\boxtimes\iota)^{\tilde{s}}=\chi^{s}\boxtimes\iota
, we deduce that the map in (i) is surjective. Since we cannot have
\chi=\chi[i,j]
for
0\neq(i,j)\in(\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})^{2}
, the map in (ii) is injective. If additionally
(\chi^{-1}\chi^{s})^{2}\neq 1
, then we can neither have
\chi=\chi^{s}[i,j]
for any non-zero pair
(i,j)
, showing that the map in (i) is also injective. If
(\chi^{-1}\chi^{s})^{2}=1
, we may choose
t\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
such that
\chi^{-1}\chi^{s}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}[\lambda]&0\\
0&[\mu]\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=(\lambda^{-1}\mu)^{t\frac{p-1}{2}}
for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}
, i.e.
\chi^{s}=\chi[t,t]
, proving that the map in (ii) is surjective.
∎

Definition 3.14
.

Let
\chi\in\widehat{H}
.

(i)

We say that
\chi
is
square-regular
if
(\chi^{-1}\chi^{s})^{2}\neq 1
.

(ii)

We say that
\chi
is
non-square-regular
if
(\chi^{-1}\chi^{s})^{2}=1
, i.e.
\chi^{s}=\chi[t,t]
for some
t\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
.

(iii)

We say that an orbit
O\in\mathcal{O}
is (non-)square-regular if one, or equivalently every, character in it is so.

Definition 3.15
.

(i)

For an orbit
O\in\mathcal{O}
, define the
Hecke algebra of
O
by

\mathcal{H}(O)=\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\bigoplus_{\chi\in O}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)).

(ii)

For
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in\widehat{H}
, put

\mathcal{H}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})=\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\iota),\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi_{2}\boxtimes\iota)).

If
\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}
, we simply write
\mathcal{H}(\chi_{1})
.

Proposition 3.16
.

The decomposition (
6
) induces a direct sum decomposition of
k
-algebras

\mathcal{H}=\bigoplus_{O\in\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{H}(O).

Proof.

We have to show that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\iota),\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi_{2}\boxtimes\iota))=0
if
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
lie in distinct orbits. This can be proved as in
[
30
, p. 9]
: For
n\in N_{G}(T)
, let
I_{n}=I\cap n^{-1}In
. Since
I=I_{1}\rtimes H
, we have
I=I_{1}I_{n}
. Using the decomposition
\widetilde{G}=\sqcup_{n\in N_{G}(T)/(T\cap I)}\widetilde{I}\tilde{n}\widetilde{I}
, Mackey’s formula reads

\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi_{2}\boxtimes\iota)|_{\widetilde{I}}\cong\bigoplus_{n\in N_{G}(T)/(T\cap I)}\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{I}}_{\widetilde{I}_{n}}((\chi_{2}\boxtimes\iota)^{\tilde{n}}).

Therefore, using Frobenius reciprocity twice, the non-vanishing of the Hom-space of interest is equivalent to
(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\iota)|_{\widetilde{I}_{n}}=(\chi_{2}\boxtimes\iota)^{\tilde{n}}|_{\widetilde{I}_{n}}
for some
n\in N_{G}(T)
. Smooth characters are trivial on the pro-
p
group
I_{1}
, so the equality
\widetilde{I}=I_{1}\widetilde{I}_{n}
completes the proof.
∎

We finish this section with two lemmas, the first of which will allow us to reduce statements in the non-square-regular case to a consideration of the trivial character, while the second one is a computational preliminary for the upcoming sections, where we determine the Hecke algebra of an orbit explicitly.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\psi\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to k^{\times}
be a smooth character. Then the map

\displaystyle\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\otimes\psi\circ\det

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto[(g,\zeta)\mapsto\psi(\det(g))f(g,\zeta)]

is a
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant isomorphism. The resulting
k
-algebra automorphism

\operatorname{tw}_{\psi}\colon\mathcal{H}=\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\otimes\psi\circ\det)\cong\mathcal{H},

where the equality is induced by the functor
(-)\otimes\psi\circ\det
, identifies
\mathcal{H}(O_{\chi})
with
\mathcal{H}(O_{\chi\psi|_{H}})
.

Proof.

Checking that the map is well-defined
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant is straight forward. Replacing
\psi
by its inverse shows that the map is an isomorphism. The rest is clear.
∎

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
r,s\in\mathbf{Z}
and let
\zeta\in\mu_{2}
.

(i)

Let
x=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in I
. Then
y=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&p^{s-r}b\\
p^{r-s}c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in I
if and only if
s-r+v(b)\geq 0
and
r-s+v(c)\geq 1
. In this case, the equality

(x,\zeta)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{r}&0\\
0&p^{s}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{r}&0\\
0&p^{s}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)(y,1)

holds if and only if

\zeta=\begin{cases}\omega(\det(x))^{r\frac{p-1}{2}}\text{ if }c\neq 0\\
\omega\left(\det(x)^{s}d^{r-s}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\text{ if }c=0.\end{cases}

(ii)

Let
x=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in I
. Then
y=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d&p^{r-s}c\\
p^{s-r}b&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in I
if and only if
r-s+v(c)\geq 0
and
s-r+v(b)\geq 1
. In this case, the equality

(x,\zeta)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{r}\\
p^{s}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{r}\\
p^{s}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)(y,1)

holds if and only if

\zeta=\begin{cases}\omega\left(d^{v(b)+v(c)}\det(x)^{s-v(c)}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\text{ if }c\neq 0,b\neq 0\\
\omega\left(d^{r-s+v(c)}\det(x)^{s-v(c)}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\text{ if }c\neq 0,b=0\\
\omega\left(d^{v(b)}\det(x)^{s}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\text{ if }c=0,b\neq 0\\
\omega\left(d^{r-s}\det(x)^{s}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\text{ if }c=0,b=0.\end{cases}

Proof.

This is straight forward.
∎

3.3.
The Hecke algebra of a regular orbit

In this section, we fix a square-regular orbit
O
and a character
\chi\in O
. Define

\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])=\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\bigoplus_{t=0,1}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\chi[t,0]\boxtimes\iota))=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{H}(\chi)&\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0],\chi)\\
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\chi[1,0])&\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0])\end{pmatrix}.

By Lemma
3.13
, Remark
3.12
and Corollary
3.10
, we have an isomorphism of algebras
\mathcal{H}(O)\cong M_{4\times 4}(\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0]))
and so:

Corollary 3.19
.

The functor

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{H}(O)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\mapsto M(e_{\chi}+e_{\chi[1,0]})

is an equivalence of categories.

In order to understand the simple objects on the left-hand side of the equivalence, it therefore suffices to study the algebra
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])
and its simple modules.

For
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
, we view the Hecke module
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0],\chi[t_{2},0])
as the space

\left\{\varphi\colon\widetilde{G}\to k:\begin{array}[]{l}\diamond\hskip 2.84544pt\varphi((x,\zeta_{x})g(y,\zeta_{y}))=(\chi[t_{2},0]\boxtimes\iota)((x,\zeta_{x}))\varphi(g)(\chi[t_{1},0]\boxtimes\iota)((y,\zeta_{y}))\\
\phantom{\diamond}\text{ for all }g\in\widetilde{G},x,y\in I,\zeta_{x},\zeta_{y}\in\mu_{2}\\
\diamond\hskip 2.84544pt|\widetilde{I}\setminus\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)/\widetilde{I}|<\infty\end{array}\right\}

with left
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0])
-, resp. right
\mathcal{H}[t_{1},0])
-action given by the convolution product. As a consequence of Lemma
3.18
and the regularity assumption on
\chi
, we obtain:

Corollary 3.20
.

For
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
,
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0],\chi[t_{2},0])
has
k
-basis
\{T_{i,t_{1}-t_{2}+j}\}_{i,j\in 2\mathbf{Z}}
, where
T_{i,t_{1}-t_{2}+j}
is uniquely determined by

\operatorname{supp}(T_{i,t_{1}-t_{2}+j})=\widetilde{I}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p^{t_{1}-t_{2}+j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\widetilde{I},\hskip 5.69046ptT_{i,t_{1}-t_{2}+j}\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p^{t_{1}-t_{2}+j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\right)=1.

Proposition 3.21
.

For each
t\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
, the map

\displaystyle k[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{H}(\chi[t,0])

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{2,2}

\displaystyle Z^{-1}

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{-2,-2}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{2,0}

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{0,2}

is a well-defined
k
-algebra isomorphism. In particular, the right-hand side is commutative.

Proof.

The proposition follows from Corollary
3.20
and the three points

(a)

T_{2,2}^{M}T_{i,j}=T_{i+2M,j+2M}=T_{i,j}T_{2,2}^{M}
for all
i,j\in 2\mathbf{Z}
and
M\in\mathbf{Z}
;

(b)

T_{2,0}^{m}=T_{2m,0}
and
T_{0,2}^{m}=T_{0,2m}
for all
m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
;

(c)

T_{2,0}T_{0,2}=0
;

which we quickly explain:

(a)

follows from Lemma
3.9
(iii);

(b)

follows from Lemma
3.9
(vi) and (vii);

(c)

follows from Lemma
3.9
(vi), (vii) (applied to
m=1
) and Lemma
3.7
(v).

∎

Fix now integers
0\leq t_{1},t_{2}\leq 1
. We want to compute
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0],\chi[t_{2},0])
as a left
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0])
- and right
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])
-module, respectively. In view of the previous proposition, we may assume that
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
.

Proposition 3.22
.

For
0\leq t_{1}\neq t_{2}\leq 1
, the maps of left
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0])
-, resp. right
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])
-modules,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0])/(T_{2,0})\oplus\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0])/(T_{0,2})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0],\chi[t_{2},0])

\displaystyle(1,0)

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{0,1}

\displaystyle(0,1)

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{2,1}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])/(T_{2,0})\oplus\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])/(T_{0,2})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0],\chi[t_{2},0])

\displaystyle(1,0)

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{0,1}

\displaystyle(0,1)

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{2,1}

are well-defined isomorphisms.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.20
, the space
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0],\chi[t_{2},0])
has
k
-basis
\{T_{i,1+j}\}_{i,j\in 2\mathbf{Z}}
. The proposition now follows from the identities, for
m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

(a)

T_{2,0}T_{0,1}=0=T_{0,1}T_{2,0}
;

(b)

T_{0,2}T_{2,1}=0=T_{2,1}T_{0,2}
;

(c)

T_{2,2}T_{i,1+j}=T_{i+2,1+j+2}=T_{i,1+j}T_{2,2}
for all
i,j\in 2\mathbf{Z}
;

(d)

T_{0,2m}T_{0,1}=T_{0,1+2m}=T_{0,1}T_{0,2m}
;

(e)

T_{2m,0}T_{2,1}=T_{2(m+1),1}=T_{2,1}T_{2m,0}
.

To check these, write
T_{0,1+2m}=T_{(\Pi s)^{1+2m}}e_{\chi[t_{1},0]}
,
T_{2(m+1),1}=T_{\Pi^{2}(s\Pi)^{2m+1}}e_{\chi[t_{1},0]}
and, for
t\in\{t_{1},t_{2}\}
,
T_{2,2}=T_{\Pi^{4}}e_{\chi[t,0]}
,
T_{2m,0}=T_{(s\Pi)^{2m}}e_{\chi[t,0]}
,
T_{0,2m}=T_{(\Pi s)^{2m}}e_{\chi[t,0]}
. The points (a)-(e) now follow from Lemma
3.9
, and Lemma
3.7
(v) to prove (a) and (b).
∎

Remark 3.23
.

The points (a)-(e) show that the left action of
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])
agrees with the right action of
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0])
on
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0],\chi[t_{2},0])
under the isomorphism
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])\cong\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0])
resulting from Proposition
3.21
. Moreover, as a consequence of the proposition, we obtain that
\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0],\chi)\cong\mathcal{H}(\chi,\chi[1,0])
as modules over the commutative ring
\mathcal{H}(\chi)\cong\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0])
.

Recall that we defined

\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathcal{H}(\chi)&\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0],\chi)\\
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\chi[1,0])&\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0])\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. By Proposition
3.21
, the
k
-algebras on the diagonal may be identified with
k[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY)
.

Corollary 3.24
.

The center
Z(\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0]))
of
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])
is isomorphic to the
k
-algebra
k[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY)
via the diagonal embedding.

Proof.

Considering elementary diagonal matrices, an element
b\in Z(\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0]))
needs to be diagonal, say
b=\operatorname{diag}(P_{0},P_{1})
, where each
P_{t}\in k[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY)
with
Z=T_{2,2},X=T_{2,0},Y=T_{0,2}\in\mathcal{H}(\chi[t,0])
.
Letting
\mathbf{T}_{0,1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{0,1}\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\mathbf{T}_{2,1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{2,1}\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
the equality
b\mathbf{T}_{0,1}=\mathbf{T}_{0,1}b
forces
P_{1}(0,Y,Z)=P_{0}(0,Y,Z)
, while the equality
b\mathbf{T}_{2,1}=\mathbf{T}_{2,1}b
forces
P_{1}(X,0,Z)=P_{0}(X,0,Z)
. Since
XY=0
, this implies
P_{1}=P_{0}
. It remains to show that such elements indeed lie in the center, which follows from Remark
3.23
.
∎

We now compute to which extend the off-diagonal entries contribute to the diagonal.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
0\leq t_{1}\neq t_{2}\leq 1
. The image of the composition map

\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0],\chi[t_{1},0])\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0])}\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0],\chi[t_{2},0])\to\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])

is equal to the ideal
(T_{2,0},T_{0,2})
generated by
T_{2,0}
and
T_{0,2}
. More precisely,

•

T_{0,1}\circ T_{2,1}=0=T_{2,1}\circ T_{0,1}
;

•

T_{0,1}\circ T_{0,1}=T_{0,2}
;

•

T_{2,1}\circ T_{2,1}=T_{4,2}=T_{2,2}T_{2,0}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.22
, we have

\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{2},0],\chi[t_{1},0])=\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])T_{0,1}+\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])T_{2,1}

and

\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0],\chi[t_{2},0])=T_{0,1}\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])+T_{2,1}\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0]).

Since the composition map is
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t_{1},0])
-equivariant from both sides, it is enough to compute how the generators compose, i.e. it is enough to check the claimed identities. These follow from Lemma
3.9
and Lemma
3.7
(v) by writing the generators
T_{0,1}
and
T_{2,1}
as in the proof of Proposition
3.22
.
∎

3.3.1.
Simple modules

In order to determine the simple modules, we first show that the center acts via scalars in the coefficient field
k
and then study those with a fixed central character.

Lemma 3.26
.

Any simple right
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])
-module admits a central character.

Proof.

To ease notation, put
R=\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])
, and identify the center of
R
with
k[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY)
as in Corollary
3.24
.
Let
M
be a simple right
R
-module. The kernel of the central action map
k[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY)\to\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
must be a prime ideal. In particular, it contains
X
or
Y
and we may without loss of generality assume that it contains
X
. Thus, the action factors through the localized polynomial ring
(k[Y])[Z^{\pm 1}]
over the PID
k[Y]
. The prime ideals in this ring are

(a)

(0)
;

(b)

(g(Z))
for an irreducible polynomial
g(Z)\in(k[Y])[Z^{\pm 1}]
;

(c)

(Y-y,Z-z)
for
y\in k
and
z\in k^{\times}
.

We need to exclude that the kernel of the map
(k[Y])[Z^{\pm 1}]\to\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
falls into (a) or (b). In case (a) and (b),
M
is naturally a
C=k(Y)
-vector space. Since
T_{2,0}=X=0
on
M
, the last relation in Lemma
3.25
implies that
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{2,1}\\
T_{2,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{2}
kills
M
. In particular, we may choose some
0\neq v\in M
with
v\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{2,1}\\
T_{2,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=0
. We may assume that
ve_{\chi[t,0]}=v
for either
t=0
or
t=1
. By Lemma
3.25
, the
C
-subvector space

(7)

C[Z^{\pm 1}]v+C[Z^{\pm 1}]v\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{0,1}\\
T_{0,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\subset M.

is stable under the action of
R
and must therefore be equal to
M
. In particular,
M
is finitely generated as a module over
C[Z^{\pm 1}]
and so the
C[Z^{\pm 1}]
-module structure on
M
cannot extend to
C(Z)
, which excludes (a). In case (b), replacing
C
by
k[Y]
in (
7
) yields an
R
-stable subspace and we deduce that
M
is finitely generated over
k[Y]
, which is impossible since
M
is a
C
-vector space by assumption.
∎

Fix now a central character
\xi\colon k[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY)\to k
corresponding to the
k
-rational point
(x,y,z)
and put

\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}=\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])\otimes_{Z(\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])),\xi}k.

Proposition 3.27
.

Assume that
x=0=y
. Up to isomorphism, there exist precisely two simple right
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
-modules, namely the
1
-dimensional
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
-algebras

\operatorname{pr}_{t}\colon\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{H}(\chi[t,0])/(T_{2,0},T_{0,2},T_{2,2}-z)\cong k,\hskip 2.84544pt\text{ for }t=0,1,

induced by projecting to the diagonal entry in
\mathcal{H}(\chi[t,0])
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
3.25
and the assumption on
x
and
y
that the projection maps
\operatorname{pr}_{t}
are indeed
k
-algebra homomorphisms. Since the respective target is
1
-dimensional over
k
, they define simple
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
-modules. Moreover, these are pairwise non-isomorphic as the kernels are distinct two-sided maximal ideals.

For showing that these are the only simple right modules, we make use of propositions
3.21
and
3.22
to identify

(8)

\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}\cong\begin{pmatrix}k&k\oplus k\\
k\oplus k&k\end{pmatrix}

with multiplication
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a^{\prime}&b^{\prime}\\
c^{\prime}&d^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}aa^{\prime}&ab^{\prime}+d^{\prime}b\\
a^{\prime}c+dc^{\prime}&dd^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}
, where
k
acts on
k\oplus k
via the usual componentwise scalar multiplication. Let now
I\subset\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
be a right ideal neither contained in
\ker(\operatorname{pr}_{0})
nor in
\ker(\operatorname{pr}_{1})
. We claim that
I
is the whole ring. Indeed, by assumption
I
contains elements
E_{0},E_{1}
, where the
(t+1)
-th entry on the diagonal in
E_{t}
is non-zero. By scaling we may assume that this non-zero entry is equal to
1
. Since
I
is a right ideal, we may further assume that
E_{t}
contains only one non-zero column for
t=0,1
. Hence,
E_{0}+E_{1}
is of the form
\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
c&1\end{pmatrix}
for some
b,c\in k\oplus k
. Since
\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
c&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-b\\
-c&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, we are done.
∎

Proposition 3.28
.

Assume that
x\neq 0
or
y\neq 0
. Up to isomorphism, there exists a unique simple right
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
-module, namely the
2
-dimensional space
kv\oplus kw
uniquely determined by
ve_{\chi}=v
and
we_{\chi[1,0]}=w
.

Proof.

Consider the idempotent
e_{\chi}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
in
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
. Since
x
or
y
is non-zero, Lemma
3.25
implies that
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}=\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
, i.e.
e_{\chi}
is a full idempotent. Hence,
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
is Morita equivalent to the field

e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}e_{\chi}=\mathcal{H}(\chi)\otimes_{k[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY),\xi}k\cong k

and therefore admits unique simple right module. The same works with
e_{\chi}
replaced by
e_{\chi[1,0]}
. Hence, if
M
denotes the unique simple right
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
-module, we have
M=Me_{\chi}\oplus Me_{\chi[1,0]}
with each summand being
1
-dimensional.
∎

Under the isomorphism
Z(\mathcal{H}(O))\cong Z(\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0]))\cong k[X,Y,Z^{\pm 1}]/(XY)
resulting from the Morita equivalence Corollary
3.19
, the elements
X,Y,Z
correspond to
T_{(s\Pi)^{2}},T_{(\Pi s)^{2}},T_{\Pi^{4}}
, respectively.

Definition 3.29
.

(i)

We say that a simple right
\mathcal{H}(O)
-module is
supersingular
if it is killed by the central elements
T_{(s\Pi)^{2}}
and
T_{(\Pi s)^{2}}
. Otherwise, we say that the module is
principal
.

(ii)

For
\chi\in O
and
z\in k^{\times}
, denote by
\operatorname{SS}(\chi,z)
the unique supersingular
\mathcal{H}(O)
-module with
\operatorname{SS}(\chi,z)e_{\chi}\neq 0
and the central element
T_{\Pi^{4}}
acting by the scalar
z
.

(iii)

For
\chi\in O
and
(x,y,z)\in k^{3}
with
xy=0\neq z
and
x\neq 0
or
y\neq 0
, denote by
\operatorname{PS}(\chi,x,y,z)
the unique principal
\mathcal{H}(O)
-module with
\operatorname{PS}(\chi,x,y,z)e_{\chi}\neq 0
such that
(T_{(s\Pi)^{2}},T_{(\Pi s)^{2}},T_{\Pi^{4}})
act on
\operatorname{PS}(\chi,x,y,z)e_{\chi}
via the scalars
(x,y,z)
.

Remark 3.30
.

The only intertwinings between the simple right
\mathcal{H}(O)
-modules are
\operatorname{SS}(\chi,z)\cong\operatorname{SS}(\chi^{s}[1,0],z)
and

\operatorname{PS}(\chi[1,0],x,y,z)\cong\operatorname{PS}(\chi,x,y,z)\cong\operatorname{PS}(\chi^{s}[1,0],y,x,z)

for
\chi\in O
. For the last isomorphism note that
T_{\Pi}(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{2}T_{\Pi}^{-1}=(T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{2}
and
T_{\Pi}(T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{2}T_{\Pi}^{-1}=(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{2}
, which follows from Lemma
3.9
(ii), (iv).

3.4.
The Hecke algebra of a non-regular orbit

In this section, we fix a non-square-regular orbit
O
and a character
\chi\in O
. Possibly replacing
\chi
by
\chi^{s}[0,1]
, we may assume that
\chi=\chi^{s}
. Twisting appropriately as in Lemma
3.17
, we may assume that
\chi=\mathbf{1}
is the trivial character. By Lemma
3.13
and Remark
3.12
, the orbit
O
of agrees with the
\Pi
-orbit of
\mathbf{1}
and thus, by Corollary
3.10
, we have an algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{H}(O)\cong M_{4\times 4}(\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1}))
and so:

Corollary 3.31
.

The functor

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{H}(O)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\mapsto Me_{\mathbf{1}}

is an equivalence of categories.

Viewing
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
as the
k
-algebra of genuine bi-
I
-invariant functions
\widetilde{G}\to k
endowed with the convolution product, Lemma
3.18
yields:

Corollary 3.32
.

The algebra
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
has
k
-basis
\{T_{i,j},S_{i,j}\}_{i,j\in 2\mathbf{Z}}
, where
T_{i,j}
and
S_{i,j}
are uniquely determined by

\operatorname{supp}(T_{i,j})=\widetilde{I}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p^{j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\widetilde{I},\hskip 5.69046ptT_{i,j}\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p^{j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\right)=1

and

\operatorname{supp}(S_{i,j})=\widetilde{I}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{i}\\
p^{j}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\widetilde{I},\hskip 5.69046ptS_{i,j}\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{i}\\
p^{j}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\right)=1,

respectively.

Proposition 3.33
.

The
k
-algebra
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
is generated by
T_{2,2},T_{-2,-2},S_{0,0},S_{0,2}
, and the following is a full set of relations:
T_{2,2}
is central and invertible with inverse
T_{-2,-2}
,
S_{0,0}^{2}=-S_{0,0}
and
S_{0,2}^{2}=0
.

Proof.

Writing
T_{2,2}=T_{\Pi^{4}}e_{\mathbf{1}}
,
T_{-2,-2}=T_{\Pi^{-4}}e_{\mathbf{1}}
,
S_{0,0}=T_{s}e_{\mathbf{1}}
and
S_{0,2}=T_{\Pi s\Pi}e_{\mathbf{1}}
, the relations follow from Lemma
3.9
, and Lemma
3.7
(v) to prove
S_{0,2}^{2}=0
. Furthermore, for all
m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

(a)

(S_{0,0}S_{0,2})^{m}=T_{2m,0}
,
(S_{0,2}S_{0,0})^{m}=T_{0,2m}
;

(b)

T_{2m,0}S_{0,0}=S_{2m,0}
,
S_{0,2}T_{2m,0}=S_{0,2(m+1)}
;

(c)

T_{2,2}T_{i,j}=T_{i+2,j+2}
,
T_{2,2}S_{i,j}=S_{i+2,j+2}
for all
i,j\in 2\mathbf{Z}
.

Indeed, (a) follows from (vi) and (vii), (b) follows from (viii) and (ix), while (c) follows from (iii) of Lemma
3.9
.
Corollary
3.32
now finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.34
.

The center of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
is a localized polynomial ring in two variables, namely the
k
-algebra morphism

\displaystyle k[\mathfrak{Z},Z^{\pm 1}]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}Z(\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1}))

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Z}

\displaystyle\mapsto S_{0,0}S_{0,2}+S_{0,2}S_{0,0}+S_{0,2}

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{2,2}

is a well-defined isomorphism.

Proof.

The explicit description of the algebra
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
in the previous proposition implies that the center is equal to the set of elements, which are up to an integral power of
T_{2,2}
of the form

bT_{2,2}^{M}+\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}T_{2,2}^{N_{i}}(S_{0,0}S_{0,2})^{i}+\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}T_{2,2}^{N_{i}}(S_{0,2}S_{0,0})^{i}+\sum_{i\geq 0}a_{i+1}T_{2,2}^{N_{i+1}}(S_{0,2}S_{0,0})^{i}S_{0,2}

for
b,a_{i}\in k
,
M\in\mathbf{Z}
and
N_{i}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
An induction shows moreover that

\mathfrak{Z}^{n}=(S_{0,0}S_{0,2})^{n}+(S_{0,2}S_{0,0})^{n}+(S_{0,2}S_{0,0})^{n-1}S_{0,2}

for all
n\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 1}
, finishing the proof.
∎

3.4.1.
Simple modules

Lemma 3.35
.

Any simple right
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
-module is finite dimensional.

Proof.

Let
M
be a simple right
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
-module. If
S_{0,0}+1
kills this module, then
M
descends to a module over the commutative finite type
k
-algebra
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})/(S_{0,0}+1)
and we are done by Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz. Hence, we may assume that there exists some non-zero
v\in M(S_{0,0}+1)
. In particular,
vS_{0,0}=0
. Writing
Z(\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1}))=k[\mathfrak{Z},Z^{\pm 1}]
as in Corollary
3.34
, one can now argue as in the square-reguler case, cf. proof of Lemma
3.26
replacing
v\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{0,1}\\
T_{0,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
by
vS_{0,2}
.
∎

Proposition 3.36
.

The simple right
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
-modules are up to isomorphism as follows:

(SS)

For each
(\lambda,z)\in\{0,-1\}\times k^{\times}
, the character
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})\to k
,
T_{2,2}\mapsto z,S_{0,2}\mapsto 0,S_{0,0}\mapsto\lambda
.

(PS)

For each
(\lambda,z)\in k^{\times}\times k^{\times}
, the
2
-dimensional space
kv\oplus kw
with basis
v,w
and action determined by
vT_{2,2}=zv,\hskip 2.84544ptvS_{0,2}=w,\hskip 2.84544ptwS_{0,0}=\lambda v.

There are no isomorphisms between objects associated to different parameters.

Proof.

By the previous lemma, each simple right
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
-module
M
is finite dimensional. The same poof used by Vignéras in
[
30
, Theorem A.2]
now works here: If
\dim_{k}M>1
, then one shows that
S_{0,2}S_{0,0}
cannot be nilpotent on
M
, so one may choose an eigenvector
v\in M
with non-zero eigenvalue
\lambda\in k^{\times}
and one has
M=kv\oplus kvS_{0,2}
.
∎

Remark 3.37
.

The center
Z(\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1}))\cong k[\mathfrak{Z},Z^{\pm 1}]
, see Lemma
3.34
, acts on the module of type (SS) (resp. (PS)) corresponding to the parameter
(\lambda,z)
via the central character corresponding to the
k
-rational point
(0,z)
(resp.
(\lambda,z)
).

Using the Morita equivalence Corollary
3.31
, we identify the center of
\mathcal{H}(O)
with
Z(\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1}))\cong k[\mathfrak{Z},Z^{\pm 1}]
.

Definition 3.38
.

(i)

We say that a simple right
\mathcal{H}(O)
-module is
supersingular
if the center
k[\mathfrak{Z},Z^{\pm 1}]
acts on it via a
k
-rational point of the form
(0,z)
for
z\in k^{\times}
. Otherwise, we say that the module is
principal
.

(ii)

For
(\lambda,z)\in\{0,-1\}\times k^{\times}
, denote by
\operatorname{SS}(\lambda,z)
the unique supersingular
\mathcal{H}(O)
-module such that
\operatorname{SS}(\lambda,z)e_{\mathbf{1}}
is equal to the character on
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
corresponding to
(\lambda,z)
as in Proposition
3.36
.

(iii)

For
(\lambda,z)\in k^{\times}\times k^{\times}
, denote by
\operatorname{PS}(\lambda,z)
the unique right
\mathcal{H}(O)
-module such that
\operatorname{PS}(\lambda,z)e_{\mathbf{1}}
is equal to the
2
-dimensional
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
-module corresponding to
(\lambda,z)
as in Proposition
3.36
.

3.5.
Extensions of simple modules

Using
[
6
, §6]
as a guideline, we compute the extensions of the simple modules determined in the previous sections in the category of
\mathcal{H}
-modules on which
T_{\Pi^{4}}
acts via a fixed scalar. Applying Lemma
3.17
to an appropriate unramified character, we may and do assume that
T_{\Pi^{4}}
acts trivially. We put

\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}=\mathcal{H}/(T_{\Pi^{4}}-1)

and use a similar notation for the smaller algebras
\mathcal{H}(O)
,
\mathcal{H}(\chi)
, etc. There can only be non-trivial extensions of two simple modules if they descend to the Hecke algebra of the same orbit. We therefore fix an orbit
O
and we may and do without loss of generality assume that
O=O_{\mathbf{1}}
in the non-square-regular case. Define the element

\mathcal{H}\ni\mathscr{Z}_{O}=\begin{cases}(T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{2}+(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{2}\text{ if $O$ is square-regular}\\
\sum_{i=0}^{3}T_{\Pi}^{-i}\left((T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{2}+(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{2}+T_{\Pi}T_{s}T_{\Pi}\right)e_{\mathbf{1}}T_{\Pi}^{i}\text{ if $O=O_{\mathbf{1}}$.}\end{cases}

which, when projected to
\mathcal{H}(O)
, is central by Corollary
3.24
, resp.
3.34
.

We start with two lemmas, the first of which we record for later reference.

Lemma 3.39
.

We have equalities of right
\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
-modules:

(i)

If
\chi\neq\chi^{s}
, then
T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}=\ker\left(T_{s}\colon e_{\chi^{s}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\twoheadrightarrow e_{\chi}T_{s}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\right)
;

(ii)

T_{s}e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}=\ker\left(T_{s}+1\colon e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\twoheadrightarrow e_{\mathbf{1}}(T_{s}+1)\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\right)
;

(iii)

(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}=\ker\left(T_{s}\colon e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\twoheadrightarrow e_{\mathbf{1}}T_{s}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\right)
;

where the map
T_{s}
, resp.
T_{s}+1
, is given by acting from the left.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the equality after applying the idempotent cutting out
\mathcal{H}(O)_{p^{2}=1}
. We distinguish between the square-regular and non-square-regular case.

Assume that
O
is square-regular. By the Morita equivalence of Corollary
3.19
, it is enough to prove the statement after applying
e:=e_{\chi}+e_{\chi[1,0]}
from the right. Applying the invertible element
T_{\Pi}
from the left, the desired equality becomes equivalent to

T_{\Pi}T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}e=\ker(T_{s}T_{\Pi}^{-1}\colon T_{\Pi}e_{\chi^{s}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}e\to e_{\chi}T_{s}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}e\subset\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}).

Using Lemma
3.9
(v), (vi) and (vii), this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{0,1}\\
T_{0,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathcal{H}(\chi)_{p^{2}=1}&\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0],\chi)_{p^{2}=1}\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\ker\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{0,-1}\\
T_{0,-1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}&\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}\right)

\displaystyle=\ker\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{2,1}\\
T_{2,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}&\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}\right).

For the last equality we used that the central element
T_{2,2}
is invertible.
The result now follows from Proposition
3.21
, Proposition
3.22
and Lemma
3.25
.

Assume now that
O=O_{\mathbf{1}}
. By the Morita equivalence
3.31
, it is enough to prove the equalities after applying the idempotent
e_{\mathbf{1}}
. In this case, (ii) and (iii) translate into

S_{0,0}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}=\ker\left(S_{0,0}+1\colon\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\twoheadrightarrow(S_{0,0}+1)\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\right)

and

(S_{0,0}+1)\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}=\ker\left(S_{0,0}\colon\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\twoheadrightarrow S_{0,0}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\right).

For (i), note that by applying
T_{\Pi^{2}}
, we may replace
\chi
by
\chi[1,1]
and so assume that
\chi=\mathbf{1}[1,0]
. Thus,
e_{\chi}T_{\Pi}=T_{\Pi}e_{\mathbf{1}}
, see Lemma
3.9
(v). Acting by
T_{\Pi}
from the left, we find that (iii) is equivalent to

\displaystyle T_{\Pi}T_{s}T_{\Pi}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}

\displaystyle=\ker\left(T_{s}T_{\Pi}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\to e_{\mathbf{1}[1,0]}T_{s}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}e_{\mathbf{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\ker\left(T_{\Pi}^{-1}T_{s}T_{\Pi}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\twoheadrightarrow T_{\Pi}^{-1}T_{s}T_{\Pi}^{-1}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\right),

where in the last equality we used the injectivity of
T_{\Pi}^{-1}
. This just means that

\displaystyle S_{0,2}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}

\displaystyle=\ker\left(T_{-2,-2}S_{0,2}\colon\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\twoheadrightarrow S_{0,2}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\right)

\displaystyle=\ker\left(S_{0,2}\colon\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\twoheadrightarrow S_{0,2}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}\right)

The assertions now follow from the description of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
in Proposition
3.33
. ∎

By similar arguments, one proves the following lemma.

Lemma 3.40
.

For all
\lambda\in k
and all
\chi\in O
, the endomorphism

\mathscr{Z}_{O}-\lambda\colon e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\to e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}

is injective.

Definition 3.41
.

For
\lambda\in k
and a character
\chi\in O
, define the
\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
-modules
M(\chi,\lambda)
by the short exact sequence

0\to T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{Z}_{O}-\lambda}T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\to M(\chi,\lambda)\to 0.

For
O=O_{\mathbf{1}}
, we also define
M(\lambda)
by
