0\to(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{Z}_{O}-\lambda}(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\to M(\lambda)\to 0.

Remark 3.42
.

Using the relations
e_{\chi}T_{\Pi}^{2}=T_{\Pi}^{2}e_{\chi[1,1]}
,
T_{s}T_{\Pi}^{2}=(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}T_{\Pi}^{2}T_{s}
and the invertibility of
T_{\Pi}
(see Lemma
3.9
), we obtain an isomorphism
T_{\Pi}^{2}\colon T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\cong T_{s}e_{\chi[1,1]}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
of right
\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
-modules inducing an isomorphism on the quotients
M(\chi,\lambda)\cong M(\chi[1,1],\lambda)
.

Proposition 3.43
.

Assume that
O
is square-regular. Let
\chi\in O
and
\lambda\in k
.

(i)

If
\lambda\neq 0
, then
M(\chi,\lambda)\cong\operatorname{PS}(\chi,0,\lambda,1)
.

(ii)

If
\lambda=0
, then there is a non-split short exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{SS}(\chi[1,0],1)\to M(\chi,0)\to\operatorname{SS}(\chi,1)\to 0

of
\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
-modules.

Proof.

Put
e=e_{\chi}+e_{\chi[1,0]}
. The Morita equivalence
3.19
reduces to proving the statement after applying
e
. Acting by
T_{\Pi}
from the left defines an isomorphism between
M(\chi,\lambda)
and the cokernel of
\mathscr{Z}_{O}-\lambda
acting on
T_{\Pi}T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
, which we denote by
T_{\Pi}M(\chi,\lambda)
. It follows from propositions
3.22
and
3.21
that the images of the elements
T_{\Pi}T_{s}e_{\chi}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
T_{0,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
T_{\Pi}T_{s}e_{\chi}T_{\Pi}T_{s}=T_{(\Pi s)^{2}}e_{\chi[1,0]}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&T_{0,2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
form a basis of the cokernel of the map

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}T_{2,0}+T_{0,2}-\lambda&0\\
0&T_{2,0}+T_{0,2}-\lambda\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
T_{0,1}\mathcal{H}(\chi)_{p^{2}=1}&(T_{0,2})\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
T_{0,1}\mathcal{H}(\chi)_{p^{2}=1}&(T_{0,2})\end{smallmatrix}\right),

where
(T_{0,2})
is the ideal generated by
T_{0,2}
in
\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}
. Moreover, the central elements
(T_{2,0},T_{0,2},T_{2,2})
act on the cokernel via the scalars
(0,\lambda,1)
.

Part (i) follows from the uniqueness in Proposition
3.28
.

For (ii), note that the
1
-dimensional subspace of
T_{\Pi}M(\chi,0)e
spanned by the image of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&T_{0,2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])
-stable and must therefore be isomorphic to
\operatorname{SS}(\chi[1,0],0)e
. The cokernel of the resulting inclusion
\operatorname{SS}(\chi[1,0])e\hookrightarrow T_{\Pi}M(\chi,0)e
is
1
-dimensional and
e_{\chi}
acts trivially on it, so we obtain an exact sequence of the desired form, by Proposition
3.27
. It is non-split for if
\alpha,\beta\in k
such that
\alpha\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
T_{0,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\beta\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&T_{0,2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is fixed by
e_{\chi}
, then
\beta=0
, but the
1
-dimensional subspace spanned by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
T_{0,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is not
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])
-stable:
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
T_{0,1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&T_{0,1}\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&T_{0,2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, by Lemma
3.25
.
∎

Proposition 3.44
.

Assume that
O=O_{\mathbf{1}}
. Let
\chi\in O
and
\lambda\in k
.

(i)

If
\lambda\neq 0
, then
M(\chi,\lambda)\cong\operatorname{PS}(\lambda,1)\cong M(\lambda)
.

(ii)

If
\lambda=0
, then
M(\chi,0)=\operatorname{SS}(0,1)\oplus\operatorname{SS}(-1,1)
for
\chi\neq\chi^{s}
, and for
\chi=\chi^{s}
there are non-split exact sequences

0\to\operatorname{SS}(0,1)\to M(\chi,0)\to\operatorname{SS}(-1,1)\to 0

and

0\to\operatorname{SS}(-1,1)\to M(0)\to\operatorname{SS}(0,1)\to 0.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the statements after applying
e_{\mathbf{1}}
.
If
\chi\neq\chi^{s}
, then, arguing as in the proof of Lemma
3.39
,
M(\chi,\lambda)
is isomorphic to the cokernel of the endomorphism on
S_{0,2}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
defined by the central element
\mathfrak{Z}-\lambda\in k[\mathfrak{Z},Z^{\pm 1}]\cong Z(\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1}))
(see Corollary
3.34
). Using the explicit description of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
in Proposition
3.33
, one checks that a basis for this cokernel as a
k
-vector space is given by the images
v
and
w
of the two elements
S_{0,2}
and
S_{0,2}S_{0,0}
, respectively. By construction, the center
k[\mathfrak{Z},Z^{\pm 1}]
acts on it via the rational point
(\lambda,z)
, which implies (i) by the uniqueness in Proposition
3.36
, see also Remark
3.37
. If
\lambda=0
, then write the cokernel as

k(v+w)\oplus kw.

Since
wS_{0,0}=-w
,
wS_{0,2}=0
, i.e.
kw=\operatorname{SS}(-1,1)
, and
(v+w)S_{0,0}=0
,
(v+w)S_{0,2}=0
, i.e.
k(v+w)=\operatorname{SS}(0,1)
, this settles the case
\chi\neq\chi^{s}
.

If
\chi=\chi^{s}
, we may assume that
\chi=\mathbf{1}
by Remark
3.42
. In this case, a basis of
M(\chi,\lambda)
(resp.
M(\lambda)
) is given by the images
v
and
w
of
S_{0,0}
and
S_{0,0}S_{0,2}
(resp.
S_{0,0}+1
and
(S_{0,0}+1)S_{0,2}
). Again by uniqueness of the principal module with central character corresponding to the rational point
(\lambda,z)
, we deduce (i). Assume that
\lambda=0
, then for
M(\chi,\lambda)
,
vS_{0,0}=-v
,
vS_{0,2}=w
and
wS_{0,0}=0
,
wS_{0,2}=0
, so we obtain a non-split short exact sequence

0\to kw=\operatorname{SS}(0,1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\to M(\chi,\lambda)e_{\mathbf{1}}\to\operatorname{SS}(-1,1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\to 0.

For
M(0)
on the other hand, we have
vS_{0,0}=0
,
vS_{0,2}=w
and
wS_{0,0}=-w
,
wS_{0,2}=0
, so we obtain a non-split short exact sequence

0\to kw=\operatorname{SS}(-1,1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\to M(0)e_{\mathbf{1}}\to\operatorname{SS}(0,1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\to 0,

finishing the proof in case
\chi=\chi^{s}
.
∎

If a non-split extension between simple modules exists, then their central characters have to coincide. In particular, there are no non-split extensions neither of supersingular and principal modules nor of different principal modules.

3.5.1.
Extensions of supersingular modules

In order to compute the extensions of supersingular modules, we will distinguish between the square-regular case and the non-square-regular case.

Square-regular case.
Assume that
O
is square-regular and write

\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{H}(\chi)&\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0],\chi)\\
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\chi[1,0])&\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0])\end{pmatrix}

and
identify

\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}\cong\begin{pmatrix}k&k\oplus k\\
k\oplus k&\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}\end{pmatrix}

as in (
8
), where
\xi
is the central character corresponding to the singular rational point
(0,0,1)
, which we may view as a central character of
\mathcal{H}(O)
via the Morita equivalence
3.19
.

Definition 3.45
.

For
\lambda,\mu\in k
, define
E_{\lambda,\mu}
to be the unique
\mathcal{H}(O)
-module so that
E_{\lambda,\mu}(e_{\chi}+e_{\chi[1,0]})
is the
2
-dimensional
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
-module
kv\oplus kw
with

v\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&(b_{1},b_{2})\\
(c_{1},c_{2})&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=dv,\hskip 5.69046ptw\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&(b_{1},b_{2})\\
(c_{1},c_{2})&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=aw+(\lambda b_{1}+\mu b_{2})v

for all
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&(b_{1},b_{2})\\
(c_{1},c_{2})&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
.

Proposition 3.46
.

(i)

The map

\displaystyle k^{2}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}(O)}(\operatorname{SS}(\chi,1),\operatorname{SS}(\chi[1,0],1))

\displaystyle(\lambda,\mu)

\displaystyle\mapsto E_{\lambda,\mu}

is a well-defined
k
-linear isomorphism.

(ii)

The space
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}(O)_{p^{2}=1}}(\operatorname{SS}(\chi,1),\operatorname{SS}(\chi,1))=0
is trivial.

Proof.

(i) Since the two supersingular modules are non-isomorphic, any non-trivial extension admits a central character, so the Ext-space in question is equal to

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}(O)_{\xi}}(\operatorname{SS}(\chi,1),\operatorname{SS}(\chi[1,0],1))\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}}(\operatorname{SS}(\chi,1)e,\operatorname{SS}(\chi[1,0],1)e),

where
e=e_{\chi}+e_{\chi[1,0]}
and the isomorphism uses the Morita equivalence
3.19
. Write
E_{\lambda,\mu}e=kv\oplus kw
as in its definition. Let
\bar{w}
denote the image of
w
in
E_{\lambda,\mu}e/kv
, so that we have a short exact sequence

0\to kv\to E_{\lambda,\mu}e\to k\bar{w}\to 0

It is now clear from the definition of
E_{\lambda,\mu}
that the kernel (resp. cokernel) is just
\operatorname{SS}(\chi[1,0],1)e
(resp.
\operatorname{SS}(\chi,1)e
) as
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{\xi}
-modules, see also Proposition
3.27
.

One verifies that the assignment is
k
-linear. If the extension
E_{\lambda,\mu}e
splits, then there exist
\alpha,\beta\in k
such that
\alpha v+\beta w
is fixed by the idempotent
e_{\chi}
, which forces
\alpha=0
and so
\lambda,\mu=0
. This proves injectivity.

Conversely, let
0\to kv\to E\to k\bar{w}\to 0
be an extension of the two supersingular modules. Then the operator
e_{\chi}
on
E
is diagonalizable with distinct eigenvalues, so we may choose an eigenvector
w
with eigenvalue
1
. Since
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&(1,0)\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&(0,1)\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
act trivially on
k\bar{w}
, we can find
\lambda,\mu\in k
so that they act on
w
via
\lambda v
and
\mu v
, respectively. In other words,
E=E_{\lambda,\mu}
.

(ii) We need to show that there are no non-trivial self-extensions of
\operatorname{SS}(\chi,1)e
in the category of
\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}
-modules. Let
E
be such an extension. The element
(e_{\chi}-1)^{2}=1-e_{\chi}
kills
E
and so for each
f\in E
,

f\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=fe_{\chi}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=fe_{\chi}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)e_{\chi}=f\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\text{ for all $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{H}(\chi\oplus\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}$.}

In particular, elements of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\ast\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
or
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
have to kill
E
. By Lemma
3.25
, this implies that also the elements of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for
a\in(T_{2,0},T_{0,2})
kill
E
. Since
\mathcal{H}(\chi)_{p^{2}=1}=k\oplus(T_{2,0},T_{0,2})
by Proposition
3.21
, we are done.
∎

Remark 3.47
.

One may check that
M(\chi,0)\cong E_{1,0}
and
M(\chi^{s}[1,0],0)\cong E_{0,1}
.

Non-square-regular case.
Assume that
O=O_{\mathbf{1}}
.

Proposition 3.48
.

Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be supersingular
\mathcal{H}(O)_{p^{2}=1}
-modules. Then

\dim_{k}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}(O)_{p^{2}=1}}(M_{1},M_{2})=1.

Proof.

It is enough to prove this in the category of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
-modules after applying
e_{\mathbf{1}}
. Write
M_{2}=\operatorname{SS}(\lambda,1)
for
\lambda\in\{0,-1\}
. Define an extension

0\to M_{1}e_{\mathbf{1}}\to E\to M_{2}e_{\mathbf{1}}\to 0

by putting
E=kv\oplus kpw
with
kv=M_{1}e_{\mathbf{1}}
and
wS_{0,0}=\lambda w
and
wS_{0,2}=v
. This is a well-defined extension which is non-split (as
S_{0,2}
acts non-trivially on
E
). Conversely, given any other such extension, say
F
, we may diagonalize the action of
S_{0,0}
since
S_{0,0}(S_{0,0}+1)=0
. Choose a basis of eigenvectors
v,w
with
v\in M_{1}e_{\mathbf{1}}
. Then necessarily
wS_{0,0}=\lambda w
. Since
S_{0,2}
acts trivially on
M_{2}e_{\mathbf{1}}
, there exists some
\mu\in k
with
wS_{0,2}=\mu v
. In conclusion, the extension
F
is equal to the
\mu
-scalar multiple of the extension
E
.
∎

3.5.2.
Extensions of principal modules

In the square-regular case, we may write a principal module as
M(\chi,\lambda)
for a suitable
0\neq\lambda\in k
and some
\chi\in O
, see Proposition
3.43
and the intertwinings in Remark
3.30
. In case
O=O_{\mathbf{1}}
, we may write each principal module of
\mathcal{H}(O)_{p^{2}=1}
as
M(\lambda)
for a suitable
\lambda\in k^{\times}
, see Proposition
3.44
. The following assertion can now either be proved as in
[
6
, Corollary 6.6]
, resp.
[
6
, Corollary 6.7]
, or is also a consequence of our later Ext-computations of principal series representations, see Remark
4.37
.

Proposition 3.49
.

Let
\lambda\in k^{\times}
.

(i)

If
O=O_{\chi}
is square-regular, then the space
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}(O)_{p^{2}=1}}(M(\chi,\lambda),M(\chi,\lambda))
is
1
-dimensional with basis

0\to M(\chi,\lambda)\xrightarrow{\mathscr{Z}_{O}-\lambda}T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}/(\mathscr{Z}_{O}-\lambda)^{2}\to M(\chi,\lambda)\to 0.

(ii)

If
O=O_{\mathbf{1}}
, then the space
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}(O)_{p^{2}=1}}(M(\lambda),M(\lambda))
is
1
-dimensional with basis given by the extension

0\to M(\lambda)\xrightarrow{\mathscr{Z}_{O}-\lambda}(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}/(\mathscr{Z}_{O}-\lambda)^{2}\to M(\lambda)\to 0.

4.
Genuine representation theory

In this part of the paper, we explain a complete classification result for the smooth irreducible genuine
k
-representations of
\widetilde{G}
as introduced in Section
2.3
. We do this by working out the strategy outlined in the introduction. For
\pi\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(k)
, the
I_{1}
-invariants

(9)

\pi^{I_{1}}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{I_{1}}(\mathbf{1},\pi|_{I_{1}})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota,\pi|_{\widetilde{I}_{1}})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota),\pi)

carry a natural right action of the pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}
. We show that the adjoint pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I})
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\colon\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(k)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{I}(\pi)=\pi^{I_{1}},

and

\displaystyle\hskip 52.63777pt\mathcal{T}\colon\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(k)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{T}(M)=M\otimes_{\mathcal{H}}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota),

induces a bijection

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Simple right}\\
\text{$\mathcal{H}$-modules}\end{array}\right\}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Smooth irreducible genuine}\\
\text{$k$-representations of $\widetilde{G}$}\end{array}\right\},

where both sides are considered up to isomorphism. The results of the previous sections provide a classification of the left-hand side, and in particular show that there are only two kinds of simple modules: principal and supersingular modules. We will prove the desired bijection by considering the two kinds of modules separately. Before we start, let us extend the list of notations given by sections
1.2
and
2.3
.

4.1.
Preliminaries

We define subgroups of
B
and
T
by

\displaystyle B_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}A&\mathbf{Q}_{p}\\
0&A\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\supset T_{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle U=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbf{Q}_{p}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle B_{S}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbf{Q}_{p}\\
0&S\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\supset T_{S}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&S\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle P=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbf{Q}_{p}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\supset P^{+}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Z}_{p}\setminus\{0\}&\mathbf{Z}_{p}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\supset\hskip 2.84544pt

\displaystyle\Gamma=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

We identify
k\llbracket U\cap I\rrbracket=k\llbracket X\rrbracket
, the power series ring in the variable
X=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)-1
. Following
[
8
]
, we let
F=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
act on
U\cap I
via conjugation:
F\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)F^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&pb\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for all
b\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, inducing the flat
k
-algebra endomorphism on
k\llbracket X\rrbracket
given by
X\mapsto X^{p}
, and we let
k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F]
be the non-commutative polynomial ring in
F
:
Ff(X)=F(f(X))F=f(X^{p})F
for all
f(X)\in k\llbracket X\rrbracket
. We also define
k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F,\Gamma]
to be the twisted group ring so that the elements of
\Gamma
commute with
F
and
\gamma X=((X+1)^{\gamma}-1)\gamma
for
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

If
M
is a
k\llbracket X\rrbracket
-module, we denote the subspace of
X
-torsion elements (i.e. those killed by
X
) in
M
by
M[X]
.

The category of smooth
P^{+}
-representations is naturally a full subcategory of the category of left
k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F,\Gamma]
-modules.

By Lemma
2.4
, we have
\widetilde{B}_{S}=B_{S}\times\mu_{2}\supset\widetilde{P}=P\times\mu_{2}
and
\widetilde{B}_{2}=B_{2}\times\mu_{2}
as groups, which we will from now on use without further mention. In particular, we will simply view
B_{S},P
and
B_{2}
as subgroups of the corresponding covering.

Without further comment, we will make use of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\pi\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(k)
and
n\in N_{G}(T)
. Under the isomorphism (
9
), the action of the Hecke operator
T_{n}
on
\pi^{I_{1}}
is given by

vT_{n}=\sum_{x\in I_{1}/(I_{1}\cap(n^{-1}I_{1}n))}x^{-1}(\tilde{n})^{-1}v,\text{ for all $v\in\pi^{I_{1}}$.}

Proof.

This follows from the definition, see also
[
21
, Corollary 2.6]
: Letting
\varphi\in\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)
be the unique function with support
\widetilde{I}_{1}
and value
1
at the identity, the isomorphism
\pi^{I_{1}}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota),\pi)
is given by
f(\varphi)\mapsfrom f
. Since
\widetilde{I}_{1}\tilde{n}I_{1}=\sqcup_{x\in I_{1}/(I_{1}\cap n^{-1}I_{1}n)}\widetilde{I}_{1}\tilde{n}x
, we have
T_{n}(\varphi)=\sum_{x\in I_{1}/(I_{1}\cap n^{-1}I_{1}n)}x^{-1}(\tilde{n})^{-1}\varphi
. Applying the
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant map
f
gives the assertion.
∎

As in
[
21
, Corollary 2.0.11]
, Lemma
3.7
has the following consequence.

Corollary 4.2
.

Given
\pi\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(k)
, the
I_{1}
-invariants
\pi^{I_{1}}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{I},\iota}(k)
decomposes as

\pi^{I_{1}}=\bigoplus_{\chi\in\widehat{H}}\pi^{I_{1}}e_{\chi},

and
\widetilde{I}
acts on
\pi^{I_{1}}e_{\chi}
via the character
\chi\boxtimes\iota
.

In the study of the Hecke algebra of a non-square-regular orbit in Section
3.4
, we used the
k
-algebra automorphism
\operatorname{tw}\colon\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}
of Lemma
3.17
induced by twisting. We will also do this on the representation theoretic side and we now check that this is harmless.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\psi\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to k^{\times}
be a smooth character. Let
\operatorname{tw}_{\psi}\colon\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}
be the automorphism induced by twisting by
\psi\circ{\det}
. For each
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{H}}
, there is a
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant isomorphism

\mathcal{T}(M)\otimes\psi\circ{\det}\cong\mathcal{T}(\operatorname{tw}_{\psi}^{\ast}(M)),

which is natural in
M
.

Proof.

Recall that
\operatorname{tw}_{\psi}
is given by
h\mapsto j(h\otimes\psi\circ\det)j^{-1}
for
h\in\mathcal{H}
, where
j\colon\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\otimes\psi\circ\det\cong\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)
is the canonical isomorphism.

Using the adjoint pairs
((-)\otimes\psi\circ\det,(-)\otimes\psi^{-1}\circ\det)
,
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I})
and
(\operatorname{tw}_{\psi}^{\ast},\operatorname{tw}_{\psi,\ast})
, it suffices, by Yoneda’s Lemma, to show that for each genuine
\widetilde{G}
-representation
\pi
,

\mathcal{I}(\pi\otimes\psi^{-1}\circ\det)\cong\operatorname{tw}_{\psi,\ast}\mathcal{I}(\pi)

as
\mathcal{H}
-modules, natural in
\pi
. The left-hand side is equal to

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota),\pi\otimes\psi^{-1}\circ\det)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\otimes\psi\circ\det,\pi)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota),\pi)

where the
\mathcal{H}
-action in the second line is given by precomposition with
h\otimes(\psi\circ\det)
for
h\in\mathcal{H}
and the isomorphism is induced by
j
, so that the induced
\mathcal{H}
-action in the last line is given by precomposition with
j(h\otimes\psi\circ\det)j^{-1}
for
h\in\mathcal{H}
, which proves the lemma.
∎

Speaking of twisting, the elements of
\widetilde{Z}
define interesting
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant isomorphisms. Besides the already introduced smooth character
\omega\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow[\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}]\cong\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}\subset k^{\times}
, we denote the unramified character mapping
p
to a fixed element
y\in k^{\times}
by
\mu_{y}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For all
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&x\end{smallmatrix}\right),\xi\right)\in\widetilde{Z}
and
(g,\zeta)\in\widetilde{G}
,

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&x\end{smallmatrix}\right),\xi\right)(g,\zeta)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&x\end{smallmatrix}\right),\xi\right)^{-1}=\left(g,\zeta\left(\mu_{-1}^{v(x)}\omega^{v(x)}\mu_{\omega(x)}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}(\det(g))\right).

In particular, for
\pi\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(k)
, the map

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&x\end{smallmatrix}\right),\xi\right)\colon\pi\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi\otimes\left(\mu_{-1}^{v(x)}\omega^{v(x)}\mu_{\omega(x)}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\circ{\det}

is a
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant isomorphism.

Proof.

This is a straight forward computation using the definition of the cocycle (
3
) defining
\widetilde{G}
.
∎

We finish the section with a quick observation.

Lemma 4.5
.

There are no finite dimensional objects in
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(k)
.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be a finite dimensional and smooth
\widetilde{G}
-representation, which we may assume to be irreducible. Its kernel is an open subgroup of
\widetilde{G}
. In particular, it contains a congruence subgroup
K_{m}
for some
m\geq 1
. Projecting to
G
and conjugating by
F^{n}
(resp.
(sFs)^{n}
) for all
n\geq 0
, shows that the image of the kernel in
G
contains
U
as well as
sUs
and thus
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
. We conclude that each element of
\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})}
acts on
\pi
by
\pm 1
(hence by scalars). It follows that the restriction of
\pi
to the subgroup
P\cap T\cong\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
is still irreducible and must therefore be
1
-dimensional. However, the commutator subgroup of
\widetilde{G}
contains
[\widetilde{T},\widetilde{T}]=\mu_{2}
(cf. Lemma
2.6
(ii)), contradicting genuinity.
∎

4.2.
Weights

We remind the reader of the well-known classification of smooth irreducible
k
-representations of
K
, so-called
weights
.

Definition 4.6
.

For
r\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
, define
\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(k^{2})
to be the space of homogeneous degree-
r
polynomials in
k[x,y]
with
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{F}_{p})
-action defined by

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)P(x,y)=P(ax+cy,bx+dy),\text{ for all $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{F}_{p})$, $P(x,y)\in\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(k^{2})$.}

We will view it as representation of
K
via inflation along the reduction map.

Proposition 4.7
.

The map

\displaystyle\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbf{Z}^{2}:\begin{array}[]{l}0\leq a-b\leq p-1\\
0\leq b<p-1\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\to\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Smooth irreducible}\\
\text{$k$-representations of $K$}\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{Sym}^{a-b}(k^{2})\otimes{\det}^{b}

is well-defined and bijective, where the right-hand side is considered up to isomorphism.

Proof.

See for example
[
15
, Proposition 14]
.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Given
(a,b)\in\mathbf{Z}^{2}
with
0\leq a-b\leq p-1
and
0\leq b<p-1
, there is a short exact sequence of
K
-representations,

0\to\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1-(a-b)}(k^{2})\otimes{\det}^{a}\to\operatorname{Ind}^{K}_{I}(\omega^{a}\otimes\omega^{b})\to\operatorname{Sym}^{a-b}(k^{2})\otimes{\det}^{b}\to 0.

Proof.

This follows from Frobenius reciprocity and counting dimensions, see for example
[
15
, p. 23]
.
∎

Definition 4.9
.

Given a character
\psi\in\widehat{H}
, define the semi-simple
K
-representation
W(\psi)
by

W(\psi)=\bigoplus_{\sigma:\sigma^{I_{1}}=\psi}\sigma,

where the direct sum is over all smooth irreducible
K
-representations
\sigma
with
\sigma^{I_{1}}=\psi
.

Remark 4.10
.

If
\psi\neq\psi^{s}
, then
W(\psi)
is irreducible. Otherwise,
W(\psi)
consists of the two constituents of
\operatorname{Ind}^{K}_{I}(\psi)
.

Definition 4.11
.

The maximal semi-simple subrepresentation of a smooth
K
-representation
\pi
will be denoted by
\operatorname{soc}_{K}(\pi)
and is called the
K
-socle of
\pi
.

4.3.
Principal series representations

Definition 4.12
.

A
genuine principal series representation
of
\widetilde{G}
is a representation of
\widetilde{G}
isomorphic to the smooth parabolic induction
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}}(\tau)
of some smooth irreducible genuine
k
-representation
\tau
of
\widetilde{T}
viewed as a representation of
\widetilde{B}
via inflation.

In order to understand these objects, an investigation of the
\widetilde{T}
-representations we are inducing is necessary, which is the content of the next section.

4.3.1.
Smooth irreducible genuine
\widetilde{T}
-representations

The classification result we are about to prove closely follows
[
18
, Chapter 5]
, which deals with smooth genuine complex representations in a more general setting, see especially Corollary 5.2 in
loc. cit.
It says that a smooth irreducible genuine
\widetilde{T}
-representation is uniquely determined by its central character. Before stating the result, we need two lemmas.

Lemma 4.13
.

Any smooth irreducible genuine
k
-representation of
\widetilde{T}
is finite dimensional.

Proof.

The proof is inspired by
[
17
, Lemma 2.4]
. By Lemma
2.6
,
Z(\widetilde{T})=T^{\operatorname{sq}}\times\mu_{2}
. Filtering the inclusion
Z(\widetilde{T})\subset\widetilde{T}
by subgroups of successive index two, and observing that, if
G_{1}\subset G_{2}
is an inclusion of groups of index two, then the restriction of an irreducible
G_{2}
-representation to
G_{1}
is semi-simple of length at most two, the lemma reduces to the statement that a smooth irreducible genuine
Z(\widetilde{T})
-representation, or equivalently a smooth irreducible
T^{\operatorname{sq}}
-representation, is finite dimensional. Modulo the pro-
p
part,
T^{\operatorname{sq}}
is a finitely generated abelian group, so Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz implies that a smooth irreducible
k
-representation of it is in fact
1
-dimensional.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
i,j\in\mathbf{Z}
. For all
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in B_{2}
,

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p^{j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p^{j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)^{-1}=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&p^{i-j}b\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right),\omega(d)^{i\frac{p-1}{2}}\omega(a)^{j\frac{p-1}{2}}\right).

Proof.

By Example
2.3
,
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p^{j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)^{-1}=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-i}&0\\
0&p^{-j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),(-1)^{ij\frac{p-1}{2}}\right)
. The lemma is now a simple computation based on the definition of the cocycle (
3
) defining
\widetilde{G}
.
∎

Notation 4.15
.

For
i,j\in\mathbf{Z}
and a smooth representation
\pi
of a subgroup of
\widetilde{B}
, denote by

\pi[j,i]=\pi\otimes(\omega^{j\frac{p-1}{2}}\otimes\omega^{i\frac{p-1}{2}})

the twist of
\pi
by the character
\omega^{j\frac{p-1}{2}}\otimes\omega^{i\frac{p-1}{2}}\colon\widetilde{B}\twoheadrightarrow B\twoheadrightarrow T\to\mu_{2}\subset k^{\times}
restricted to the subgroup of consideration.

Definition-Proposition 4.16
.

The map

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Smooth characters}\\
T^{\operatorname{sq}}\to k^{\times}\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Smooth irreducible genuine}\\
\text{$k$-representations of $\widetilde{T}$}\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\chi\colon T^{\operatorname{sq}}\to k^{\times}

\displaystyle\mapsto\tau_{\chi}:=\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{T}}_{\widetilde{T}_{2}}\left(\chi^{\prime}\boxtimes\iota\right)

is a well-defined bijection, where
\chi^{\prime}
is any extension of
\chi
to
T_{2}
and the right-hand side is considered up to isomorphism.

Proof.

Note that, since
k
is divisible and so injective in the category of abelian groups, any (smooth) character of
T^{\operatorname{sq}}
extends to a (smooth) character of
T_{2}
. Let now
\chi
be a smooth character on
T_{2}
. By Lemma
4.14
we have

(10)

\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{T}}_{\widetilde{T}_{2}}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)|_{T_{2}}=\bigoplus_{i,j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}}\chi[j,i],

which is the direct sum over all distinct characters extending
\chi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
. This proves irreducibility as well as the independence of the chosen extension.

Conversely, by Lemma
4.13
, any smooth irreducible genuine
k
-representation
\tau
of
\widetilde{T}
is finite dimensional and thus admits a central character, i.e. there exists a smooth character
\chi\colon T^{2}\to k^{\times}
with
\chi\subset\tau|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
. Using Frobenius reciprocity and that
\operatorname{Ind}^{T_{2}}_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}(\chi)
is the direct sum of the characters extending
\chi
(since
T_{2}/T^{\operatorname{sq}}
is finite of order prime to
p
), we deduce that there exists a character
\chi^{\prime}
of
T_{2}
extending
\chi
with
\chi^{\prime}\subset\tau|_{T_{2}}
. Using Frobenius reciprocity once again, we obtain a non-trivial
\widetilde{T}
-equivariant morphism
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{T}}_{\widetilde{T}_{2}}(\chi^{\prime}\boxtimes\iota)\to\tau
, which has to be an isomorphism as both sides are irreducible.
∎

Remark 4.17
.

(i)

In the previous proposition, the subgroup
\widetilde{T}_{2}
can be replaced by any other maximal abelian subgroup of
\widetilde{T}
, for example by
\widetilde{T}_{S}
.

(ii)

A genuine principal series of
\widetilde{G}
is of the form
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)
for a smooth character
\chi\colon T_{2}\to k^{\times}
viewed as a character of
B_{2}
via inflation.

Definition 4.18
.

For a smooth character
\chi\colon T_{2}\to k^{\times}
, define the
standard basis

\{f_{i,j}\}_{i,j=0,1}
of
\tau_{\chi}
by letting
f_{i,j}
be the unique function with

\operatorname{supp}(f_{i,j})=\widetilde{T}_{2}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p^{j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\text{ and }f_{i,j}\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p^{j}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\right)=1.

Lemma 4.19
.

For all
i,j=0,1
, the following hold true.

(i)

f_{i,j}
is an
H
-eigenvector with eigencharacter
\chi|_{H}[j,i]
;

(ii)

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)f_{i,0}=(-1)^{i\frac{p-1}{2}}f_{i,1}
;

(iii)

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)f_{i,1}=(-1)^{i\frac{p-1}{2}}\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)f_{i,0}
;

(iv)

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)f_{0,j}=f_{1,j}
;

(v)

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)f_{1,j}=\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)f_{0,j}
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from Lemma
4.14
. Part (ii) follows from the equation
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&p\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{i}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),(-1)^{i\frac{p-1}{2}}\right)
, see Example
2.3
. Part (iii) follows from (ii) since the element
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is central and
\tau_{\chi}
has central character
\chi
. The remaining two parts are proved analogously.
∎

4.3.2.
Irreducibility of principal series representations

Let us fix a smooth character
\chi\colon T_{2}\to k^{\times}
and denote by

\tilde{\pi}(\chi)=\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}}(\tau_{\chi})

the corresponding principal series. The goal of this section it to prove:

Proposition 4.20
.

The genuine principal series
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)
is irreducible as a representation of
\widetilde{G}
. Moreover,
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)\cong\tilde{\pi}(\psi)
for another smooth character
\psi\colon T_{2}\to k^{\times}
if and only if
\chi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}=\psi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
.

The if-direction of the proposition trivially holds true as it does already for
\tau_{\chi}
. In particular, to prove irreducibility, we may replace
\chi
by one of its twists
\chi[i,j]
. In the non-square-regular case, we may and do therefore assume that
\chi|_{H}=\chi|_{H}^{s}
.

Definition 4.21
.

For
i,j=0,1
, define elements
F_{i,j}^{0},F_{i,j}^{1}\in\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}
uniquely determined by

\operatorname{supp}(F_{i,j}^{0})=\widetilde{B}I_{1}\text{ and }F_{i,j}^{0}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)=f_{i,j}

and

\operatorname{supp}(F_{i,j}^{1})=\widetilde{B}\tilde{s}I_{1}\text{ and }F_{i,j}^{1}(\tilde{s})=f_{i,j}.

The smooth character
\chi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
being trivial on the pro-
p
group
T\cap I_{1}
implies that these elements are well-defined. Since
\{f_{i,j}\}_{i,j=0,1}
is a
k
-basis of
\tau_{\chi}
, the decomposition
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{B}I_{1}\sqcup\widetilde{B}\tilde{s}I_{1}
immediately implies that
\{F_{i,j}^{0},F_{i,j}^{1}\}_{i,j=0,1}
is a
k
-basis of
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}
.

Lemma 4.22
.

For all
i,j=0,1
, the following hold true.

(i)

F_{i,j}^{0}e_{\chi|_{H}[j,i]}=F_{i,j}^{0}
and
F_{i,j}^{1}e_{\chi|_{H}[j,i]^{s}}=F_{i,j}^{1}
;

(ii)

F_{i,0}^{0}T_{\Pi}=(-1)^{i\frac{p-1}{2}}F_{i,1}^{1}
and
F_{i,1}^{0}T_{\Pi}=(-1)^{i\frac{p-1}{2}}\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)F_{i,0}^{1}
;

(iii)

F^{1}_{0,j}T_{\Pi}=F_{1,j}^{0}
and
F^{1}_{1,j}T_{\Pi}=\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)F_{0,j}^{0}
;

(iv)

F^{0}_{i,j}T_{s}=F_{i,j}^{1}
.

Proof.

Parts (i) to (iii) follow from Lemma
4.19
. For part (iv) recall that in the non-square-regular case we agreed to choose
\chi
so that
\chi|_{H}=\chi|_{H}^{s}
. Part (i) together with Lemma
3.7
(iv) implies that
F_{i,j}^{0}T_{s}
is a linear combination of
F_{i,j}^{1}
and
F_{j,i}^{0}
(in the square-regular case, even just a scalar multiple of
F_{i,j}^{1}
). It therefore suffices to evaluate at
\tilde{s}
and
1
. Now,
I_{1}sI_{1}=\sqcup_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}I_{1}s\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&[\lambda]\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and so

F_{i,j}^{0}T_{s}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&[\lambda]\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\tilde{s}F_{i,j}^{0}.

Since
\operatorname{supp}(F_{i,j}^{0})=\widetilde{B}I_{1}
, each summand evaluated at
1
gives
0
, while only the summand corresponding to
\lambda=0
contributes with value
1
if one evaluates at
\tilde{s}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.20
.

It is enough to prove that
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}
is a simple right
\mathcal{H}
-module. Indeed, this follows from
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}
generating
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)
as a
\widetilde{G}
-representation, which in turn is a consequence of the fact
2
2

2

E.g. see
[
30
, p. 24 (a)]
; a shorter proof: the Iwahori decomposition for the congruence subgroup
K_{m}
, for
m\geq 1
, implies that
BK_{m}=BI_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{m-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
that each compact open subgroup of
\widetilde{B}\setminus\widetilde{G}\cong B\setminus G
is a finite disjoint union of subsets of the form
B\setminus BI_{1}g
for suitable
g\in G
. In order to prove the irreducibility of
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}
as an
\mathcal{H}
-module, first note that
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}e_{O_{\chi|_{H}}}=\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}
, where
e_{O_{\chi|_{H}}}\in\mathcal{H}
is the idempotent cutting out
\mathcal{H}(O_{\chi|_{H}})
. We distinguish two cases.

If
\chi|_{H}
is square-regular, it suffices to prove that
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}(e_{\chi|_{H}}+e_{\chi|_{H}[1,0]})
is simple as a module over
\mathcal{H}(\chi|_{H}\oplus\chi|_{H}[1,0])
, see Corollary
3.19
. By Lemma
4.22
, we have

\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}(e_{\chi|_{H}}+e_{\chi|_{H}[1,0]})=kF_{0,0}^{0}+kF_{0,1}^{0},

and the Hecke operators
(T_{(s\Pi)^{2}},T_{(\Pi s)^{2}},T_{\Pi^{4}})
act on this space via the scalars
\left(\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right),0,\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\right)
.
The uniqueness in Proposition
3.28
now gives

(11)

\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}\cong\operatorname{PS}\left(\chi|_{H},\left(\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right),0,\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\right)\right).

Assume now that
\chi|_{H}=\chi|_{H}^{s}
. By twisting, we may even assume that
\chi|_{H}=\mathbf{1}
. By Lemma
4.22
, we have

\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}e_{\mathbf{1}}=kF_{0,0}^{0}+kF_{0,0}^{1}

and the center
k[\mathfrak{Z},Z^{\pm 1}]
of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
(Corollary
3.34
) acts via the character corresponding to the
k
-rational point
\left(\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right),\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\right)
. Thus, by Proposition
3.36
and the remark thereafter,

(12)

\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}=\operatorname{PS}\left(\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right),\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\right).

This concludes the proof of the irreducibility of
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)
.
Concerning the intertwinings: in the square-regular case, the isomorphism (
11
) together with Remark
3.30
implies that
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)\cong\tilde{\pi}(\psi)
for another smooth character
\psi
on
T_{2}
only if
\psi|_{H}=\chi|_{H}[i,j]
for some
i,j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
,
\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\psi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)
and
\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\psi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)
, i.e. only if
\chi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}=\psi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
.

In the non-square-regular case, an isomorphism
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}\cong\tilde{\pi}(\psi)^{I_{1}}
of
\mathcal{H}
-modules implies that
O_{\chi|_{H}}=O_{\psi|_{H}}
, i.e.
\psi|_{H}=\chi|_{H}[i,j]
for some
i,j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
. From the isomorphism (
12
) we can read off the values of
\chi
and
\psi
at
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, so in total
\chi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}=\psi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
.
∎

4.3.3.
From principal Hecke modules to principal series representations

The proof of the previous proposition shows that the assignment
\pi\mapsto\mathcal{I}(\pi)
defines a bijection between the isomorphism classes of principal
\mathcal{H}
-modules and the isomorphism classes of (irreducible) genuine principal series representations of
\widetilde{G}
. For showing that the inverse is given by the functor
\mathcal{T}
, we need to check that the canonical map

(13)

\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi)))\to\tilde{\pi}(\chi)

is an isomorphism. To ease notation, put
M=\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi))
. After an unramified twist, we may and do also assume that
\chi|_{H}=\mathbf{1}
in the non-square-regular case, cf. Lemma
4.3
. The map (
13
) is non-trivial (by adjunction), so the source must be non-zero. In particular, the unit
M\to\mathcal{I}\mathcal{T}(M)
is an injection, via which we will view
M=\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}
as a subspace of
\mathcal{T}(M)
.

Denote by

\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}=\left\{f\in\tilde{\pi}(\chi):\operatorname{supp}(f)\subset\widetilde{B}\tilde{s}\widetilde{B}\right\}

the
\widetilde{B}
-stable subspace consisting of those functions whose support is contained in the big cell
\widetilde{B}\tilde{s}\widetilde{B}
. We thus have an exact sequence of
\widetilde{B}
-representations

0\to\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\to\tilde{\pi}(\chi)\to\tau_{\chi}\to 0

induced by evaluation at the identity. In order to prove the canonical map (
13
) to be an isomorphism, we will construct an analogue of this sequence for the object
\mathcal{T}(M)
and show that the canonical map induces an isomorphism on the outer terms of the short exact sequences.

For a smooth
k
-representation
\tau
of
\widetilde{T}
, denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\tau)
the space of locally constant compactly supported
\tau
-valued functions on
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
. We endow it with a
\widetilde{B}
-action via the formula

(14)

\left[\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right),\zeta\right).L\right](x)=\tau\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d&0\\
0&a\end{smallmatrix}\right),\zeta(a,d^{-1})\right)\right)L\left(\frac{b+xd}{a}\right),

for all
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in B
,
\zeta\in\mu_{2}
and
L\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\tau)
.

If
\tau=\mathbf{1}
is the trivial representation of
T
viewed as a
\widetilde{T}
-representation via inflation, we simply write
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},k)
.

Lemma 4.23
.

We have the chain of
\widetilde{B}
-equivariant isomorphisms

\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\cong\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\tau_{\chi})\cong\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},k)\otimes\tau_{\chi^{s}},

where the first isomorphism is given by
f\mapsto\left[x\mapsto f\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&x\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\right]
and the second one is given by
[x\mapsto L(x)v]\mapsfrom L\otimes v
implicitly identifying
\tau_{\chi^{s}}=\tau_{\chi}^{\tilde{s}}
.

Proof.

The equality
\widetilde{B}\tilde{s}\widetilde{B}=\widetilde{B}\tilde{s}U
implies the first isomorphism. Here, note that the function
L
attached to
f
is locally constant since so is
f
; it has compact support since
f
is locally constant around the identity element of
\widetilde{G}
. The last isomorphism is clear. One checks that these are indeed
\widetilde{B}
-equivariant.
∎

Define
M_{0}=\bigoplus_{i,j=0,1}kF_{i,j}^{1}\subset\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\cap M
.

Corollary 4.24
.

The
\widetilde{B}
-representation
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}
is generated by
M_{0}
.

Proof.

Under the isomorphism
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\cong\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},k)\otimes\tau_{\chi^{s}}
of the previous lemma, the function
F_{i,j}^{1}
corresponds to the element
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\otimes f_{i,j}
, where
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}
is the indicator function of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. It suffices to show that
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},k)
is generated as a
B_{2}
-representation by this indicator function, which is evident from the definition of the action (
14
).
∎

Recall that
X=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)-1\in k\llbracket U\cap I\rrbracket\cong k\llbracket X\rrbracket
and
F=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Definition 4.25
.

Define
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}\subset\mathcal{T}(M)
to be the
\widetilde{B}
-subrepresentation generated by
M_{0}
.

We also let
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}=k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F]M_{0}\subset\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}
be the
P^{+}
-subrepresentation generated by
M_{0}
; this is
\widetilde{Z}
-stable.

We will prove that
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}=\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}[F^{-1}]
is irreducible as a
\widetilde{B}
-representation. In order to do this, we first show that
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}
is irreducible as a
P^{+}\widetilde{Z}
-representation, for which we determine its
X
-torsion.

Lemma 4.26
.

For all
i,j=0,1
, up to a non-zero scalar,

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}(X+1)^{[\lambda]}FF_{i,j}^{1}=F_{i,j+1}^{1}.

Proof.

On the subspace
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{I_{1}}
, the operator
\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}(X+1)^{[\lambda]}F
is simply the Hecke operator
T_{\Pi^{-1}s}=T_{\Pi^{-1}}T_{s}
, so the assertion follows from Lemma
4.22
.
∎

Corollary 4.27
.

For all
i,j=0,1
, we have the following equalities of
X
-torsion subspaces inside the space
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}
.

(i)

\left(k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F^{2}]F_{i,j}^{1}\right)[X]=kF_{i,j}^{1}
;

(ii)

\left(k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F]F_{i,j}^{1}\right)[X]=kF_{i,j}^{1}\oplus kF_{i,j+1}^{1}
;

(iii)

\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}[X]=M_{0}
.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
4.26
twice yields, up to a non-zero scalar,

(15)

F_{i,j}^{1}=\sum_{\lambda,\mu\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}(X+1)^{[\lambda]+p[\mu]}F^{2}F_{i,j}^{1}.

Iterating this
n
-times shows that
kF_{i,j}^{1}\subset\left(k\llbracket X\rrbracket F^{2n}F_{i,j}^{1}\right)[X]\subset k\llbracket X\rrbracket F^{2n}F_{i,j}^{1}
. The latter is a module over
k\llbracket X\rrbracket/F^{2n}XF^{-2n}=k\llbracket X\rrbracket/X^{p^{2n}}
generated by a single element and thus has
1
-dimensional
X
-torsion, so the first inclusion has to be an equality. Passing to the colimit over all
n\in\mathbf{N}
, which is filtered by (
15
), finishes the proof of part (i).

For part (ii), note that Lemma
4.26
implies that
k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F]F_{i,j}^{1}=k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F^{2}]F_{i,j}^{1}+k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F^{2}]F_{i,j+1}^{1}
. Applying part (i) now gives (ii).

Part (iii) can be deduced from (ii) just like (ii) itself was deduced from (i).
∎

Proposition 4.28
.

The
\widetilde{B}
-subrepresentation
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}\subset\mathcal{T}(M)
is irreducible.

Proof.

We first claim that
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}
is irreducible as a
P^{+}\widetilde{Z}
-representation. Indeed, by the previous corollary,
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}[X]=M_{0}
and
H
acts on the basis elements
\{F_{i,j}^{1}\}_{i,j=0,1}
via pairwise distinct characters, cf. Lemma
4.22
(i). Thus, by smoothness, any non-zero
P^{+}\widetilde{Z}
-subrepresentation of
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}
must contain some
F_{i,j}^{1}
. Using Lemma
4.26
and the action of
\tilde{\Pi}^{2}
, it must then contain
M_{0}
and must therefore be equal to
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}
. The proposition now follows from the claim by writing
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}=\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}[F^{-1}]=\varinjlim_{n\geq 1}F^{-n}\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 4.29
.

The
\widetilde{B}
-representation
\mathcal{T}(M)/\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}
is equal to the image of
M_{0}^{\perp}:=\bigoplus_{i,j=0,1}kF_{i,j}^{0}\subset M\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}(M)

under the projection map.

Proof.

Let
Q
denote the image of
M_{0}^{\perp}
in the quotient
\mathcal{T}(M)/\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}
. By definition,
\mathcal{T}(M)
is generated as a
\widetilde{G}
-representation by
M
. Writing
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{B}I_{1}\sqcup\widetilde{B}I_{1}\tilde{\Pi}
and noting that
\tilde{\Pi}M=MT_{\Pi}^{-1}=M
, it follows from the inclusion
M\subset\mathcal{T}(M)^{I_{1}}
(in fact,
I_{1}
-stability of
M
would be enough) that
\mathcal{T}(M)
is generated as a
\widetilde{B}
-representation by
M=M_{0}\oplus M_{0}^{\perp}
. As
M_{0}\subset\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}
, we deduce that
\mathcal{T}(M)/\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}
is generated as a
\widetilde{B}
-representation by
Q
. It therefore suffices to prove that
Q
is
\widetilde{B}
-stable.

It is certainly stable under the action of
B\cap I
and
\tilde{\Pi}^{2\mathbf{Z}}
, so it remains to show that
Q
is
F^{\pm 1}
-stable. The subspace
M_{0}^{\perp}
is stable under the Hecke operator
T_{s\Pi}=T_{s}T_{\Pi}
by Lemma
4.22
. The operator
T_{s\Pi}
is given by
\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p[\lambda]&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)F^{-1}
, and we claim that for all
m\in M_{0}^{\perp}
, the element

F^{-1}m-mT_{s}T_{\Pi}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p[\lambda]&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)F^{-1}m\in\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}

lies in
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}
. Now for all
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}
,
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p[\lambda]&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)F^{-1}\in Bs(I_{1}\cap U)=B\Pi(I_{1}\cap U)
. Since
M_{0}^{\perp}
is
(I_{1}\cap U)
-stable and
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}
is
\widetilde{B}
-stable, it is enough to observe that
\tilde{\Pi}M_{0}^{\perp}=M_{0}\subset\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}
, which follows from Lemma
4.22
.
∎

Corollary 4.30
.

The canonical map
\mathcal{T}(M)=\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi)))\to\tilde{\pi}(\chi)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By construction, the canonical map of interest fits into a map of short exact sequences

(16)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(M)/\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tilde{\pi}(\chi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau_{\chi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

By Corollary
4.24
and Proposition
4.28
, the left vertical arrow is an isomorphism. The right vertical map is an isomorphism by the irreducibility of its
4
-dimensional target and by Lemma
4.29
. In conclusion, also the middle arrow must be an isomorphism.
∎

We summarize the results of this section in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.31
.

The adjoint pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I})
induces a bijection

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Principal right}\\
\text{$\mathcal{H}$-modules}\end{array}\right\}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Genuine principal series}\\
\text{representations of $\widetilde{G}$}\end{array}\right\},

where both sides are considered up to isomorphism.

Corollary 4.32
.

Let
\pi
be a genuine principal series of
\widetilde{G}
. Then each element in
\pi/X\pi
is killed by a polynomial in
k[F]
.

Proof.

Write
\pi=\mathcal{T}(M)
for a principal
\mathcal{H}
-module
M
. It is enough to show that the statement is true for the kernel and cokernel in the top row of diagram (
16
). For the cokernel this is clear as it is finite dimensional. For the kernel, write
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}=\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}[F^{-1}]
and use Lemma
4.26
, which implies that
\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}=X\mathcal{T}(M)^{\operatorname{bc}}_{0}
.
∎

4.3.4.
Extensions of principal series representations

In this section, we compute the extensions of two genuine principal series representations. Quite surprisingly, this can be deduced from the already existing results for principal series representations of the group
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
. In what follows, the Ext-groups appearing will be computed in the category of smooth
k
-representations of
\widetilde{G}
-, resp.
G
.

We will use the same notation for a genuine principal series as in the last section, i.e. given a smooth character
\chi\colon T_{2}\to k^{\times}
, we put
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)=\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)
, which up to isomorphism only depends on the restriction of
\chi
to
T^{\operatorname{sq}}
.

Given a smooth character
\chi\colon T\to k^{\times}
, we put

\pi(\chi)=\operatorname{Ind}^{G}_{B}(\chi)\text{ and }\tilde{\pi}(\chi)=\tilde{\pi}(\chi|_{T_{2}})=\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi|_{T_{2}}\boxtimes\iota).

Proposition 4.33

(
[
10
, Proposition 4.3.15]
)
.

Let
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\colon T\to k^{\times}
be smooth characters.

(i)

The space

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{G}(\pi(\chi),\pi(\chi^{\prime}))=0

vanishes
unless
\chi^{\prime}=\chi
or
\chi^{\prime}=\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
.

(ii)

The map

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{T}(\chi,\chi)\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{G}(\pi(\chi),\pi(\chi)),

induced by the exact functor
\operatorname{Ind}^{G}_{B}(-)
, is an isomorphism.

(iii)

If
\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})\neq\chi
, then

\dim_{k}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{G}(\pi(\chi),\pi(\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})))=1.

As in the genuine case, we let
\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\subset\pi(\chi)
denote the
B
-invariant subspace consisting of those functions whose support is contained in
BsB
and we identify it
B
-equivariantly with
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},k)\otimes\chi^{s}
. The key ingredient for this section is the
\widetilde{B}
-equivariant isomorphism

(17)

\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\cong\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{B}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}|_{B_{2}}\boxtimes\iota)

resulting from Lemma
4.23
.

Evaluation at the identity yields a short exact sequence

(18)

0\to\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\to\pi(\chi)\to\chi\to 0

of
B
-representations.

Lemma 4.34
.

Let
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\colon T\to k^{\times}
be smooth characters.

(i)

Assume that
\chi^{\prime}\neq\chi
. The short exact sequence (
18
) induces a natural isomorphism

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{G}(\pi(\chi),\pi(\chi^{\prime}))\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}},\chi^{\prime}).

(ii)

Assume that
\chi^{\prime}=\chi
or
\chi^{\prime}\neq\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
. The canonical map

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}},\chi^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{B}(\chi,\chi^{\prime}),

induced by the sequence (
18
),
is injective.

Proof.

By Shapiro’s Lemma we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{G}(\pi(\chi),\pi(\chi^{\prime}))\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{G}(\pi(\chi)|_{B},\chi^{\prime}))
.
The short exact sequence (
18
) induces an exact sequence

(19)

0\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\chi,\chi^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)|_{B},\chi^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}},\chi^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{B}(\chi,\chi^{\prime}).

The zero on the left-hand side holds because
\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}},\chi^{\prime})=0
(as both are irreducible, but non-isomorphic). By
[
10
, Lemma 4.3.7]
, we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{B}(\chi,\chi^{\prime})=\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{T}(\chi,\chi^{\prime})
for
0\leq i\leq 2
.

Assume that
\chi^{\prime}\neq\chi
. By
[
10
, Lemma 4.3.10]
, we then have
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{T}(\chi,\chi^{\prime})=0
for all
i\geq 0
, which gives the assertion in (i).

Assume now that
\chi^{\prime}=\chi
or
\chi^{\prime}\neq\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
. By Proposition
4.33
, the map
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\chi,\chi^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)|_{B},\chi^{\prime})
is surjective proving (ii). Indeed, if
\chi^{\prime}=\chi
, then it is even isomorphism; if
\chi\neq\chi^{\prime}\neq\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
, then the target is trivial.
∎

Recall from Notation
4.15
that for
i,j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
and a smooth representation of a subgroup of
\widetilde{B}
, we put
\pi[i,j]=\pi\otimes(\omega^{i\frac{p-1}{2}}\otimes\omega^{j\frac{p-1}{2}})
. For the purpose of this section, we also define

\pi(i,j)=\pi\otimes(\mu_{-1}^{i}\otimes\mu_{-1}^{j}),

where, as introduced earlier in Secion
4.1
,
\mu_{-1}
is the smooth unramified character mapping
p
to
-1
.

Lemma 4.35
.

Let
\chi\colon T^{\operatorname{sq}}\to k^{\times}
be a smooth character such that
\chi=\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
(as characters on
T^{\operatorname{sq}}
). Then there exists a smooth character
\chi_{e}\colon T\to k^{\times}
such that
\chi_{e}|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}=\chi
and
\chi_{e}=\chi_{e}^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
(as characters on
T
).

Proof.

Let
\psi\colon T\to k^{\times}
be any smooth character extending
\chi
, i.e.
\psi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}=\chi
. The assumption on
\chi
means that
\psi[i,j](a,b)=\psi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
for some (unique) quadruple
(i,j,a,b)
of elements in
\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
. One quickly verifies that necessarily
i=j
and
a=b
as elements of
\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
. Then
\chi_{e}:=\psi[0,j](0,b)
does the job.
∎

Proposition 4.36
.

Let
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\colon T\to k^{\times}
be smooth characters.

(i)

The space

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi),\tilde{\pi}(\chi^{\prime}))=0

vanishes
unless
\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}=\chi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
or
\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}=\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
.

(ii)

The map

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{T_{2}}(\chi|_{T_{2}},\chi|_{T_{2}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi),\tilde{\pi}(\chi)),

induced by the exact functor
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(-\boxtimes\iota)
, is an isomorphism.

(iii)

If
\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}\neq\chi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
, then

\dim_{k}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi),\tilde{\pi}(\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})))=1.

Proof.

Assume first that
\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}\neq\chi|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
, i.e.
\chi^{\prime}\neq\chi[i,j](a,b)
for any
i,j,a,b\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
. Writing
\tau_{\chi}|_{B_{2}}=\oplus_{i,j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}}\chi[i,j]
, we deduce that

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi),\tilde{\pi}(\chi^{\prime}))\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B_{2}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}|_{B_{2}},\chi^{\prime})

just as in the proof of Lemma
4.34
(i). Restricting the isomorphism (
17
) to
B_{2}
and then moving the resultings twists (obtained from Mackey’s decomposition and Lemma
4.14
) over to
\chi^{\prime}
, Frobenius reciprocity implies that the above Ext-space is isomorphic to

\bigoplus_{i,j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}},\operatorname{Ind}^{B}_{B_{2}}(\chi^{\prime}[i,j]))=\bigoplus_{i,j,a,b\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}},\chi^{\prime}[i,j](a,b)).

By our assumption and Lemma
4.34
(i), this is isomorphic to

\bigoplus_{i,j,a,b\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{G}(\pi(\chi),\pi(\chi^{\prime}[i,j](a,b))),

which by Proposition
4.33
is non-zero (and then
1
-dimensional) if and only if
\chi^{\prime}[i,j](a,b)=\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
for some (necessarily unique) quadruple
(i,j,a,b)
of elements in
\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
, or equivalently if and only if
\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}=\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})|_{T^{\operatorname{sq}}}
. This proves (i) and (iii).

To prove (ii), note that the statement only depends on the restriction of
\chi
to
T^{\operatorname{sq}}
. If
\chi=\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
as characters of
T^{\operatorname{sq}}
, Lemma
4.35
allows us to assume that this equality holds on the full group
T
, which we will do. Using the evident analogue of the long exact sequence (
19
), we reduce to showing the injectivity of the map

(20)

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B_{2}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}|_{B_{2}},\chi)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{B_{2}}(\tau_{\chi}|_{T_{2}},\chi).

As before, the left-hand side is equal to
\oplus_{i,j,a,b\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}},\chi[i,j](a,b))
. The right-hand side is similarly equal to
\oplus_{i,j,a,b\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}}\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{B}(\chi,\chi[i,j](a,b))
and, by
[
10
, Lemma 4.3.7, Lemma 4.3.10]
, this equals
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{B}(\chi,\chi)
. By Proposition
4.33
, the extension space
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}},\chi[i,j](a,b))
is trivial unless
3
3

3

Here we are using that we chose
\chi
so that
\chi=\chi^{s}\otimes(\omega\otimes\omega^{-1})
if this happens to be true on
T^{\operatorname{sq}}
.

i=j=a=b=0
in
\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
. Thus, the injectivity of (
20
) is equivalent to the injectivity of the connecting homomorphism

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{B}(\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}},\chi)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{B}(\chi,\chi).

This is the content of Lemma
4.34
(ii).
∎

Remark 4.37
.

Twisting induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{T_{2}}(\chi|_{T_{2}},\chi|_{T_{2}})\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{T_{2}}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})
, which in turn can be identified with
\operatorname{Hom}^{\operatorname{cts}}(T_{2},\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p})
. The latter contains the subspace of unramified homomorphisms, i.e. those trivial on
T_{2}\cap K
, and the resulting inclusion induces a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}^{\operatorname{cts}}(T_{2},k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{T_{2}}(\chi|_{T_{2}},\chi|_{T_{2}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi),\tilde{\pi}(\chi))}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}^{\operatorname{unr}}(T_{2},k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi)),\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi))).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}}

Looking only at those continuous homomorphisms killing the scalar matrix
p^{2}
yields the isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{p^{2}=0}^{\operatorname{unr}}(T_{2},k)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}}(\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi)),\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\pi}(\chi)))
. In particular, the latter Ext-space is
1
-dimensional, cf. Proposition
3.49
.

Remark 4.38
.

Consider the diagram induced by evaluation at the identity,

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tilde{\pi}(\chi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau_{\chi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi(\chi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\chi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the left vertical
B_{2}
-equivariant map is given by sending
f\in\pi(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}
to
\tilde{f}\in\tilde{\pi}(\chi)^{\operatorname{bc}}
uniquely determined by
\tilde{f}\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&x\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\right)=f\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&x\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)f_{0,0}
for all
x\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, and the right vertical
B_{2}
-equivariant map is given by mapping
1\in\chi
to the standard basis vector
f_{0,0}\in\tau_{\chi}
. By the isomorphism (
17
) and the very definition of
\tau_{\chi}
, the
\widetilde{B}
-equivariant maps obtained via Frobenius reciprocity from the outer vertical arrows are isomorphisms. It is tempting to believe that this is also true for
\tilde{\pi}(\chi)
in a compatible way, i.e. such that the top sequence in the above diagram is obtained from the lower one by applying the functor
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{B}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}((-)\boxtimes\iota)
. However, one may check that this diagram cannot be filled in, which also seems to explain why the application of Lemma
4.35
in the proof of the previous proposition is crucial.

4.4.
Supersingular representations

Definition 4.39
.

A
genuine supersingular representation
of
\widetilde{G}
is a smooth irreducible genuine
k
-representation of
\widetilde{G}
, which is not isomorphic to a subquotient of a principal series.

Remark 4.40
.

By Proposition
4.20
, every genuine principal series is irreducible, so removing “a subquotient of” gives an equivalent definition.

The goal of this section is to prove that the adjoint pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I})
induces a bijection between the set of isomorphism classes of supersingular
\mathcal{H}
-modules and the set of isomorphism classes of
admissible
genuine supersingular representations. We will later show that any smooth irreducible representation of
\widetilde{G}
is already admissible, see Theorem
5.17
. For showing that
\mathcal{T}(M)
is irreducible, for a supersingular Hecke module
M
, we will use a weight-cycling argument, that has already been used for supersingular representations of
G
, see
[
22
, §4.1]
or
[
15
, §10]
.

4.4.1.
Basic lemma

In a previous draft of this work we worked out the weight-cycling in the specific case of interest (in dimension four). However, in a lecture
[
14
]
of Florian Herzig at the Spring School on the mod-
p
Langlands Correspondence, which took place in Essen (online) in 2021, he formulated it more generally (in dimension
n\geq 1
) and called the resulting statement “Basic Lemma”. In this subsection, we simply reproduce his result.

Setup.
Let
\pi
be a smooth
k
-representation of the mirabolic subgroup
P
. Suppose that we are given
k
-linearly independent
\Gamma
-eigenvectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\pi[X]
killed by
X
, satisfying

(21)

X^{s_{i}}F(v_{i})=c_{i}v_{i+1}\text{ for some $s_{i}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}$ and $c_{i}\in k^{\times}$}

for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Here and in what follows we understand the subscript
i
to be the unique element in
\{1,\ldots,n\}
congruent to
i
modulo
n
. We define

M=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}kv_{i}

and let

\pi_{M}=k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F]M\subset\pi.

be the
k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F]
-submodule generated by
M
in
\pi
. Finally, for
1\leq i\leq n
, we put

\pi_{i}=k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F^{n}]v_{i}\subset\pi_{M}.

We wish to prove the following result.

Lemma 4.41

(Basic Lemma)
.

(i)

For each
1\leq i\leq n
, the subspace
\pi_{i}\subset\pi
is
\Gamma
-stable.

(ii)

The subspace
\pi_{M}\subset\pi
is
P^{+}
-stable.

(iii)

For each
1\leq i\leq n
,
\pi_{i}[X]=kv_{i}
.

(iv)

As
k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F^{n},\Gamma]
-modules,
\pi_{M}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i}
.

(v)

If
s_{i}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, then
\pi_{i}=kv_{i}
.

(vi)

If
s_{j}>0
for some
1\leq j\leq n
, then
\pi_{i}\cong\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U\cap I,k)
as
U\cap I
-representations and the inclusion
kv_{i}\subset\pi_{i}
is an injective envelope in the category of smooth
U\cap I
-representations, for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

(vii)

Assume that the following condition is satisfied: If for some
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
, the vectors
v_{i},v_{j}
have the same
\Gamma
-eigencharacter, then
s_{i}\neq s_{j}
. Then
\pi_{M}
is an irreducible
P^{+}
-representation.

This is a more detailed version of the Basic Lemma in Herzig’s lecture alluded to above. The last part on the irreducibility was for instance not contained in Herzig’s version. However, the additional details are essentially just a byproduct of the proof of the original version. Before jumping into the proof, we make some preparations.

Definition 4.42
.

For
1\leq i\leq n
, define

e(i)=p^{n-1}s_{i}+p^{n-2}s_{i+1}+\ldots+p^{2}s_{i+n-3}+ps_{i+n-2}+s_{i+n-1}

and, if
m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 1}
, put

e(i)_{m}=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}p^{jn}e(i).

We let

c=\prod_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}

be the product of the scalars appearing in (
21
).

Lemma 4.43
.

Let
1\leq i\leq n
. The following hold true:

(i)

X^{p^{n-1}s_{i}+p^{n-2}s_{i+1}+\ldots+p^{2}s_{i+n-3}+ps_{i+n-2}}F^{n}(v_{i})=\left(\prod_{j\neq i-1}c_{j}\right)F(v_{i-1})
;

(ii)

for all
m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
X^{e(i)_{m}}F^{nm}(v_{i})=c^{m}v_{i}
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from the relation
FX=X^{p}F
and inductively applying the equations (
21
) starting with
X^{s_{i}}F(v_{i})=c_{i}v_{i+1}
. The case of
m=1
in part (ii) now follows from this by applying
X^{s_{i-1}}
to (i) and using (
21
). Inductively applying the resulting relation gives the assertion for an arbitrary
m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
∎

Proof of the Basic Lemma
4.41
.

Part (i) and (ii) follow from the relations
\gamma F=F\gamma
and
\gamma X=((X+1)^{\gamma}-1)\gamma
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
. By Lemma
4.43
(i),
F\pi_{i-1}\subset\pi_{i}
and thus
\pi_{M}=\sum_{i}\pi_{i}
. In particular, part (iv) will follow from part (iii) as the vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
are linearly independent.

Fix
1\leq i\leq n
. For
m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let
\pi_{i,m}=k\llbracket X\rrbracket F^{nm}(v_{i})
be the
k\llbracket X\rrbracket
-submodule of
\pi_{i}
generated
F^{nm}(v_{i})
. Applying
F^{nm}
to Lemma
4.43
(ii) (with
m=1
), shows that
kv_{i}\subset\pi_{i,m}\subset\pi_{i,m+1}
. By Lemma
4.43
(ii), the
k\llbracket X\rrbracket
-linear map

(22)

\displaystyle k\llbracket X\rrbracket/(X^{e(i)_{m}+1})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{i,m}

\displaystyle f(X)

\displaystyle\mapsto c^{-m}f(X)F^{nm}(v_{i})

is a well-defined isomorphism. Comparing
X
-torsion shows that
\pi_{i,m}[X]
is
1
-dimensional, necessarily equal to
kv_{i}
. This proves (iii). The isomorphisms (
22
) fit into a commutative diagram

(23)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 46.01022pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.19478pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\pi_{i,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.1948pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 133.48546pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 133.48546pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\pi_{i,m+1}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-46.01022pt\raise-31.09222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{k\llbracket X\rrbracket/(X^{e(i)_{m}+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.25pt\raise-15.54611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-8.3611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.01024pt\raise-31.09222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.27667pt\raise-24.70831pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.38391pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cdot X^{p^{nm}e(i)}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 94.01022pt\raise-31.09222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 94.01022pt\raise-31.09222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{k\llbracket X\rrbracket/(X^{e(i+1)_{m+1}+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.74277pt\raise-15.54611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.74277pt\raise-8.3611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

For
d\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we identify the
k
-linear dual
\left(k\llbracket X\rrbracket/(X^{d+1})\right)^{\vee}
with
k\llbracket X\rrbracket/(X^{d+1})
via the
k\llbracket X\rrbracket
-linear map corresponding to the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{d}\in\left(k\llbracket X\rrbracket/(X^{d+1})\right)^{\vee}
defined by
\operatorname{pr}_{d}(X^{j})=\delta_{jd}
, the Kronecker delta, for
0\leq j\leq d
. Under these identifications, the Pontryagin dual of the lower horizontal map is simply the canonical projection. Passing to the inverse limit, we obtain

\pi_{i}^{\vee}=(\varinjlim_{m\geq 1}\pi_{i,m})^{\vee}=\varprojlim_{m\geq 1}\pi_{i,m}^{\vee}\cong\varprojlim_{m\geq 1}k\llbracket X\rrbracket/(X^{e(i)_{m}+1}).

If
s_{j}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq n
, then
e(i)_{m}=0
, so we obtain
\pi_{i}=kv_{i}
, which proves (v). If however
s_{j}
is non-zero for some
1\leq j\leq n
, then
e(i)_{m}
tends to infinity as
m\to\infty
. In this case we therefore obtain
\pi_{i}^{\vee}\cong k\llbracket X\rrbracket
as
k\llbracket X\rrbracket
-modules, or equivalently
\pi_{i}\cong\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U\cap I,k)
as smooth
U\cap I
-representations. In particular,
\pi_{i}
is injective in the category of smooth
U\cap I
-modules, and since
\pi_{i}[X]=kv_{i}
, we deduce that the inclusion
kv_{i}\subset\pi_{i}
is an injective envelope proving part (vi).

Assume now that the condition formulated in (vii) holds.
Let
\tau\subset\pi_{M}
be a non-zero
P^{+}
-subrepresentation (or equivalently a
k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F,\Gamma]
-submodule). Since
\pi_{M}[X]=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}kv_{i}
is a
\Gamma
-stable subspace, there exists a subset
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
, for some
r\leq n
, such that

\sum_{j=1}^{r}a_{i_{j}}v_{i_{j}}\in\tau\text{ for some $a_{i_{j}}\in k^{\times}$,}

where the
v_{i_{j}}
, for
1\leq j\leq r
, have the same
\Gamma
-eigencharacter. After reordering we may assume that
s_{i_{j+1}}\geq s_{i_{j}}
for all
1\leq j\leq r-1
. Applying
X^{1+s_{i_{r-1}}}F(-)
will kill
v_{i_{j}}
for
1\leq j\leq r-1
. Here we are using equation (
21
) and the fact the
Xv_{j}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq n
. Thus,
a_{i_{r}}X^{1+s_{i_{r-1}}}F(v_{i_{r}})\in\tau
. Since
s_{i_{r-1}}\neq s_{i_{r}}
(and so
s_{i_{r-1}}<s_{i_{r}}
) by our assumption, applying
X^{s_{i_{r}}-s_{i_{r-1}}-1}
and using equation (
21
) once again, we see that
v_{i_{r}+1}\in\tau
. Another application of the same equation now shows that
\tau
contains
v_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
and must therefore be equal to
\pi_{M}
.
∎

Corollary 4.44
.

Assume that the following condition is satisfied: If for some
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
, the vectors
v_{i},v_{j}
have the same
\Gamma
-eigencharacter, then
s_{i}\neq s_{j}
. Then the subspace

\pi_{M}[F^{-1}]=\varinjlim_{m\geq 1}F^{-m}\pi_{M}\subset\pi

is
P
-stable and -irreducible.

Proof.

By the Basic Lemma
4.41
, the subspace
\pi_{M}\subset\pi
is
P^{+}
-stable and -irreducible. The equality
P=F^{-\mathbf{N}}P^{+}
now implies the assertion.
∎

4.4.2.
From supersingular Hecke modules to supersingular representations

Let
M
be a supersingular
\mathcal{H}(O)
-module for some orbit
O
. Applying the Basic Lemma, we now prove that the
\mathcal{T}(M)
is irreducible as a
P
-representation (hence as a
\widetilde{G}
-representation), and that the canonical map
M\to\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{T}(M))
is an isomorphism. Using Lemma
4.3
, we may and do assume that, in the non-square-regular case,
\mathbf{1}\in O
. We fix a character
\chi\in O
, with
\chi=\mathbf{1}
in the non-square-regular case. By Definition
3.29
, resp.
3.38
, we can write

M=\begin{cases}\operatorname{SS}(\chi,z)\text{ if $\chi$ is square-regular}\\
\operatorname{SS}(\lambda,z)\text{ if $\chi=\mathbf{1}$}\end{cases}

for some
z\in k^{\times}
and
\lambda\in\{0,-1\}
. By Proposition
3.27
, resp.
3.36
, we have

(24)

M=\bigoplus_{\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}Me_{\psi}\text{ and }\dim_{\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}}Me_{\psi}=1,

where
\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
denotes the
\Pi
-orbit of
\chi
as introduced in Definition
3.11
.

It is explained in Remark
5.14
below that
\mathcal{T}(M)
is non-zero, so that the canonical map
M\hookrightarrow\mathcal{I}\mathcal{T}(M)
is an injection, via which we will view elements of
M
as elements of
\mathcal{T}(M)
.

Lemma 4.45
.

For each
\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
, the Hecke operator
T_{s}
acts on
Me_{\psi}
via a scalar. More precisely:

(i)

If
O
is square-regular, then
T_{s}=0
on
M
.

(ii)

If
O
is non-square-regular, then
T_{s}
acts on
Me_{\psi}
via
\lambda
(resp.
0
) if
\psi=\psi^{s}
(resp.
\psi\neq\psi^{s}
).

Proof.

The decomposition (
24
) together with the relation
e_{\psi}T_{s}=T_{s}e_{\psi^{s}}
of Lemma
3.7
(iv) already implies (i). For (ii), note that by applying the element
T_{\Pi}^{2}
and using Lemma
3.9
(iv) and (v), it suffices to treat the characters
\psi=\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}[0,1]
. Using part (v) once again and applying the invertible element
T_{\Pi}
from the right, the vanishing of
Me_{\mathbf{1}[0,1]}T_{s}=Me_{\mathbf{1}}T_{\Pi}T_{s}
is equivalent to the vanishing of
Me_{\mathbf{1}}T_{\Pi}T_{s}T_{\Pi}
, which by parts (vi) and (ix) of the cited lemma means that
T_{\Pi s\Pi}
, or equivalently
S_{0,2}\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
, kills
Me_{\mathbf{1}}
. This holds true by the very definition of
M
, which also says that
T_{s}
, or equivalently
S_{0,0}\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})
, acts on
Me_{\mathbf{1}}
by the scalar
\lambda
.
∎

Recall from Definition
4.9
that to a character
\psi\in\widehat{H}
we attached the semi-simple smooth
K
-representation
W(\psi)
, that is irreducible if and only if
\psi\neq\psi^{s}
.

Corollary 4.46
.

For each
\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
, the
\widetilde{K}
-subrepresentation
\sigma(\psi):=\left\langle\widetilde{K}Me_{\psi}\right\rangle\subset\mathcal{T}(M)
generated by
Me_{\psi}
is irreducible. More precisely:

(i)

If
O
is square-regular, then
\sigma(\psi)=W(\psi)\boxtimes\iota
.

(ii)

If
O
is non-square-regular and
\psi\neq\psi^{s}
, then
\sigma(\psi)=W(\psi)\boxtimes\iota
