, while

\sigma(\mathbf{1})=\begin{cases}\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota\text{ if $\lambda=0$}\\
\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}(k^{2})\boxtimes\iota\text{ if $\lambda=-1$}\end{cases}\text{ and \phantom{m}}\sigma(\mathbf{1}[1,1])=\sigma(\mathbf{1})\otimes{\det}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}.

Proof.

Frobenius reciprocity yields a
\widetilde{K}
-equivariant surjection
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{K}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\psi\boxtimes\iota)\twoheadrightarrow\sigma(\psi)
mapping the
I_{1}
-invariant function
\varphi
with
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)=\widetilde{I}
and
\varphi(1)=1
to a fixed element in
Me_{\psi}
. By Lemma
4.45
, the operator
T_{s}
acts on
Me_{\psi}
by a scalar. However, it does not act by a scalar on
\varphi
, and so the surjection cannot be an isomorphism, i.e. the kernel is non-trivial.

Assume that
\psi\neq\psi^{s}
. By Proposition
4.8
, we have a short exact sequence

0\to W(\psi^{s})\boxtimes\iota\to\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{K}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\psi\boxtimes\iota)\to W(\psi)\boxtimes\iota\to 0

of
\widetilde{K}
-representation with the outer terms being irreducible. Since this sequence is non-split, we deduce that
\sigma(\psi)\cong W(\psi)\boxtimes\iota
.

Assume now that
\psi=\psi^{s}
. By Lemma
4.4
, the element
\tilde{\Pi}^{2}
defines a
\widetilde{K}
-equivariant isomorphism
\mathcal{T}(M)\cong\mathcal{T}(M)\otimes\omega^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\circ{\det}
restricting to an isomorphism
\sigma(\mathbf{1}[1,1])\cong\sigma(\mathbf{1})\otimes{\det}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}
, so it is enough to treat the case
\psi=\mathbf{1}
. By Proposition
4.8
once again, we have
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{K}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)=\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}(k^{2})\boxtimes\iota
. The
I_{1}
-invariants of the second summand are
1
-dimensional generated by
\varphi T_{s}
. Hence, the kernel of
\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{K}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\twoheadrightarrow\sigma(\psi)
meets
\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}(k^{2})\boxtimes\iota
trivially if and only if
\varphi T_{s}
does not lie in the kernel, i.e. if and only if
T_{s}
does not kill
Me_{\mathbf{1}}
, which by Lemma
4.45
just means that
\lambda=-1
.
∎

By Proposition
4.7
, we may write, for each
\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
,

\sigma(\psi)=\operatorname{Sym}^{r_{\psi}}(k^{2})\otimes{\det}^{b_{\psi}}

for a unique
0\leq r_{\psi}\leq p-1
and
0\leq b_{\psi}\leq p-2
.

Remark 4.47
.

By Lemma
4.4
, the element
\tilde{\Pi}^{2}
defines an isomorphism
\sigma(\psi[1,1])\cong\sigma(\psi)\otimes\omega^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\circ{\det}
, i.e.
r_{\psi[1,1]}=r_{\psi}
and
b_{\psi[1,1]}\equiv b_{\psi}+\frac{p-1}{2}\bmod p-1
. It follows more generally from Lemma
4.46
that a single weight
\sigma(\psi)
determines all the other ones, namely

b_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}\equiv b_{\psi}+r_{\psi}\bmod p-1\text{ and }r_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}=\begin{cases}\frac{p-1}{2}-r_{\psi}\text{ if $0\leq r_{\psi}\leq\frac{p-1}{2}$}\\
\frac{3(p-1)}{2}-r_{\psi}\text{ if $\frac{p-1}{2}\leq r_{\psi}\leq p-1$.}\end{cases}

Corollary 4.48
.

For each
\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
v_{\psi}\in Me_{\psi}
,

X^{r_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}}F(v_{\psi})=(-1)^{b_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}}r_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}!v_{\psi}T_{\Pi}^{-1}.

Proof.

We may assume that
v_{\psi}\neq 0
. Since
F=\tilde{s}\tilde{\Pi}
and
\tilde{\Pi}
acts on
M
via the Hecke operator
T_{\Pi}^{-1}
, we need to show that

(25)

X^{r_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}}\tilde{s}(v_{\psi}T_{\Pi}^{-1})=(-1)^{b_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}}r_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}!v_{\psi}T_{\Pi}^{-1}.

The relation
e_{\psi}T_{\Pi}^{-1}=T_{\Pi}^{-1}e_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}
of Lemma
3.9
(i) implies that
0\neq v_{\psi}T_{\Pi}^{-1}\in Me_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}\subset\sigma(\psi^{s}[1,0])^{I_{1}}
. By Lemma
4.46
,
\sigma(\psi^{s}[1,0])
is a weight. Therefore, equation (
25
) is an explicit computation in
\sigma(\psi^{s}[1,0])=\operatorname{Sym}^{r_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}}(k^{2})\otimes{\det}^{b_{\psi^{s}[1,0]}}
, that is for example carried out in
[
22
, Lemma 3.2]
.
∎

We now bring ourselves into the following setup: Fix
0\neq v_{1}\in Me_{\chi}
and put
v_{i}=v_{1}T_{\Pi}^{-i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq 4
. We then have

M=\underbrace{kv_{1}}_{Me_{\chi}}\oplus\underbrace{kv_{2}}_{Me_{\chi^{s}[1,0]}}\oplus\underbrace{kv_{3}}_{Me_{\chi[1,1]}}\oplus\underbrace{kv_{4}}_{Me_{\chi^{s}[0,1]}}

We further set

s_{1}=r_{\chi^{s}[1,0]},\hskip 5.69046pts_{2}=r_{\chi[1,1]},\hskip 5.69046pts_{3}=r_{\chi^{s}[0,1]},\hskip 5.69046pts_{4}=r_{\chi}

and

\displaystyle c_{1}=(-1)^{b_{\chi^{s}[1,0]}}r_{\chi^{s}[1,0]}!,\hskip 8.5359pt

\displaystyle c_{2}=(-1)^{b_{\chi[1,1]}}r_{\chi[1,1]}!,

\displaystyle c_{3}=(-1)^{b_{\chi^{s}[0,1]}}r_{\chi^{s}[0,1]}!,\hskip 8.5359pt

\displaystyle c_{4}=(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}z^{-1}(-1)^{b_{\chi}}r_{\chi}!.

By Corollary
4.48
, we then have

X^{s_{i}}F(v_{i})=c_{i}v_{i+1}\text{ for all $1\leq i\leq 4$.}

Here, note that, by Lemma
3.9
(iv),
T_{\Pi}^{-4}=(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}T_{\Pi^{-4}}
, which acts on
M
via
(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}z^{-1}
. By the Basic Lemma
4.41
(vii),

\pi_{M}=k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F]M,

is an irreducible
P^{+}
-subrepresentation of
\mathcal{T}(M)
: in the square-regular case, the
\Gamma
-eigencharacters of the vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{4}
are pairwise distinct, while in case
\mathbf{1}\in O
, the
\Gamma
-eigencharacters of
Me_{\mathbf{1}}
and
Me_{\mathbf{1}[0,1]}
(resp.
Me_{\mathbf{1}[1,1]}
and
Me_{\mathbf{1}[1,0]}
) coincide, but the corresponding
s_{i}
’s differ by
\frac{p-1}{2}
(see Remark
4.47
), so we can indeed apply the Basic Lemma.

Proposition 4.49
.

The equality
\mathcal{T}(M)=\pi_{M}[F^{-1}]
holds true. In particular, the restriction of
\mathcal{T}(M)
to
P
is irreducible.

Proof.

We first claim that
\pi_{M}
contains the weights
\sigma(\psi)
for all
\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
. The Iwahori decomposition and the inclusion
(I\cap sUs)F\subset F(I\cap sUs)
imply that
\pi_{M}
is
I
-stable. Now
\sigma(\psi)
, being a weight, is generated as a
K
-representation by its
I_{1}
-invariants
Me_{\psi}
, which are contained in
\pi_{M}
by definition. Using the decomposition
K=I\sqcup IsI
, it suffices to prove that
\tilde{s}Me_{\psi}\in\pi_{M}
. Writing
\tilde{s}=F\tilde{\Pi}^{-1}
, we have

\tilde{s}Me_{\psi}=F(Me_{\psi}T_{\Pi})=F(Me_{\psi^{s}[0,1]})\subset\pi_{M}.

As already used a few times, the element
\tilde{\Pi}^{2}
acting on
\mathcal{T}(M)
restricts to an isomorphism
\sigma(\psi[1,1])\cong\sigma(\psi)\otimes{\det}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}
, which implies that
M
is stable under the action of
\widetilde{Z}
. Putting this together with the previous claim, we obtain

(26)

\mathcal{T}(M)\supset\pi_{M}[F^{-1}]=\left\langle\widetilde{B}.\sum_{\psi}\sigma(\psi)\right\rangle=\left\langle\widetilde{B}K.\sum_{\psi}\sigma(\psi)\right\rangle\supset\left\langle\widetilde{G}.M\right\rangle=\mathcal{T}(M),

i.e.
\pi_{M}[F^{-1}]=\mathcal{T}(M)
. As explained prior to this proposition, the condition of Corollary
4.44
is satisfied, whence
\pi_{M}[F^{-1}]=\mathcal{T}(M)
is irreducible as a
P
-representation.
∎

4.4.3.
…and back

It remains to prove that the unit
M\to\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{T}(M))
is an isomorphism. We will use an analogue of the short exact sequence in
[
22
, Theorem 6.3]
, the proof of which we will closely follow.

For
1\leq i\leq 4
, let

\pi_{i}=k\llbracket X\rrbracket[F^{4}]v_{i}

as in the setup of Section
4.41
. The Iwahori decomposition and the inclusion
(I\cap sUs)F\subset F(I\cap sUs)
imply that
\pi_{i}
is
\widetilde{I}
-stable, and
Z_{1}:=I_{1}\cap Z
acts trivially on it. By part (iii) and (vi) of the Basic Lemma
4.41
,
0\neq\pi_{i}^{I_{1}}\subset\pi_{i}^{I\cap U}=kv_{i}
(hence
\pi_{i}^{I_{1}}=kv_{i}
) and the inclusion
kv_{i}\subset\pi_{i}
is an injective envelope in the category of smooth
I\cap U
-representations. Thus,
(\pi_{i}/kv_{i})^{I\cap U}=H^{1}(I\cap U,kv_{i})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},k)
is
1
-dimensional; in particular,
(\pi_{i}/kv_{i})^{I_{1}}=(\pi_{i}/kv_{i})^{I\cap U}
.

Consider the short exact sequence

0\to kv_{i}\to\pi_{i}\to\pi_{i}/kv_{i}\to 0

of
\widetilde{I}/Z_{1}
-representations. The injection becomes an equality on
I_{1}
-invariants, so we obtain an inclusion

(\pi_{i}/kv_{i})^{I_{1}}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},kv_{i})\subset H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M),

whose
1
-dimensional image we denote by
\Delta_{i}
. Using the decomposition
\pi_{M}=\oplus_{i=1}^{4}\pi_{i}
, we take the direct sum of the above inclusions and call the resulting image
\Delta
:

\Delta=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{4}\Delta_{i}=\operatorname{image}\left((\pi_{M}/M)^{I_{1}}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M)\right).

The element
\tilde{\Pi}
normalizes
I_{1}
and its subgroup
Z_{1}
. Its action on
M
thus induces an action on
H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M)
:

[\tilde{\Pi}.f](x)=\tilde{\Pi}f(\tilde{\Pi}^{-1}x\tilde{\Pi})\text{ for all $x\in I_{1}$ and $f\in\operatorname{Hom}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M)$.}

In particular,
\tilde{\Pi}\Delta_{i}\subset H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},kv_{i+1})
. Note that
\tilde{\Pi}^{2}\Delta_{i}=\Delta_{i+2}
since
\tilde{\Pi}^{2}\pi_{i}=\pi_{i+2}
.

Lemma 4.50
.

The intersection
\Delta\cap\tilde{\Pi}\Delta=0
is trivial.

Proof.

We need to show that for each
1\leq i\leq 4
,
\tilde{\Pi}\Delta_{i}\cap\Delta_{i+1}=0
. Acting by
\tilde{\Pi}
, we may replace
i
by
i+1
and thus assume that the
H
-eigencharacter
\chi_{i}
of
v_{i}
is regular, i.e.
\chi_{i}\neq\chi_{i}^{s}
. By
[
22
, Proposition 5.2]
, a
k
-basis of
H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}v_{j})\cong\operatorname{Hom}(I_{1}/Z_{1},kv_{j})
, for
1\leq j\leq 4
, is given by the homomorphisms
\kappa^{u}_{j},\kappa^{\ell}_{j}
defined by

\kappa^{u}_{j}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\omega(b)v_{j}\text{ and }\kappa^{\ell}_{j}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\omega(p^{-1}c)v_{j}\text{ for all $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in I_{1}$.}

Since the eigencharacter of
v_{i}
is regular, we have
\Delta_{i}=k\kappa^{u}_{i}
, by Proposition 6.2 (i) in
loc. cit.
The cited proposition also implies that
\Delta_{i+1}=k(\kappa^{u}_{i+1}+b\kappa^{\ell}_{i+1})
for some
b\in k
. Noting that
\tilde{\Pi}\kappa^{u}_{i}
is a non-zero scalar multiple of
\kappa^{\ell}_{i+1}
, we obtain

\tilde{\Pi}\Delta_{i}\cap\Delta_{i+1}=k\kappa^{\ell}_{i+1}\cap k(\kappa^{u}_{i+1}+b\kappa^{\ell}_{i+1})=0,

which finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.51
.

The
\langle\widetilde{I},\tilde{\Pi}\rangle
-equivariant sequence

0\to M\xrightarrow{\operatorname{diag}}\pi_{M}\oplus\tilde{\Pi}\pi_{M}\xrightarrow{(v,w)\mapsto v-w}\mathcal{T}(M)\to 0

is exact.

Proof.

With the previous preparations, the same proof as in
[
22
, Theorem 6.3]
works.
∎

Corollary 4.52
.

The unit
M\to\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{T}(M))
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Since
M
is irreducible, the unit is an inclusion, so it is enough to prove that
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{T}(M))
has the same dimension as
M
. The equality
M=\pi_{M}^{I_{1}}
reduces us to proving that the sequence in Proposition
4.51
remains exact after taking
I_{1}
-invariants, i.e. we need that the map
H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M)\to H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},\pi_{M}\oplus\tilde{\Pi}\pi_{M})
is injective. This amounts to showing that
W\cap\tilde{\Pi}W=0
, where
W
is the kernel of
H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M)\to H^{1}(I_{1},/Z_{1},\pi_{M})
. Since
\pi_{M}^{I_{1}}=M
, the kernel
W
is equal to the image of the injection
(\pi_{M}/M)^{I_{1}}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(I_{1}/Z_{1},M)
, i.e.
W=\Delta
. The corollary now follows from Lemma
4.50
.
∎

Corollary 4.53
.

The
K
-socle of
\mathcal{T}(M)
is equal to
\bigoplus_{\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}\sigma(\psi)|_{K}
with
\sigma(\psi)
as in Corollary
4.46
.

Proof.

By Corollary
4.46
,
\mathcal{T}(M)
contains the pairwise distinct weights
\sigma(\psi)
for
\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
, so we obtain an inclusion
\bigoplus_{\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}\sigma(\psi)|_{K}\subset\operatorname{soc}_{K}(\mathcal{T}(M))
. By Corollary
4.52
this is an equality on
I_{1}
-invariants, which implies the assertion.
∎

Theorem 4.54
.

The adjoint pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I})
induces a bijection

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Supersingular right}\\
\text{$\mathcal{H}$-modules}\end{array}\right\}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Admissible genuine supersingular}\\
\text{representations of $\widetilde{G}$},\end{array}\right\}

where both sides are considered up to isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
M
be a supersingular
\mathcal{H}
-module. By Proposition
4.49
, the
\widetilde{G}
-representation
\mathcal{T}(M)
is irreducible. If it was not supersingular, then it would be isomorphic to a principal series, see Remark
4.40
. By Theorem
4.31
and Corollary
4.52
, this is impossible and we deduce that
\mathcal{T}(M)
is supersingular and that the assignment
M\mapsto\mathcal{T}(M)
is injective with inverse
\mathcal{I}
. Let now
\pi
be a genuine supersingular
\widetilde{G}
-representation. Then
\mathcal{I}(\pi)
is a non-zero finite dimensional
\mathcal{H}
-module and hence contains some simple module
M
. By the classification results for simple Hecke modules,
M
is either principal or supersingular. In any case, the attached
\widetilde{G}
-representation
\mathcal{T}(M)
is irreducible (as we just explained in the supersingular case and by Theorem
4.31
in the principal case). By adjunction we obtain a non-trivial
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant map
\mathcal{T}(M)\to\pi
, which has to be an isomorphism as both sides are irreducible. Once again using Theorem
4.31
, we see that
M
cannot be a principal module and therefore needs to be supersingular. This proves surjectivitiy.
∎

Corollary 4.55
.

Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
be genuine supersingular
\widetilde{G}
-representations. Then the restriction functor from
\widetilde{G}
to
P
induces an equality

\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})=\operatorname{Hom}_{P}(\pi_{1}|_{P},\pi_{2}|_{P}).

In other words,
\pi_{1}\cong\pi_{2}
as
\widetilde{G}
-representations if and only if
\pi_{1}|_{P}\cong\pi_{2}|_{P}
as
P
-representations.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.54
, we may write
\pi_{i}=\mathcal{T}(M_{i})
for a supersingular
\mathcal{H}(O_{i})
-module
M_{i}
, for some orbit
O_{i}
, for
i=1,2
. By Proposition
4.49
,
\pi_{i}
is then irreducible as a
P
-representation. Assume now that
f\colon\pi_{1}|_{P}\cong\pi_{2}|_{P}
is an isomorphism of
P
-representations. Since
\pi_{2}=\pi_{M_{2}}[F^{-1}]
, we can choose
N\gg 0
such that
F^{4N}f(M_{1})\subset\pi_{M_{2}}
. Using Lemma
4.43
(ii), we then obtain
f(M_{1})\subset\pi_{M_{2}}
. Comparing
X
-torsion shows that
f(M_{1})\subset M_{2}
, which is an equality by symmetry, i.e.
f
restricts to a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism
f\colon M_{1}\cong M_{2}
.

We now check that
O_{1}=O_{2}
. The space
M_{i}
is the direct sum of
1
-dimensional
\Gamma
-stable subspaces. If
O_{i}
is square-regular, then the
\Gamma
-characters in
M_{i}
appear with multiplicity one. If
O_{i}
is non-square-regular, then the
\Gamma
-characters in
M_{i}
appear with multiplicity two. This already implies that
O_{1}
is square-regular if and only if
O_{2}
is square-regular. For
i=1,2
, let us now fix a character
\chi_{i}\in O_{i}
, which we choose to satisfy
\chi_{i}=\chi_{i}^{s}
in the non-square-regular case. Let
O_{i}^{2}
denote the set of characters on
H
obtained by squaring those in
O_{i}
. Then
O_{i}^{2}
determines
O_{i}
. Concretely,
O_{i}^{2}=\{\chi_{i}^{2},(\chi_{i}^{2})^{s}\}
, which has cardinality two if and only if
O_{i}
is square-regular. We can recover
O_{i}^{2}
from
\{\chi_{i}^{2}|_{\Gamma},(\chi_{i}^{2})^{s}|_{\Gamma}\}
, and so
O_{1}=O_{2}
. After possibly replacing
\chi_{2}
, we may assume that
\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}
, which we denote by
\chi
. In the non-square-regular case we may, after possibly twisting (Lemma
4.3
), assume that
\chi=\mathbf{1}
.

For
i=1,2
and
\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
, let
\sigma_{i}(\psi)\subset\pi_{i}
denote the weight generated by
M_{i}e_{\psi}
, see Lemma
4.46
, and put
r_{i}(\psi)=\dim_{k}\sigma_{i}(\psi)-1
.
We claim that
r_{1}(\psi)=r_{2}(\psi)
for all
\psi\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
. By Remark
4.47
, it suffices to prove this for a single
\psi
. Choose
\psi
such that
r_{1}(\psi)\geq r_{1}(\psi^{\prime})
for all
\psi^{\prime}\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
. By symmetry, we may assume that
r_{1}(\psi)\geq r_{2}(\psi)
. Assume by contradiction that
r_{1}(\psi)>r_{2}(\psi)
. Looking at the
\Gamma
-eigencharacter, we must have

f(v_{\psi^{s}[0,1]})=\alpha w_{\psi^{s}[0,1]}+\beta w_{\psi^{s}}\text{ for some $\alpha,\beta\in k$ with $\alpha\beta\neq 0$,}

where we put
w_{\psi^{s}}:=0
in the square-regular case.
By the recursion formula in Corollary
4.48
, we have

(27)

X^{r_{1}(\psi)}F(v_{\psi^{s}[0,1]})=av_{\psi}\text{ for some $a\in k^{\times}$.}

Applying the
P
-equivariant isomorphism
f
to this equation, we obtain

(28)

af(v_{\psi})=\alpha X^{r_{1}(\psi)}F(w_{\psi^{s}[0,1]})+\beta X^{r_{1}(\psi)}F(w_{\psi^{s}}),

where the first summand vanishes since
r_{1}(\psi)>r_{2}(\psi)
(using the analogue of equation (
27
) for
w_{\psi^{s}[0,1]}
and the fact that
Xw_{\psi}=0
). It follows that
\beta\neq 0
and

(29)

X^{r_{1}(\psi)}F(w_{\psi^{s}})=\beta^{-1}af(v_{\psi})\neq 0.

This already gives a contradiction in the square-regular case (as we put
w_{\psi^{s}}=0
in this case). It remains to treat the non-square-regular case. Since the expression in (
29
) is non-zero, we deduce that
r_{1}(\psi)\leq r_{2}(\psi[1,0])
; and since it is killed by
X
, we then deduce that
r_{1}(\psi)=r_{2}(\psi[1,0])
(both of these observations follow from Corollary
4.48
). Now either
\psi=\psi^{s}
or
\psi[1,0]=\psi[1,0]^{s}
, i.e. either
r_{i}(\psi)\in\{0,p-1\}
(independent of
i
) or
r_{i}(\psi[1,0])\in\{0,p-1\}
(independent of
i
), but not both. Hence, the equality
r_{1}(\psi)=r_{2}(\psi[1,0])
is impossible. This proves the claim.

Using the claim we obtain that
f(v_{\psi})=\gamma w_{\psi}
for some
\gamma\in k^{\times}
. Indeed, in the square-regular case, there is nothing to prove. In the non-square-regular case, note that
r_{2}(\psi[1,0])=r_{1}(\psi[1,0])<r_{1}(\psi)=r_{2}(\psi)
, so the second summand in equation (
28
) vanishes, while the first one is a non-zero scalar multiple of
w_{\psi}
.

For
i=1,2
, let us now write

M_{i}=\begin{cases}\operatorname{SS}(\chi,z_{i})\text{ if $O$ is square-regular}\\
\operatorname{SS}(\lambda_{i},z_{i})\text{ if $\mathbf{1}\in O$,}\end{cases}

where
z_{i}\in k^{\times}
and
\lambda_{i}\in\{0,-1\}
as in Definition
3.29
, resp.
3.38
. It follows from Corollary
4.46
(ii) that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
since
r_{1}(\chi)=r_{2}(\chi)
. In order to conclude that
M_{1}=M_{2}
, it remains to show that
z_{1}=z_{2}
. By Lemma
4.43
(ii), we have

X^{e(\psi)}F^{4}(v_{\psi})=(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}z_{i}^{-1}\left(\prod_{\psi^{\prime}\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}r_{1}(\psi^{\prime})!\right)v_{\psi},

where the exponent
e(\psi)
only depends on
r_{1}(\psi)
(which determines all the other
r_{1}(\psi^{\prime})
). A similar equality also holds true for
w_{\psi}
. Applying the
P
-equivariant map
f
and using the equalities
r_{1}(\psi^{\prime})=r_{2}(\psi^{\prime})
for all
\psi^{\prime}\in\chi^{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
allows us to conclude
z_{1}=z_{2}
.
∎

Corollary 4.56
.

Let
\pi
be a genuine supersingular
\widetilde{G}
-representation. Then each element in
\pi/X\pi
is killed by a power of
F
.

Proof.

Write
\pi=\mathcal{T}(M)
for a supersingular
\mathcal{H}
-module
M
. By Proposition
4.49
, we have
\pi=\pi_{M}[F^{-1}]
and the relations in Corollary
4.48
imply that
\pi_{M}=X\pi_{M}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

5.
Spherical Hecke algebras

In this section, we study the genuine mod-
p
representation theory of
\widetilde{G}
via spherical Hecke algebras by following the approach of Barthel-Livné as outlined in the introduction. The spherical Hecke algebra, defined to be the endomorphism ring of the compact induction of some weight, turns out to be a polynomial ring in a single operator
\widetilde{T}
(not to be confused with the metaplectic torus). Any smooth irreducible admissible genuine
\widetilde{G}
-representation is a quotient of the cokernel of a linear polynomial in
\widetilde{T}
, for a suitable weight. By following
[
6
]
, we show that these cokernels are of finite length and determine their Jordan-Hölder decomposition. As an application, we prove that a smooth
k
-representation of
\widetilde{G}
is of finite length if and only if it is finitely generated and admissible, extending the known result for
G
.

We set things up a bit more generally to also include characteristic
0
coefficients as in
[
5
]
allowing us to show in the next section that in case
k=\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
the aforementioned cokernels arise as the mod-
p
reduction of an invariant bounded lattice of a locally algebraic
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-representations of
\widetilde{G}

5.1.
Computational preparations

Let
R
be a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-algebra. We fix an integer
r\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
and define the
R
-linear representation
\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(R^{2})
of
K
just as in the case of
R=k
, Definition
4.6
, i.e. the underlying
R
-module is the space of homogeneous polynomials in
R[x,y]
of degree
r
with action defined by

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)P(x,y)=P(ax+cy,bx+dy),\text{ for all $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in K$ and $P(x,y)\in\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(R^{2})$.}

Extend the
K
-action to an action of
KZ^{\operatorname{sq}}
by letting the scalar matrix
p^{2}
act trivially, and denote the resulting representation by
\underline{\operatorname{Sym}}^{r}(R^{2})
. By Lemma
2.6
(i), we have
Z(\widetilde{G})=Z^{\operatorname{sq}}\times\mu_{2}
and we write

\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R):=\underline{\operatorname{Sym}}^{r}(R^{2})\boxtimes\iota

for the corresponding genuine
\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})
-representation. We identify the
spherical Hecke algebra

\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R)))
with the algebra

(30)

\biggl\{\varphi\colon\widetilde{G}\to\operatorname{End}_{R}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R)):\begin{array}[]{l}\diamond\hskip 2.84544pt\varphi(k_{2}gk_{2})=k_{2}\circ\varphi(g)\circ k_{1},\forall g\in\widetilde{G},k_{1},k_{2}\in\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})\\
\diamond\hskip 2.84544pt\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)\text{ is compact}\end{array}\biggr\},

whose multiplication is given by convolution.

The following is an analogue of
[
5
, Lemme 2.1.4.1]
and its proof is similar.

Definition-Lemma 5.1
.

There exists a unique function
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{R}
in the algebra (
30
) with

\bullet

\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{R})=\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})
;

\bullet

\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{R}\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\right)(x^{r-i}y^{i})=p^{2(r-i)}x^{r-i}y^{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq r
.

The corresponding endomorphism in
\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R)))
is denoted by
\widetilde{T}_{R}
. If
R=k
, we simply write
\widetilde{T}
instead of
\widetilde{T}_{k}
.

Proposition 5.2
.

If
R
is an
\mathbf{F}_{p}
-algebra and
0\leq r\leq p-1
, then the
R
-algebra
\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R)))
is a commutative polynomial ring in the operator
\widetilde{T}_{R}
.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\mathbf{F}_{p}))
is finitely generated over
\mathbf{F}_{p}[\widetilde{G}]
, one may reduce to the case
R=\mathbf{F}_{p}
, see also
[
23
, Lemma 5.1]
.
Using the description of the Hecke algebra given by (
30
) and the Cartan decomposition, the assertion can be proved as in the case of
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
, see
[
15
, Theorem 21]
. We only explain why a function supported on
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&p^{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)
, for
n\geq m\geq 0
, can only exist if
n
and
m
are even. Let
F
be such a function and put
f=F\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&p^{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\right)\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\mathbf{F}_{p}))
. First assume that
n>m
. For
x\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, the equation

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&p^{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&p^{n-m}x\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&p^{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)

implies that
f
factors through the
1
-dimensional
U\cap K
-coinvariants
\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(k)_{U\cap K}
, while the equation

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
x&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&p^{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&p^{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p^{n-m}x&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)

implies that
f
takes values in the
1
-dimensional
sUs\cap K
-invariants
\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(k)^{sUs\cap K}
. In conclusion,
f
must be a scalar multiple of the canonical map

\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\mathbf{F}_{p})\twoheadrightarrow\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\mathbf{F}_{p})_{U\cap K}\xleftarrow{\cong}\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\mathbf{F}_{p})^{sUs\cap K}\subset\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\mathbf{F}_{p}),

where the isomorphism is the projection. In particular,
f
must be
T\cap K
-equivariant. Now, by Example
2.3
, for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}
,

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&p^{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}[\lambda]&0\\
0&[\mu]\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}[\lambda]&0\\
0&[\mu]\end{smallmatrix}\right),\lambda^{m\frac{p-1}{2}}\mu^{n\frac{p-1}{2}}\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&p^{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right),

and so the
T\cap K
-equivariance of
f
can only hold true if
f=0
or
m
and
n
are even.

If
n=m
, then Lemma
4.4
implies that
f
defines a
K
-equivariant map between
\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(k)
and its twist by
\omega^{n\frac{p-1}{2}}\circ{\det}
. Such a map can only be non-zero if the twist is trivial, i.e. if
n
is even.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

If
R
is a
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
-algebra and
r=0
, then the proposition is still valid as follows for example from the Satake isomorphism
[
18
, Theorem 10.1]
.

Definition 5.4
.

Given a vector
v\in\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R)
and an element
g\in\widetilde{G}
, denote by
[g,v]\in\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R))
the unique function with support
\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})g^{-1}
and value
v
at
g^{-1}
.

Next, we define an explicit set of representatives of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{m}\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, for all
m\geq 0
, by letting
J_{m}=\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}p^{j}[\lambda_{j}]:\lambda_{j}\in\mathbf{F}_{p}\right\}
if
m\geq 1
and setting
J_{0}=\{0\}
. Moreover, for
m\geq 0
and
\lambda\in J_{m}
, we put

g^{0}_{m,\lambda}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{m}&\lambda\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
g^{1}_{m,\lambda}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p\lambda&p^{m+1}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

It follows from
[
5
, Equation (1)]
that

(31)

\widetilde{G}=\bigsqcup_{m\geq 0,\lambda\in J_{m}}\bigsqcup_{q,j=0,1}\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})(\tilde{g}^{q}_{m,\lambda})^{-1}\tilde{\Pi}^{-2j}.

Definition 5.5
.

For
m\geq 0
and
q,j=0,1
, define the subspaces
S_{m}(R)\subset B_{m}(R)
of
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R))
by

S_{m}(R)=\left\{f:\operatorname{supp}(f)\subset\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in J_{m}}\bigsqcup_{q,j=0,1}\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})(\tilde{g}^{q}_{m,\lambda})^{-1}\tilde{\Pi}^{-2j}\right\},

and
B_{m}(R)=\sum_{n\leq m}S_{n}(R)
, respectively.

Note that
B_{m}(R)\subset B_{m+1}(R)
for all
m\geq 0
. The decomposition (
31
) implies that
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R))=\sum_{m\geq 0}B_{m}(R)
. Given a function
f
in this space, one may think of the minimal
m\geq 0
satisfying
f\in B_{m}(R)
as the radius of the support of
f
.

The decomposition

\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})=\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in J_{2}}\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})(\tilde{g}^{0}_{2,\lambda})^{-1}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in J_{1}}\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})(\tilde{g}^{1}_{1,\lambda})^{-1},

which is quickly verified using
K=I\sqcup IsI
, implies that the endomorphism
\widetilde{T}_{R}\in\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}\left(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R))\right)
is explicitly given by

(32)

\widetilde{T}_{R}([1,v])=\sum_{\lambda\in J_{2}}[\tilde{g}^{0}_{2,\lambda},\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{R}((\tilde{g}^{0}_{2,\lambda})^{-1})(v)]+\sum_{\mu\in J_{1}}[\tilde{g}^{1}_{1,\mu},\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{R}((\tilde{g}^{1}_{1,\mu})^{-1})(v)],

for all
v\in\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R)
. Motivated by this description, we make the following definition in analogy to
[
5
, Lemme 2.2.1]
.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
m\geq 0
,
\mu\in J_{m}
and
v\in\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R)
.

(i)

Define
\widetilde{T}_{R}^{+}([\tilde{g}^{0}_{m,\mu},v])=\sum_{\lambda\in J_{2}}[\tilde{g}^{0}_{m+2,\mu+p^{m}\lambda},\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{R}((\tilde{g}^{0}_{2,\lambda})^{-1})(v)]
.

(ii)

Noting that
\tilde{g}^{1}_{m,\mu}=\tilde{\Pi}\tilde{g}^{0}_{m,\mu}\tilde{s}
, set
\widetilde{T}_{R}^{+}([\tilde{g}^{1}_{m,\mu},v])=\tilde{\Pi}\widetilde{T}_{R}^{+}([\tilde{g}^{0}_{m,\mu},\tilde{s}v])
.

(iii)

For
q\in\{0,1\}
, put
\widetilde{T}_{R}^{+}([\tilde{\Pi}^{2}\tilde{g}^{q}_{m,\mu},v])=\tilde{\Pi}^{2}\widetilde{T}_{R}^{+}([\tilde{g}^{q}_{m,\mu},v]).

Extending
R
-linearly, cf. (
31
), yields the endomorphism
\widetilde{T}^{+}_{R}
on
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R))
.

For
m\geq 0
, we thus have
\widetilde{T}^{+}_{R}(S_{m}(R))\subset S_{m+2}(R)
and one checks that the difference
\widetilde{T}_{R}-\widetilde{T}^{+}_{R}
preserves
B_{m}(R)
. We obtain:

Observation 5.7
.

Let
f\in\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(R))
. If
\widetilde{T}_{R}(f)\in B_{m}(R)
for some
m\geq 0
, then
f\in B_{m-2}(R)
. In particular, the image of
\widetilde{T}_{R}
intersects
B_{1}(R)
trivially, and for any
\lambda\in R
, the endomorphism
\widetilde{T}_{R}-\lambda
is not surjective.

5.2.
Jordan-Hölder sequence

We now take
R=k
and restrict
0\leq r\leq p-1
so that the
K
-representation
\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(k)
is irreducible. Following the exposition in
[
6
, §6]
, we show that
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(k))/(\widetilde{T}-\lambda)
, for
\lambda\in k
, has finite length as a
\widetilde{G}
-representation and compute its Jordan-Hölder decomposition.

To ease notation let us put
\tilde{\sigma}_{r}:=\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(k)
and let
\chi=\omega^{r}\otimes\mathbf{1}\in\widehat{H}
so that
\chi\boxtimes\iota=\tilde{\sigma}_{r}^{I_{1}}
. We extend
\chi
to a character of
HZ^{\operatorname{sq}}
by letting
p^{2}
act trivially.

Proposition 5.8
.

(i)

If
\chi\neq\chi^{s}
, then

\displaystyle\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})

\displaystyle=\ker\left(T_{s}\colon\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\chi^{s}\boxtimes\iota)\to\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)\right)

\displaystyle=T_{s}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\chi\boxtimes\iota).

(ii)

If
r=p-1
, then

\displaystyle\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{p-1})

\displaystyle=\ker\left(T_{s}+1\colon\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\to\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\right)

\displaystyle=T_{s}\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota).

(iii)

If
r=0
, then

\displaystyle\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0})

\displaystyle=\ker\left(T_{s}\colon\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\to\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\right)

\displaystyle=(T_{s}+1)\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota).

Proof.

Since the functor
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})})(-)
is exact, it suffices to prove the equalities for
\widetilde{G}
replaced by
\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})\cong KZ^{\operatorname{sq}}\times\mu_{2}
. In this case, the assertions follow from Proposition
4.8
and Lemma
3.7
(v).
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

We have equalities of right
\mathcal{H}
-modules:

(i)

If
\chi\neq\chi^{s}
or
r=p-1
,

\mathcal{I}\left(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})\right)=T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}.

(ii)

If
r=0
,

\mathcal{I}\left(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0})\right)=(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}.

Proof.

By Frobenius reciprocity,
e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\cong\mathcal{I}\left(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)\right)
. Taking
I_{1}
-invariants in Proposition
5.8
reduces us to proving the equalities

(i)

\ker(T_{s}\colon e_{\chi^{s}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\to e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1})=T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
for
\chi\neq\chi^{s}
;

(ii)

\ker(T_{s}+1\colon e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\to e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1})=T_{s}e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
;

(iii)

\ker(T_{s}\colon e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\to e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1})=(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
.

This is the content of Lemma
3.39
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

We will fix the identifications of the corollary so that the function
[1,x^{r}]
corresponds to
T_{s}e_{\chi}
(resp.
(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}
).

Let now
O=O_{\chi}
be the orbit of
\chi
. As in Section
3.5
, we put

\mathscr{Z}_{O}=\begin{cases}(T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{2}+(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{2}\text{ if $O$ is square-regular}\\
\sum_{i=0}^{3}T_{\Pi}^{-i}\left((T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{2}+(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{2}+T_{\Pi}T_{s}T_{\Pi}\right)e_{\mathbf{1}}T_{\Pi}^{i}\text{ if $O$ is non-square-regular},\end{cases}

which defines a central element in
\mathcal{H}(O)
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Under the identifications in Corollary
5.9
, the action of the (restriction to
I_{1}
-invariants of the) Hecke operator
\widetilde{T}
is given by the action of
\mathscr{Z}_{O}
.

Proof.

Since both
\widetilde{T}
and
\mathscr{Z}_{O}
are equivariant for the right
\mathcal{H}
-action, it suffices to prove that
\widetilde{T}([1,x^{r}])=\mathscr{Z}_{O}([1,x^{r}])
. The identifications of Corollary
5.9
are induced by the isomorphism
e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}\cong\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)
obtained by Frobenius reciprocity, i.e. if
\varphi\in\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}_{1}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)
denotes the unique function with support
\widetilde{I}_{1}Z(\widetilde{G})
and value
1
at the identity, then the image of an element
f\in e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
is given by
f(\varphi)
.

Together with the relations
(\tilde{g}^{0}_{2,\lambda})^{-1}=\tilde{s}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&-\lambda\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)
and
(\tilde{g}^{1}_{1,\mu})^{-1}=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-p\mu&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)
, equation (
32
) implies that in characteristic
p
,

\widetilde{T}([1,x^{r}])=\sum_{\lambda\in J_{2}}[\tilde{g}^{0}_{2,\lambda},x^{r}]+\sum_{\mu\in J_{1}}[\tilde{g}^{1}_{1,\mu},p^{2r}x^{r}].

Assume that
\chi\neq\chi^{s}
. The element
[1,x^{r}]
is identified with the element
T_{s}e_{\chi}\in T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
. By Lemma
3.7
(v),
T_{s}^{2}e_{\chi}=0
and so the operator
\mathscr{Z}_{O}
acts on
T_{s}e_{\chi}
via
(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{2}
. By Lemma
3.9
(vii),
(T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{2}=T_{(\Pi s)^{2}}
. The decomposition
I_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)I_{1}=\sqcup_{\lambda\in J_{2}}I_{1}(\Pi s)^{2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
implies that

(33)

(T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{2}e_{\chi}(\varphi)=\sum_{\lambda\in J_{2}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)e_{\chi}(\varphi).

Since
T_{\Pi^{4}}
acts trivially, we may replace
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)
by
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)
. Applying the
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant homomorphism
T_{s}
to the above equation and using that
T_{s}(T_{\Pi}T_{s})^{2}=(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{2}T_{s}
, see Lemma
3.9
(viii), gives the assertion in case
\chi\neq\chi^{s}
.

Assume now that
r=p-1
or
r=0
. By Lemma
3.9
(vi) and (ix), we have
T_{\Pi}T_{s}T_{\Pi}=T_{\Pi s\Pi}
. The decomposition
I_{1}(\Pi s\Pi)I_{1}=\sqcup_{\lambda\in J_{1}}I_{1}(\Pi s\Pi)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p\lambda&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
yields

(34)

T_{\Pi s\Pi}e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi)=\sum_{\lambda\in J_{1}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p\lambda&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{-2}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi).

Applying the
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant map
T_{s}
to it gives the formula

(35)

(T_{s}T_{\Pi})^{2}e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi)=\sum_{\lambda\in J_{1}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p\lambda&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{-2}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)T_{s}e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi).

The equalities
T_{s}(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}=0
of Lemma
3.7
(v) allows one to deduce from (
33
), (
34
) and (
35
) that

\mathscr{Z}_{O}T_{s}e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi)=\sum_{\lambda\in J_{2}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)T_{s}e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi),

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{Z}_{O}(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in J_{2}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\lambda\in J_{1}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
p\lambda&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{-2}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi)

Applying
T_{\Pi^{4}}=1
, the first equality settles the case
r=p-1
, while the second equality covers the case
r=0
by noting that
\tilde{s}
fixes
(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}(\varphi)
.
∎

Recall from Definition
3.41
that for each
\psi\in O
and the element
\lambda\in k
, we have attached a right
\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
-module
M(\psi,\lambda)
as well as the module
M(\lambda)
in the non-regular case. The previous corollary immediately gives:

Corollary 5.12
.

As right
\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
-modules,

\mathcal{I}\left(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})\right)/(\widetilde{T}-\lambda)=\begin{cases}M(\chi,\lambda)\text{ if $r\neq 0$}\\
M(\lambda)\text{ if $r=0$.}\end{cases}

We remind the reader that we parametrized the supersingular Hecke modules in definitions
3.29
and
3.38
, respectively.

Theorem 5.13
.

(i)

If
\lambda\neq 0
, then

\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})/(\widetilde{T}-\lambda)\cong\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\psi\boxtimes\iota),

where
\psi\colon T_{2}\to k^{\times}
is the smooth character defined by the requirements
\psi|_{H}=\chi^{s}
,
\psi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\lambda
and
\psi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-2}&0\\
0&p^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=1
.

(ii)

Assume now that
\lambda=0
.

(a)

If
O
is square-regular, then there is a non-split short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{T}(\operatorname{SS}(\chi[1,0],1))\to\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})/(\widetilde{T})\to\mathcal{T}(\operatorname{SS}(\chi,1))\to 0.

(b)

For
r=p-1
and
r=0
, there are non-split short exact sequences

0\to\mathcal{T}(\operatorname{SS}(0,1))\to\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{p-1})/(\widetilde{T})\to\mathcal{T}(\operatorname{SS}(-1,1))\to 0

and

0\to\mathcal{T}(\operatorname{SS}(-1,1))\to\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0})/(\widetilde{T})\to\mathcal{T}(\operatorname{SS}(0,1))\to 0,

respectively, while for
r=\frac{p-1}{2}
there is a direct sum decomposition

\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{\frac{p-1}{2}})/(\widetilde{T})\cong\mathcal{T}(\operatorname{SS}(0,1))\oplus\mathcal{T}(\operatorname{SS}(-1,1)).

Proof.

(i) Assume that
\bar{a}_{p}\neq 0
. By equation (
11
) (resp. (
12
)), Proposition
3.43
(resp.
3.44
) and Corollary
5.12
, we have

\mathcal{I}(\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\psi\boxtimes\iota))\cong\mathcal{I}\left(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})\right)/(\widetilde{T}-\lambda).

(In the square-regular case, use
\operatorname{PS}(\chi^{s},x,0,z)=\operatorname{PS}(\chi,0,x,z)
, see Remark
3.30
). The evident map from this object to the
I_{1}
-invariants of the cokernel of
\widetilde{T}-\lambda
is non-trivial by Observation
5.7
(as the element
[1,x^{r}]
is
I_{1}
-invariant and generates the compact induction as a
\widetilde{G}
-representation). Hence, by adjunction, we obtain a non-trivial
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant map

\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\psi\boxtimes\iota)=\mathcal{T}\mathcal{I}(\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\psi\boxtimes\iota))\to\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})/(\widetilde{T}-\lambda),

where the first equality is due to Theorem
4.31
. The source being irreducible, this map is necessarily injective, while the right-hand side being generated by the image of the
I_{1}
-invariant function
[1,x^{r}]
implies that it is surjective.

(ii) Assume now that
\lambda=0
. By Propositions
3.43
(resp.
3.44
) and Corollary
5.12
, there are short exact sequences of the desired form if the middle term is replaced by
\mathcal{T}\left(\mathcal{I}\left(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})\right)/(\widetilde{T})\right)
. For this we use the right exactness of the functor
\mathcal{T}
and the fact that
\mathcal{T}
applied to a supersingular modules yields an irreducible representation by Theorem
4.54
. It remains to show that the canonical map

\mathcal{T}\left(\mathcal{I}\left(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})\right)/(\widetilde{T})\right)\to\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})/(\widetilde{T})

is an isomorphism. The consideration of the element
[1,x^{r}]
again shows the surjectivity. By what we have just explained, it suffices to prove that each supersingular subrepresentation (or equivalently its
I_{1}
-invariants) of the source survives in the target.

In the square-regular case, the proof of Proposition
3.43
shows that the element
T_{s}e_{\chi}T_{\Pi}T_{s}
is a basis of
\operatorname{SS}(\chi[1,0],1)(e_{\chi}+e_{\chi[1,0]})
. In the non-square-regular case, the proof of Proposition
3.44
shows that the element
T_{s}e_{\mathbf{1}}T_{\Pi}T_{s}T_{\Pi}
is a basis element of
\operatorname{SS}(0,1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\subset M(\mathbf{1},0)e_{\mathbf{1}}
, while
(T_{s}+1)e_{\mathbf{1}}T_{\Pi}T_{s}T_{\Pi}
is a basis of
\operatorname{SS}(-1,1)e_{\mathbf{1}}\subset M(0)
; it also shows that in case
\chi=\omega^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\otimes\mathbf{1}
(i.e.
r=\frac{p-1}{2}
), the element
T_{s}e_{\chi}T_{\Pi}T_{s}
is a basis for the subspace
\operatorname{SS}(-1,1)e_{\mathbf{1}}
, while
T_{s}e_{\chi}T_{\Pi}(T_{s}+1)
is a basis element of the subspace
\operatorname{SS}(0,1)e_{\mathbf{1}}
. By the Morita equivalences
3.19
and
3.31
, respectively, it is enough to prove that these basis elements survive in the cokernel of
\widetilde{T}
. We thus need to show that

(36)

\begin{cases}[1,x^{r}]T_{\Pi}T_{s}\notin\operatorname{Im}(\widetilde{T})\text{ if $0<r<p-1$}\\
[1,x^{r}]T_{\Pi}T_{s}T_{\Pi}\notin\operatorname{Im}(\widetilde{T})\text{ if $r=0,p-1$}\\
[1,x^{r}]T_{\Pi}(T_{s}+1)\notin\operatorname{Im}(\widetilde{T})\text{ if $r=\frac{p-1}{2}$.}\end{cases}

Since these elements lie in
B_{1}(k)
, an application of Observation
5.7
finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

The last part of the proof in particular shows that a supersingular
\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}
-module
M
admits a non-trivial
\mathcal{H}
-equivariant morphism to the non-zero (Observation
5.7
) space
\mathcal{I}\left(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma})/(\widetilde{T})\right)
for some weight
\tilde{\sigma}
. In particular, by adjunction,
\mathcal{T}(M)
is non-zero.

Definition 5.15
.

For each
0\leq r\leq p-1
with
r\neq\frac{p-1}{2}
, let
\tilde{\pi}(r)
be the unique irreducible (supersingular) quotient of
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})/(\widetilde{T})
. If
\eta\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to k^{\times}
is a smooth character, we put
\tilde{\pi}(r,\eta)=\tilde{\pi}(r)\otimes\eta\circ{\det}
.

Corollary 5.16
.

Each genuine supersingular
\widetilde{G}
-representation is of the form
\tilde{\pi}(r,\eta)
for some
0\leq r\leq p-1
,
r\neq\frac{p-1}{2}
, and a smooth character
\eta\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to k^{\times}
.

Moreover,
\tilde{\pi}(r)\cong\tilde{\pi}(r^{\prime},\eta)
if and only if one of the following holds

\displaystyle r^{\prime}=r,\text{ $\eta_{\operatorname{nr}}^{4}=1$ and $(\eta\eta_{\operatorname{nr}}^{-1})^{2}=1$;}

\displaystyle r^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\frac{p-1}{2}-r\text{ if $0<r<\frac{p-1}{2}$}\\
\frac{3}{2}(p-1)-r\text{ if $\frac{p-1}{2}<r<p-1$}\end{cases}\text{ and \phantom{m} $\eta_{\operatorname{nr}}^{4}=1$, $(\eta\eta_{\operatorname{nr}}^{-1}\omega^{-r})^{2}=1$,}

where
\eta_{\operatorname{nr}}
denotes the unramified part of
\eta
.

Proof.

Corollary
4.53
gives an explicit description of the socle of a genuine supersingular representation. In particular, after twisting by a character
\eta\circ\det
it contains a weight
\tilde{\sigma}_{r}
for some
0\leq r\leq p-1
with
r\neq\frac{p-1}{2}
. We may also assume that the central element
p^{2}
acts trivially. Since the weight appears with multiplicity one, the operator
\widetilde{T}
must act on the corresponding weight space via a scalar. By Theorem
5.13
(i), this scalar must be
0
, i.e. the supersingular representation is isomorphic to
\tilde{\pi}(r)
. This proves the first part.

Any unramified character
\eta
of order at most
4
satisfies
(\eta\circ\det)|_{Z^{\operatorname{sq}}}=1
and so

\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})/(\widetilde{T})\otimes(\eta\circ{\det})\cong\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})/(\widetilde{T}).

By Lemma
4.4
, the element
\tilde{\Pi}^{2}
defines a
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant isomorphism

\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})/(\widetilde{T})\cong\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r})/(\widetilde{T})\otimes(\mu_{-1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\omega^{\frac{p-1}{2}})\circ{\det}.

Putting these two observations together, we obtain that
\tilde{\pi}(r)\cong\tilde{\pi}(r,\eta)
for
\eta
satisfying
\eta_{\operatorname{nr}}^{4}=1
and
(\eta\eta_{\operatorname{nr}}^{-1})^{2}=1
(in fact this holds for the full cokernel of
\widetilde{T}
). By Corollary
4.53
and Remark
4.47
, we know that
\tilde{\sigma}_{r^{\prime}}\otimes\omega^{r}\circ{\det}
lies in the
K
-socle of
\tilde{\pi}(r)
and thus
\tilde{\pi}(r)\cong\tilde{\pi}(r^{\prime})\otimes\omega^{r}\circ{\det}
. This shows that the described intertwinings hold true.

That these exhaust all intertwinings can be deduced from looking at the
K
-socle and the central character.
∎

5.3.
Howe’s theorem

The goal of this section is to explain a proof of the following theorem.

Theorem 5.17
.

A smooth
k
-representation of
\widetilde{G}
is of finite length if and only if it is finitely generated and admissible.

For the full subcategory
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{G}(k)
of
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G}}(k)
this is a combination of
[
9
, Theorem 2.3.8]
and
[
2
]
, so we will only focus on the subcategory of genuine objects. The strategy is the same as that for the group
G
, but we still need to justify certain auxiliary results.

Theorem
5.13
implies that the cokernels of linear polynomials in the Hecke operator
\widetilde{T}
are of finite length and the proof of
[
9
, Theorem 2.3.8]
then goes through to show that any finitely generated admissible object of
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{G},\iota}(k)
is indeed of finite length.

For the converse, we need to show that a smooth irreducible genuine representation of
\widetilde{G}
is admissible. As a first step, we need to check that the analogue of (the proof of)
[
1
, Proposition 32]
goes through to prove the following result.

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
\ell
be a field of characteristic
p
. Let
\pi
be a smooth irreducible genuine
\ell
-representation of
\widetilde{G}
admitting a central character. Then
\pi
is a quotient of
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota)/P(\widetilde{T})
for some weight
\sigma
and some irreducible polynomial
P(\widetilde{T})\in\ell[\widetilde{T}]
.

The proof of this proposition requires two lemmas, the second of which is a metaplectic version of
[
1
, Proposition 18]
.

Lemma 5.19
.

Assume that
\chi:=\sigma^{I_{1}}
is square-regular. The Hecke operator
T_{\Pi s}
acts injectively on
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota)^{I_{1}}e_{\chi[1,0]}
.

Proof.

After twisting by a character, we may assume that
\sigma=\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\ell^{2})
, for some
0\leq r\leq p-1
, and the central element
p^{2}
acts trivially. By Corollary
5.9
, we have
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota)^{I_{1}}e_{\chi[1,0]}=T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}e_{\chi[1,0]}
. For proving the desired injectivity, we may act by
T_{\Pi}
from the left and it suffices to prove injectivity of the map
T_{\Pi s}^{2}=T_{(\Pi s)^{2}}
. In view of the isomorphisms in propositions
3.21
and
3.22
, we need to show that the element
Y\in\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0])_{p^{2}=1}\cong\ell[X,Y]/(XY)
acts injectively on
T_{\Pi s}\mathcal{H}(\chi[1,0],\chi)_{p^{2}=1}\cong Y\ell[X,Y]/(XY)
, which is clear. We note that the results we used hold true over an arbitrary field of characteristic
p
; alternatively, the lemma can be checked after passing to an algebraic closure.
∎

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
\psi\colon HZ^{\operatorname{sq}}\to k^{\times}
be a smooth character. Any non-zero subspace
W\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\psi\boxtimes\iota),\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota))
stable under the action of
\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\psi\boxtimes\iota))
has finite codimension.

Proof.

After twisting we may assume that
\sigma=\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\ell^{2})
, for some
0\leq r\leq p-1
, and the central element
p^{2}
acts trivially. Write
\chi=\sigma^{I_{1}}
, viewed as a character of
HZ^{\operatorname{sq}}
. The Hom-space in the statement is equal to the
\mathcal{H}(\psi)_{p^{2}=1}
-module
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota)^{I_{1}}e_{\psi}
.

Assume that
\chi
is square-regular. By Corollary
5.9
we then have
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota)^{I_{1}}e_{\psi}=T_{s}e_{\chi}\mathcal{H}_{p^{2}=1}e_{\psi}
. Since the statement is empty if
\psi
does not lie in the orbit of
\chi
, we do from now on assume that
\psi\in O_{\chi}
. Acting by
T_{\Pi}
from the right, we may assume that
\psi=\chi[t,0]
for
t=0,1
. If
t=0
, then acting from the left by
T_{\Pi}
, we need to check that a non-zero
\mathcal{H}(\chi)_{p^{2}=1}
-submodule of
T_{\Pi s}\mathcal{H}(\chi)_{p^{2}=1}
has finite codimension. But by propositions
3.21
and
3.22
, this just means that a
\ell[X,Y]/(XY)
-submodule of
\ell[Y]
(with
X=0
on it) has finite codimension, i.e. that every non-zero ideal of the polynomial ring
\ell[Y]
has finite codimension. This is clear. The case
t=1
is treated similarly.

Assume now that
\chi
is not square-regular, i.e.
r
is a multiple of
\frac{p-1}{2}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\psi=\mathbf{1}
.

If
r=p-1
, then by Corollary
5.9
, we are considering a non-zero
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
-submodule
W
of
T_{s}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
. Using the description of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
as in Proposition
3.33
, we decompose
W
as

WS_{0,0}\oplus W(S_{0,0}+1)\subset S_{0,0}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}S_{0,0}\oplus S_{0,0}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}(S_{0,0}+1)=S_{0,0}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}.

We note that each summand on the left-hand side is non-zero (act by
S_{0,2}
). Now, as
\ell
-algebras,
\ell[Y]\cong S_{0,0}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}S_{0,0}
via
1\mapsto-S_{0,0}
and
Y\mapsto S_{0,0}S_{0,2}S_{0,0}
, so we are done with the inclusion of the first direct summand just like in the regular case. For the second one, note that we again have
\ell[Y]\cong(S_{0,0}+1)\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}(S_{0,0}+1)
, where now
1\mapsto S_{0,0}+1
and
Y\mapsto(S_{0,0}+1)S_{0,2}(S_{0,0}+1)
. As a module over this ring, the summand
S_{0,0}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}(S_{0,0}+1)
is generated by the element
S_{0,0}S_{0,2}(S_{0,0}+1)
and in particular any non-zero submodule does indeed have finite codimension.

If
r=0
, then by Corollary
5.9
, we are considering a non-zero
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
-submodule
W
of
(S_{0,0}+1)\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
. Interchanging
S_{0,0}+1
and
-S_{0,0}
defines an automorphism of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
, which reduces to the previously treated case
r=p-1
.

In case
r=\frac{p-1}{2}
, we make use of Corollary
5.9
once again and act by
T_{\Pi}
from the left to reduce to showing that a non-zero
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
-submodule
W
of
T_{\Pi}T_{s}T_{\Pi}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}=S_{0,2}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1})_{p^{2}=1}
has finite codimension (here we used that
T_{\Pi}e_{\mathbf{1}}=e_{\chi}T_{\Pi}
, Lemma
3.9
(v)), which can now be proved as for
r=p-1
by decomposing the problem according to the idempotent
-S_{0,0}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.18
.

Let
\pi
be as in the statement. Picking a weight
\sigma\subset\pi|_{K}
and letting the central element
p^{2}
act on it according to the central character of
\pi
, we obtain a surjection
\varphi\colon\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
. Since the compact induction is not irreducible (e.g. Theorem
5.13
), the kernel is non-zero and thus
\ker(\varphi)^{I_{1}}e_{\psi}\neq 0
for some
\psi\in O_{\chi}
. Either acting by
T_{\Pi}
and using Lemma
3.9
(v) or acting by
T_{\Pi s}
and using Lemma
3.9
(v), (vii) and Lemma
3.7
(iv), we may assume that
\psi=\chi
; here note that
T_{\Pi}
(Lemma
3.9
(i)) and
T_{\Pi s}
(Lemma
5.19
) act injectively.

The inclusion
\chi=\sigma^{I_{1}}\subset\sigma
gives rise to a surjective map
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\chi\boxtimes\iota)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota)
, which in turn induces a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\chi\boxtimes\iota),\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\chi\boxtimes\iota),\pi)}
\textstyle{\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\sigma\boxtimes\iota),\pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

with injective vertical maps, where the horizontal maps are induced by composing with
\varphi
. Lemma
5.20
implies that the top arrow has finite image and thus the lower arrow does as well. But the image of the morphism at the bottom is non-zero (it contains
\varphi
) and it is stable under the action of the spherical Hecke algebra
\ell[\widetilde{T}]
appearing as the source of the arrow. Picking a non-zero element in this finite dimensional
\ell[\widetilde{T}]
-submodule killed by some irreducible polynomial finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.21
.

A smooth irreducible genuine
k
-representation of
\widetilde{G}
is admissible.

Proof.

As in
[
2
]
, this can now be deduced from Proposition
5.18
and Theorem
5.13
.
∎

6.
Relation to locally algebraic representations

Fix an algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
with ring of integers
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
. Keeping the notation of the previous section and putting
k=\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
, we would like to relate the smooth
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
-representations
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}))/(\widetilde{T}-\bar{a}_{p})
, for
\bar{a}_{p}\in\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
and
0\leq r\leq p-1
, to the reduction modulo the maximal ideal in
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
of a
\widetilde{G}
-stable bounded lattice of a locally algebraic
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-representation (or equivalently its unitary completion) of
\widetilde{G}
. Choosing a lift
a_{p}\in\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
of
\bar{a}_{p}
, a natural candidate to consider is the representation

\widetilde{\Pi}_{r,a_{p}}:=\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}))/(\widetilde{T}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}-a_{p}).

Although it will not be necessary for us, we first describe
\widetilde{\Pi}_{r,a_{p}}
in terms of (constituents of) locally algebraic unramified principal series, which can be deduced from the corresponding statement
[
5
, Proposition 3.2.1]
for the group
G
by utilizing Savin’s local Shimura correspondence.

6.1.
Savin’s local Shimura correspondence

Let us denote by
\mathcal{H}_{\iota}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I})=\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{G}}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{I}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota))
the genuine Iwahori Hecke algebra, where
\mathbf{1}
is the trivial
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
-valuded character, and let
\mathcal{H}(G,I)=\operatorname{End}_{G}(\operatorname{c-Ind}^{G}_{I}(\mathbf{1}))
be the usual Iwahori Hecke algebra of
G
. The work
[
27
, Proposition 3.1.2]
of Savin provides us with a description of
\mathcal{H}_{\iota}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I})
in terms of generators and relations. Comparing with the description of
\mathcal{H}(G,I)
, see for example
[
26
]
, we obtain an explicit isomorphism

(37)

\mathcal{H}_{\iota}(\widetilde{G},I)\cong\mathcal{H}(G,I)

of
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-algebras, see also
[
27
, §4]
. In representation theoretic terms, this translates into an equivalence of categories

(38)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Smooth genuine}\\
\text{$\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}$-representations of $\widetilde{G}$}\\
\text{generated by their $I$-invariants}\end{array}\right\}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Smooth }\\
\text{$\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}$-representations of $G$}\\
\text{generated by their $I$-invariants}\end{array}\right\}.

Lemma 6.1
.

The equivalence (
38
) maps
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)
to
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{G}_{KZ}(\mathbf{1})
and the endomorphism
\widetilde{T}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}
of the former to the endomorphism
p^{1/2}T_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}
of the latter, where
T_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}
corresponds to the double coset
KZ\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&p^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)KZ
.

Proof.

It is enough to check the assertions on
I
-invariants. Let
\widetilde{T}_{s}\in\mathcal{H}_{\iota}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I})
be the function with support
\widetilde{I}\tilde{s}\widetilde{I}
sending
\tilde{s}
to
1
and let
T_{s}\in\mathcal{H}(G,I)
be the indicator function of
IsI
. For
n\geq m
, let
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}_{2n,2m}\in\mathcal{H}_{\iota}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I})
be the function with support
\widetilde{I}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{2n}&0\\
0&p^{2m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)\widetilde{I}
and value
1
at
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{2n}&0\\
0&p^{2m}\end{smallmatrix}\right),1\right)
, and let
\mathbf{1}_{n,m}\in\mathcal{H}(G,I)
be the indicator function of
I\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&p^{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right)I
. The isomorphism (
37
) then maps
\widetilde{T}_{s}
to
T_{s}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}_{2n,2m}
to
p^{\frac{n-m}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{n,m}
.

Considered as a subspace
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{G}_{K}(\mathbf{1})^{I}\subset\operatorname{c-Ind}^{G}_{I}(\mathbf{1})^{I}\cong\mathcal{H}(G,I)
, we have the equality
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{G}_{K}(\mathbf{1})^{I}=(1+T_{s})\mathcal{H}(G,I)
since
1+T_{s}
is the indicator function of
K
. One similarly has
\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)^{I}=(1+\widetilde{T}_{s})\mathcal{H}_{\iota}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I})
. This proves the first part.
Concerning the Hecke operators, one may check that
T_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}=p^{-1}(1+T_{s})\mathbf{1}_{0,-1}(1+T_{s})
in
\mathcal{H}(G,I)/(\mathbf{1}_{1,1}-1)
and
\widetilde{T}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}=p^{-1}(1+\widetilde{T}_{s})\tilde{\mathbf{1}}_{0,-2}(1+\widetilde{T}_{s})
in
\mathcal{H}_{\iota}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I})/(\tilde{\mathbf{1}}_{2,2}-1)
.
∎

6.2.
Genuine principal series in characteristic
0

Fix an isomorphism
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}\cong\mathbf{C}
and let
|\cdot|\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}\to\mathbf{Q}_{\geq 0}
be the
p
-adic norm so that
|p|=p^{-1}
. Via the fixed isomorphism, we will view fractional powers of
|\cdot|
as
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
-valued characters of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
.

Definition 6.2
.

(i)

For two smooth characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon A\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
, define the normalized principal series

\iota^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})=\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi_{1}|\cdot|^{1/2}\otimes\chi_{2}|\cdot|^{-1/2}\boxtimes\iota),

which only depends on the restriction of
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
to the subgroup
S
of squares in
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}
.

(ii)

For two smooth characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
, let

\iota^{G}_{B}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})=\operatorname{Ind}^{G}_{B}(\chi_{1}|\cdot|^{1/2}\otimes\chi_{2}|\cdot|^{-1/2})

be the usual normalized principal series of
G
attached to
(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})
.

Given a character
\chi\colon A\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
, denote by
\chi^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
the character defined by

\chi^{\prime}(x)=\chi(x^{2}),\text{ for all $x\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\times}$.}

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon A\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
be smooth characters trivial on
T\cap K
. The equivalence (
38
) maps
\iota^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})
to
\iota^{G}_{B}(\chi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\chi_{2}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

First note that the genuine principal series must correspond to an (unramified) principal series, say
\iota^{G}_{B}(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})
for two smooth unramified characters
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
. A basis for the space
\iota^{G}_{B}(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})^{I}
is given by the two functions
\varphi,\varphi_{s}
defined by the conditions
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)=BI
,
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi_{s})=BsI
and
\varphi(1)=\varphi_{s}(s)=1
. A basis for the space
\iota^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})^{I}
is given by the two functions
\tilde{\varphi},\tilde{\varphi}_{s}
defined similarly but requiring that
\tilde{\varphi}(1)=\tilde{\varphi}_{s}(\tilde{s})\in\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{T}}_{\widetilde{T}_{2}}(\chi_{1}|\cdot|^{1/2}\otimes\chi_{2}|\cdot|^{-1/2}\boxtimes\iota)^{T\cap K}
is the standard basis vector
f_{0,0}
as in Definition
4.18
. Letting
\mathbf{1}_{0,-1}\in\mathcal{H}(G,I)
and
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}_{0,-2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\iota}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I})
be as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, one checks that with respect to the chosen bases,

\mathbf{1}_{0,-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{1/2}\psi_{2}(p)&p^{1/2}(p-1)\psi_{2}(p)\\
0&p^{1/2}\psi_{1}(p)\end{smallmatrix}\right)\text{ and }\tilde{\mathbf{1}}_{0,-2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p\chi_{2}(p^{2})&p(p-1)\chi_{2}(p^{2})\\
0&p\chi_{1}(p^{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Since the isomorphism (
37
) identifies
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}_{0,-2}
with
p^{1/2}\mathbf{1}_{0,-1}
, we deduce that
\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}=\{\chi_{1}^{\prime},\chi_{2}^{\prime}\}
. If
\psi_{1}\psi_{2}^{-1}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}
, then
\iota^{G}_{B}(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})\cong\iota^{G}_{B}(\psi_{2}\otimes\psi_{1})
, so we are done. Assume now by contradiction that
\psi_{1}\psi_{2}^{-1}=|\cdot|
and
\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\chi_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}=|\cdot|^{-1}
. By
[
16
, Theorem I.2.9]
,
\iota^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}}(\chi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\chi_{2}^{\prime})
contains a unique irreducible subrepresentation admitting a
K
-invariant vector (and so the quotient cannot admit one), while
\iota^{G}_{B}(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})
is a non-split extensions of an unramified character by the corresponding twist of the Steinberg representation (not admitting a
K
-invariant vector). Since the equivalence (
38
) preserves the property of having
K
-invariant vectors (the isomorphism (
37
) maps the genuine indicator function of
\widetilde{K}
to the indicator function of
K
as observered in the proof of Lemma
6.1
), we obtain the desired contradiction.
∎

Definition-Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon A\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
be smooth characters.

(i)

If
\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\chi_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}
, then
\iota^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})
is irreducible.

Moreover, if
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\colon A\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
is another pair of smooth characters, then
\iota^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})\cong\iota^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})
if and only if
(\psi_{1}^{\prime},\psi_{2}^{\prime})=(\chi_{1}^{\prime},\chi_{2}^{\prime})
or
(\psi_{1}^{\prime},\psi_{2}^{\prime})=(\chi_{2}^{\prime},\chi_{1}^{\prime})
.

(ii)

If
\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\chi_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}=|\cdot|^{-1}
, then there are non-split short exact sequence

0\to\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})\to\iota^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})\to\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{2},\chi_{1})\to 0

and

0\to\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{2},\chi_{1})\to\iota^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\chi_{2}\otimes\chi_{1})\to\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})\to 0,

with
\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})
,
\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{2},\chi_{1})
irreducible. Moreover,
\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})^{K}\neq 0
if and only if
\chi_{2}^{\prime}
is unramified, in which case
\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})
and
\tilde{\pi}(\chi_{2},\chi_{1})
correspond to
\chi_{2}^{\prime}|\cdot|^{-1/2}\circ\det
and
\operatorname{St}\otimes\chi_{2}^{\prime}|\cdot|^{-1/2}\circ\det
, respectively, under the equivalence (
38
).

Proof.

(i) The irreducibility is
[
16
, Corollary I.2.8]
, while the intertwinings follow from the computation of the Jacquet module of a genuine principal series, see Proposition I.2.1 of
loc. cit.

(ii) After twisting, we may assume that
\chi_{2}^{\prime}
is unramified, in which case the assertion follows from the equivalence (
38
) and Lemma
6.3
.
∎

For
y\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
, let
\tilde{\mu}_{y}\colon A\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
denote the unique character trivial on
T\cap K
and mapping
p^{2}
to
y
.

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
r\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
a_{p}\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
. Let
\alpha,\beta\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
be the roots of the polynomial
X^{2}-a_{p}X+p^{2(r+1)}
.

(i)

If
a_{p}\notin\{\pm p^{r+1/2}(1+p)\}
, then

\widetilde{\Pi}_{r,a_{p}}\cong\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{2})\otimes\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}(\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha^{-1}}\otimes\tilde{\mu}_{p^{2}\beta^{-1}}\boxtimes\iota).

(ii)

If
a_{p}\in\{\pm p^{r+1/2}(1+p)\}
, then there is a short exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{2})\otimes\tilde{\pi}(\tilde{\mu}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\mu}_{\varepsilon}|\cdot|^{-1/2})\to\widetilde{\Pi}_{r,a_{p}}\to\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{2})\otimes\tilde{\pi}(\tilde{\mu}_{\varepsilon}|\cdot|^{-1/2},\tilde{\mu}_{\varepsilon})\to 0,

where
\varepsilon=\frac{p^{-2r}a_{p}}{p(p+1)}
.

Proof.

The case
r=0
follows from the equivalence (
38
), Lemma
6.1
, Lemma
6.3
and the corresponding statement for the group
G
, which is a special case of
[
5
, Proposition 3.2.1]
.
The general case is now a consequence of this by noting that the isomorphism

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{2})\otimes|\cdot|^{r/2}\circ\det)\otimes\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\iota)\cong\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}))

\displaystyle v\otimes f\mapsto[g\mapsto f(g)(g.v)]

identifies
\operatorname{id}\otimes\widetilde{T}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}
with
p^{-r}\widetilde{T}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}
.
∎

6.3.
Mod-
p
reduction

Let now
0\leq r\leq p-1
and
a_{p}\in\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose that for
0\leq i\leq p-1
we are given elements
a_{i}\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}a_{i}[\lambda]^{i}\in\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}
. Then
a_{i}\in\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}

Proof.

Choose
L/\mathbf{Q}_{p}
finite large enough to contain all the
a_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{L}
be the ring of integers with uniformizer
\varpi
and residue field
k_{L}
. Let
N\gg 0
such that
b_{i}:=\varpi^{N}a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{L}
for all
0\leq i\leq p-1
. By assumption,
\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}b_{i}[\lambda]^{i}\in\varpi^{N}\mathcal{O}_{L}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}
. We claim that
b_{i}\in\varpi^{N}\mathcal{O}_{L}
. By induction, we may assume that
N=1
. In this case, the mod-
\varpi
reduction
\bar{f}(X)\in k_{L}[X]
of the polynomial
\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}b_{i}X^{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{L}[X]
vanishes on the set
\mathbf{F}_{p}
of cardinality
p
, which forces
\bar{f}(X)=0
for degree reasons, i.e.
b_{i}\in\varpi\mathcal{O}_{L}
as desired.
∎

By our restriction on
r
being at most
p-1
, the following assertion can be proved in the same way as Breuil shows Corollaire 4.1.2 in
[
5
]
by utilizing the previous lemma, which allows to obtain appropriate versions of Proposition 3.3.3 and Théoréme 4.1.1 in
loc. cit.

Proposition 6.7
.

The
\widetilde{G}
-equivariant map

(39)

\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}))/(\widetilde{T}_{\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}}-a_{p})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}))/(\widetilde{T}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}-a_{p})=\widetilde{\Pi}_{r,a_{p}}

is injective and defines a
\widetilde{G}
-stable bounded lattice in the
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}[\widetilde{G}]
-module
\widetilde{\Pi}_{r,a_{p}}
, i.e. a
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}[\widetilde{G}]
-submodule generating the whole space over
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
but not containing a
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-line.

Remark 6.8
.

(i)

We have

\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}))/(\widetilde{T}_{\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}}-a_{p})\otimes_{\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}}\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}=\operatorname{c-Ind}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{K}Z(\widetilde{G})}(\tilde{\sigma}_{r}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}))/(\widetilde{T}-\bar{a}_{p}),

where
\bar{a}_{p}
is the image of
a_{p}
under the projection
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
, so from Theorem
5.13
we deduce a description of the (semi-simplified) mod-
p
reduction of (any
\widetilde{G}
-stable bounded lattice in)
\widetilde{\Pi}_{r,a_{p}}
.

(ii)

In fact, Breuil allows
0\leq r\leq 2(p-1)
. But under the more restrictive assumption
r\leq p-1
only the
\widetilde{T}^{+}
-part of the Hecke operator, see Definition
5.6
, needs to be considered. We have not attempted to investigate the more complicated cases
p\leq r\leq 2(p-1)
in our setting.
